A chromatic approach to homological stability
Oscar Randal-Williams

Abstract
. We propose a way to organise the subject of “higher-order homological stability”, in the context of a graded E 2 E_{2} -algebra 𝐑 \mathbf{R} , along the same lines that the chromatic perspective organises stable homotopy theory. From this point of view proving a (higher-order) homological stability theorem corresponds to producing Smith–Toda complexes in the category of 𝐑 \mathbf{R} -modules: using this perspective we prove that whenever 𝐑 \mathbf{R} is defined over a field of positive characteristic and satisfies some standard properties, there is a sequence of higher-order homological stability theorems whose slopes tend to 1. We propose that in a higher-order stable range the “stable homology” should be interpreted as certain Bousfield localisations in the category of 𝐑 \mathbf{R} -modules, leading to a chromatic tower and monochromatic layers. Given the existence of suitable Smith–Toda complexes we establish several properties of these localisations, in particular explaining how higher-order stabilisation maps yield periodic families in the monochromatic layers. We explain how to associate to such an 𝐑 \mathbf{R} a Hopf algebra which completely governs the kinds of higher-order stability maps that it enjoys, in the sense that the cohomology of this Hopf algebra has precisely the same stability patterns as 𝐑 \mathbf{R} . When 𝐑 \mathbf{R} comes from a sequence of groups, this Hopf algebra has a concrete description as the coinvariants of the E 1 E_{1} -Steinberg modules.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Premise

Recent thinking on the phenomenon of homological stability for a sequence

X_{0}\longrightarrow X_{1}\longrightarrow X_{2}\longrightarrow\cdots

of spaces (or groups, or ….) has been to arrange their chains into an
E_{2}
-algebra

\mathbb{R}\simeq\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}C_{*}(X_{n};\mathbbm{k})

in the category of chain complexes of
\mathbbm{k}
-modules with an additional
\mathbb{N}
-grading, and to recognise the original stabilisation maps as multiplication by an element
\sigma\in C_{0}(X_{1};\mathbbm{k})
with respect to this
E_{2}
-structure. From this point of view formulating the question of homological stability uses only a small part of the
E_{2}
-algebra structure on
\mathbb{R}
, but in answering this question one can make use of the full
E_{2}
-algebra structure. This point of view, initiated in
[
34
]
and developed in
[
14
]
, has led to improvements in homological stability ranges for many examples
[
15
,
17
,
16
,
47
,
35
,
31
,
30
,
6
]
.

More interestingly, it has suggested that homological stability is perhaps only the beginning of the story. In
[
15
]
Galatius, Kupers, and the author applied this point of view to the mapping class groups
\Gamma_{g,1}
of genus
g
surfaces with one boundary, and showed that not only does one have
H_{d}(\Gamma_{g,1},\Gamma_{g-1,1};\mathbb{Z})=0
for
d<\tfrac{2}{3}g
, corresponding to homological stability for these groups, but there are
secondary
stabilisation maps

\varphi_{*}:H_{d-2}(\Gamma_{g-3,1},\Gamma_{g-4,1};\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H_{d}(\Gamma_{g,1},\Gamma_{g-1,1};\mathbb{Z})

which are epimorphisms for
d<\tfrac{3}{4}g
and isomorphisms for
d<\tfrac{3}{4}g-1
. This kind of secondary homological stability has since been discovered in further examples and contexts
[
43
,
23
]
. As these maps change the genus
g
by 3, rather than by 1, it would perhaps be better to call the phenomenon periodicity rather than stability.

In this paper we will make the case that such higher-order homological stability phenomena should be organised along the same lines that the chromatic perspective organises stable homotopy theory. This will provide answers to questions such as:

(i)

When should “higher-order homological stability” be expected to hold for
\mathbb{R}
?

(ii)

What should be meant by e.g. “homology of
\mathbb{R}
in the secondary stable range”, and what properties does it have?

(iii)

How does one tell what kind of higher-order stabilities a given
\mathbb{R}
should have?

1.2.
Context and notation

In Section
2
we will give a detailed account of the technical framework in which we shall work, but for now we make the following brief remarks. Fix a commutative ring
\mathbbm{k}
and write
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})
for the derived
\infty
-category of
\mathbbm{k}
(i.e. the category of
H\mathbbm{k}
-module spectra, or the localisation of the category of chain complexes
\mathsf{Ch}(\mathbbm{k})
at the quasiisomorphisms). This is a stable
\infty
-category, and has a symmetric monoidality given by (derived) tensor product
-\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}-
. The
\mathbb{Z}
-graded objects in this category
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
inherits a symmetric monoidality by Day convolution, and our basic datum will be

\mathbb{R}\in\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{2}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}),

an
E_{2}
-algebra in
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
. Most of our discussion will take place in the
\infty
-category
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
of left
\mathbb{R}
-module objects in
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
, which has an
E_{1}
-monoidal structure
-\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}-
because
\mathbb{R}
is an
E_{2}
-algebra.

Objects
X\in\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})
have homology(=homotopy) groups, which we write as
\pi_{d}(X)
. They are represented by the object
S^{d}\in\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})
, the
d
-th suspension of the monoidal unit
\mathbbm{k}
. Objects
X\in\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
therefore have bigraded homotopy groups
\pi_{n,d}(X)=\pi_{d}(X(n))
. They are represented by the object
S^{n,d}\in\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
which evaluates to
S^{d}
at the integer
n
and to 0 otherwise. We will say that an object
X\in\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}

has a slope
\lambda
vanishing line
if there exists a
\kappa
such that
\pi_{n,d}(X)=0
for
d<\lambda n+\kappa
.

1.3.
Kinds of stability

We wish to contemplate the phenomenon of homological stability in sufficient breadth that it includes all the following kinds of examples.

1.3.1.
Ordinary homological stability

The most common situation in the study of homological stability is to have an element
\sigma\in\pi_{1,0}(\mathbb{R})
, form the mapping cone
\mathbb{R}/\sigma
of the left
\mathbb{R}
-module endomorphism

S^{1,0}\otimes\mathbb{R}\cong\mathbb{R}\otimes S^{1,0}\overset{\mathbb{R}\otimes\sigma}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{R}\otimes\mathbb{R}\overset{-\cdot-}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{R},

and be able to show that there is a vanishing line of the form
\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{R}/\sigma)=0
for
d<\lambda n+\kappa
. When
\mathbb{R}
arises from taking the
\mathbbm{k}
-chains of an
E_{2}
-algebra
\coprod_{n\geq 0}X_{n}
in
\mathbb{N}
-graded spaces, then

\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{R}/\sigma)=H_{d}(X_{n},X_{n-1};\mathbbm{k})

so a vanishing range for these groups is equivalent to a range in which the maps
H_{d}(X_{n-1};\mathbbm{k})\to H_{d}(X_{n};\mathbbm{k})
are isomorphisms or epimorphisms.

This kind of homological stability theorem, in most cases with
\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{R}/\sigma)=0
for
d<\tfrac{1}{2}n
, is known for an enormous number of examples, far too many to do justice to here. In the special case where
\mathbb{R}=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}C_{*}(G_{n};\mathbbm{k})
comes from a family of discrete groups
G_{n}
, examples include: symmetric groups; braid groups; Coxeter groups; general linear groups or unitary groups over quite general rings; mapping class groups of orientable surfaces, or of non-orientable surfaces, or of certain 3-manifolds; automorphism groups of free groups, or of free nilpotent groups, or of RAAGs. There are also many examples not associated to discrete groups, including: moduli spaces of high-dimensional manifolds; Artin monoids; diagram algebras such as the Iwahori–Hecke or Temperley–Lieb algebra; reductive Borel–Serre spaces.

1.3.2.
Homological multi-stability

The following simple example is quite enlightening. Consider the
E_{2}
-algebra
\coprod_{n\geq 0}X_{n}
where
X_{n}=\mathrm{Conf}_{n}^{\text{rb}}(I^{2})
is the space of configurations of
n
points in the interior of
I^{2}=[0,1]^{2}
, each of which is coloured either red or blue. This is the free
\mathbb{N}
-graded
E_{2}
-algebra on two generators both of grading 1. Taking
\mathbbm{k}
-chains gives
\mathbf{RB}\simeq\mathbb{E}_{2}(S^{1,0}r\oplus S^{1,0}b)
. This has
\pi_{*,0}(\mathbf{RB})=\mathbbm{k}[r,b]
a polynomial ring on two generators, so
\mathbf{RB}
cannot have ordinary homological stability in the sense of the previous section, as the ranks of these groups grow.

However
\mathbf{RB}
does enjoy the following property: the square

(1.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{RB}\otimes S^{2,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{RB}\otimes S^{1,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{RB}\otimes S^{1,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{RB},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\cdot r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\cdot b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\cdot b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\cdot r}

which is commutative up to a preferred homotopy, is (co)cartesian in a range of degrees. This is immediate from the known homology of the free
E_{2}
-algebra
\mathbb{E}_{2}(S^{1,0}r\oplus S^{1,0}b)
, given by the work of F. Cohen
[
10
]
. In fact, the total homotopy cofibre
\mathbf{RB}/(r,b)
of this square has
\pi_{n,d}(\mathbf{RB}/(r,b))=0
for
d<\tfrac{1}{2}n
.

Viewing this square as being
cocartesian
in a range of degrees, it gives a way to determine
\mathbf{RB}(n)
, in a range of degrees, from
\mathbf{RB}(n-1)
and
\mathbf{RB}(n-2)
, namely as the pushout of the diagram obtained by removing the bottom-right corner. Similarly, viewing it as being
cartesian
in a range of degrees it gives a way to determine
\mathbf{RB}(n)
, in a range of degrees, from
\mathbf{RB}(n+1)
and
\mathbf{RB}(n+2)
, namely as the corresponding pullback. Either interpretation lets us reasonably refer to the high (co)cartesianness of this square as a kind of homological stability theorem for
\mathbf{RB}
. (In ordinary homology stability we try to approximate
\mathbb{R}(n)
using
\{\mathbb{R}(m)\}_{m<n}
or
\{\mathbb{R}(m)\}_{m>n}
, and the recipe for doing so is
\mathbb{R}(n-1)
or
\mathbb{R}(n+1)
respectively: in the situation at hand the recipe is just slightly more complicated.) The slight downside is that at the level of homotopy groups (
1.1
) only gives a Mayer–Vietoris-style sequence

\pi_{n-2,d}(\mathbf{RB})\overset{(r_{*},b_{*})}{\longrightarrow}\pi_{n-1,d}(\mathbf{RB})\oplus\pi_{n-1,d}(\mathbf{RB})\overset{(\begin{smallmatrix}b_{*}\\
-r_{*}\end{smallmatrix})}{\longrightarrow}\pi_{n,d}(\mathbf{RB})\overset{\partial}{\longrightarrow}\pi_{n-2,d-1}(\mathbf{RB})

valid in a stable range. This is not exactly a recipe for determining
\pi_{n,*}(\mathbf{RB})
from
\{\pi_{m,*}(\mathbf{RB})\}_{m<n}
, but will have to do. (In fact in this particular example, and when
\mathbbm{k}
a field, the connecting maps
\partial
are zero. But this cannot be expected in general, see Example
10.1
.)

1.3.3.
Secondary homological stability

We describe again the “secondary homological stability” for mapping class groups of surfaces, in the terms we have now introduced. There is a
\mathbf{MCG}\in\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{2}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{Z})^{\mathbb{Z}})
such that
\mathbf{MCG}(g)\simeq C_{*}(\Gamma_{g,1};\mathbb{Z})
, the
\mathbb{Z}
-chains of the mapping class group of a genus
g
surface with one boundary. It has ordinary homological stability using the generator
\sigma
of
H_{0}(\Gamma_{1,1};\mathbb{Z})=\pi_{1,0}(\mathbf{MCG})
, in the sense that
\pi_{g,d}(\mathbf{MCG}/\sigma)=0
for
d<\tfrac{2}{3}g
. Furthermore, Galatius, Kupers, and the author
[
15
]
have constructed a left
\mathbf{MCG}
-module map

\varphi:\mathbf{MCG}/\sigma\otimes S^{3,2}\longrightarrow\mathbf{MCG}/\sigma

and shown that its cofibre
\mathbf{MCG}/(\sigma,\varphi)
satisfies
\pi_{g,d}(\mathbf{MCG}/(\sigma,\varphi))=0
for
d<\tfrac{3}{4}g
. The map
\varphi
does
not
come from an endomorphism of
\mathbf{MCG}
reduced modulo
\sigma
: it is essential that
\sigma
be killed before
\varphi
can be defined.

1.4.
A general higher-order homological stability theorem

The examples of the previous section lead us to set our goal as follows: a higher order homological stability theorem for
\mathbb{R}
means producing an
\mathbb{R}
-module
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
as the iterated cofibre of a sequence of endomorphisms, and proving that it has a vanishing line for its homotopy groups.

We will usually assume that
\mathbbm{k}
is a field and
\mathbb{R}
is an
E_{2}
-algebra in
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
satisfying:

(C)

It is
connected
in the sense that

\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{R})=0\text{ for $d<0$ or $n<0$, and }1:\mathbbm{k}\to\pi_{0,0}(\mathbb{R})\text{ is an isomorphism.}

(SCE)

By (C) there is a unique augmentation
\epsilon:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbbm{k}
, making
\mathbbm{k}
into a
\mathbb{R}
-module and we will further assume the
standard connectivity estimate

\pi_{n,d}(\mathbbm{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{k})=0\text{ for }d<n.

(F)

It is
finite type
in the sense that

\pi_{n,n}(\mathbbm{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{k})\text{ is finite-dimensional for each $n$}.

In practice we often verify (F) by instead verifying:

(F
′
)

\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{R})
is finite-dimensional for each
n
and
d
.

That (F
′
) implies (F) is by running the bar spectral sequence. If (F) is only known to hold for
n<N
then all our results will go through in a range of degrees, but for simplicity we will not keep track of this.

For examples of
E_{2}
-algebras
\mathbb{R}\simeq\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}C_{*}(G_{n};\mathbbm{k})
coming from a sequence of groups, Axiom (C) always holds and Axiom (F
′
) is simply that each
H_{d}(G_{n};\mathbbm{k})
is finitely-generated. Axiom (SCE) has an interpretation in terms of the high-connectivity of the “splitting complex”
[
14
, Section 17.2]
associated to the groups
G_{n}
, and is known to hold in many examples. We will discuss some particular examples in the companion paper
[
48
]
, but here we shall instead focus on the consequences of satisfying these axioms.

Theorem A
.

Let
\mathbbm{k}
be a field of positive characteristic and
\mathbb{R}\in\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{2}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})
satisfy (C), (SCE), and (F). Let
\lambda<1
be given. Then there is a sequence of
\mathbb{R}
-module endomorphisms

(1.2)

\alpha_{i}:\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{i-1})\otimes S^{n_{i},d_{i}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{i-1}),\text{ for }1\leq i\leq r,

with cofibres
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{i})
, such that:

(i)

The sequence of slopes
\tfrac{d_{i}}{n_{i}}
is non-decreasing, they are all strictly less than
\lambda
, and they are all rational numbers of the form
\tfrac{k}{k+1}
.

(ii)

\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
has a vanishing line of slope
\lambda
, and in fact has a vanishing line of slope
\min\{\tfrac{k}{k+1}\,|\,k\in\mathbb{N}\text{ with }\lambda\leq\tfrac{k}{k+1}\}
.

(iii)

Each endomorphism
\alpha_{i}
is non-nilpotent.

(iv)

Furthermore, assuming that
\mathbb{R}
is
E_{3}
, the
\alpha
’s
\alpha_{s},\ldots,\alpha_{t}
having a fixed slope
\lambda^{\prime}<\lambda
may all be realised as endomorphisms of
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{s-1})
, and may be arranged into a coherently homotopy commutative cube.

Remark 1.1
.

(i)

With only the stated axioms one cannot expect a similar result for slopes
\lambda\geq 1
, see Section
10.2
.

(ii)

It was a surprise to the author that the analogue of this theorem is false for
\mathbbm{k}=\mathbb{Q}
, at least if we insist on property (
iii
): see Section
10.3
for a counterexample, which we learnt from Robert Burklund.

(iii)

We will prove more precise, and more technical, versions of this theorem in Sections
5
and
9
. Those sections develop a collection of tools to construct (non-nilpotent) endomorphisms of
\mathbb{R}
-modules, of more general scope than Theorem
A
.

(iv)

The additional assumption in part (
iv
) that
\mathbb{R}
be an
E_{3}
-algebra could be relaxed if one knew that certain structural results about connected graded commutative Hopf algebras over a field of positive characteristic also held in the non-commutative setting. We discuss this in Remark
9.3
.

By analogy with chromatic homotopy theory we call an
\mathbb{R}
-module
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{i-1})
obtained as the iterated cofibre of a sequence of inductively-defined endomorphisms a
Smith–Toda complex
. This name does not fit perfectly but the alternative “generalised Moore complex” suggests that there is a homology theory in which they realise a certain cyclic module, which we doubt is true.

Such complexes, and their non-nilpotent endomorphisms
\alpha_{i}
, lead to infinite families of non-trivial elements in
\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{R})
in exactly the same way (and with the same kind of indeterminacies and difficulties) that ordinary Smith–Toda complexes lead to the
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\ldots
families in the stable homotopy groups of spheres. See
[
49
, Sections 2.4, 2.5]
for an overview, which the reader will easily translate into our setting. We will not dwell on it here, as the discussion in the following section accounts for such patterns more systematically.

A significant distinction between our setting and ordinary chromatic homotopy theory is that our
\alpha_{i}
have no intrinsic meaning. They are not e.g. characterised by their effect on a suitable homology theory (though our discussion in Section
9
tries to come close to this), and this makes them quite different to the
v_{n}
’s. This presents a difficulty in proving Theorem
A
, which we will solve by working in a suitable deformation of the category of
\mathbb{R}
-modules in which analogues of the
\alpha_{i}
can be characterised and then running something close to the proof of the Periodicity Theorem of Hopkins–Smith
[
25
]
. But even here there is a basic distinction to the ordinary situation, in that our
\alpha_{i}
can be coarsely ordered by their slopes
\tfrac{d_{i}}{n_{i}}
but there can be several of the same slope and there is no preferred total ordering (or even basis) of the collection of
\alpha
’s of a given slope.

1.5.
Homology in a higher-order stable range

For an
\mathbb{R}
-module
\mathbb{M}
we propose the definition of its “stable homology up to slope
\lambda
” to be the Bousfield localisation
L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M})
of
\mathbb{M}
away from the class
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}^{f}
of all finite
\mathbb{R}
-modules with a slope
\lambda
vanishing line. This is a localisation at an essentially small class of compact objects, so can be constructed “telescopically” and is therefore smashing:
L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M})\simeq L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{M}
. We will give an introduction to these notions in Section
3
.

The existence of a Smith–Toda complex
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
as guaranteed by Theorem
A
allows us to establish some basic properties of these approximations.

Theorem B
.

Let
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
be a Smith–Toda complex with a slope
\lambda
vanishing line, and such that the sequence of slopes
\tfrac{d_{i}}{n_{i}}
is non-decreasing and are all
<\lambda
. Write
\lambda^{\prime}:=\tfrac{d_{r}}{n_{r}}
.

(i)

The homotopy fibre
C_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})
of the localisation map
\mathbb{R}\to L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})
has a vanishing line of slope
\lambda
, and also has a vanishing line of slope
\lambda^{\prime}
.

(ii)

If
\lambda^{\prime}<\bar{\lambda}\leq\lambda
then any finite
\mathbb{R}
-module with a slope
\bar{\lambda}
vanishing line in fact has a slope
\lambda
vanishing line. Equivalently
L^{f}_{\bar{\lambda}}=L^{f}_{\lambda}
.

Remark 1.2
.

We prove more precise, and more technical, versions of these statements in Theorems
4.4
and
4.8
.

Example 1.3
.

In the case of ordinary homological stability this recovers what we expect. If
\mathbb{R}
has a
\sigma\in\pi_{1,0}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\mathbb{R}/\sigma
has a slope
\lambda
vanishing line, then
L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})\simeq\sigma^{-1}\mathbb{R}
and the fibre of the map
\mathbb{R}\to\sigma^{-1}\mathbb{R}
clearly has a slope
\lambda
vanishing line. This is the ordinary meaning of “stable homology”. When
\mathbb{R}
arises from taking the
\mathbbm{k}
-chains of an
E_{2}
-algebra in spaces, the homology of this localisation is accessible via the Group-completion Theorem
[
41
]
, and indeed that has been the most successful method for calculating stable homology.

Example 1.4
.

A more interesting example is the
E_{2}
-algebra
\mathbf{RB}
from Section
1.3.2
, of chains on the configuration spaces of red-or-blue points in
I^{2}
, where
\mathbf{RB}/(r,b)
has a slope
\tfrac{1}{2}
vanishing line. In this case we will explain in Example
3.9
how
L^{f}_{1/2}(\mathbf{RB})
may be described by the homotopy cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{f}_{1/2}(\mathbf{RB})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{-1}\mathbf{RB}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{-1}\mathbf{RB}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{-1}b^{-1}\mathbf{RB}.}

For
\mathbbm{k}
a field one may calculate
\pi_{*,*}(\mathbf{RB})
and hence
\pi_{*,*}(L^{f}_{1/2}(\mathbf{RB}))
, using F. Cohen’s description of the homology of free
E_{2}
-algebras. We do so in Example
3.9
. Perhaps surprisingly, it is no longer connective: for example
\partial(r^{-1}b^{-1})\neq 0\in\pi_{-2,-1}(L^{f}_{1/2}(\mathbf{RB}))
.

1.5.1.
Quantisation of vanishing lines

When
\mathbbm{k}
is a field of positive characteristic and (C), (SCE), and (F) hold, so that Theorem
A
applies, it follows from Theorem
B
that a finite
\mathbb{R}
-module
\mathbb{M}
with a vanishing line of slope
\lambda<1
in fact has a vanishing line of slope
\min_{k}\{\tfrac{k}{k+1}\,|\,\lambda\leq\tfrac{k}{k+1}\}
. We call this phenomenon
quantisation of vanishing lines
. Applied to
\mathbb{R}/\sigma
it explains why all known ordinary homological stability theorems have slopes of the form
\tfrac{k}{k+1}
(when they are
<1
).

1.5.2.
Monochromatic layers and Adams periodicity

A consequence of the quantisation of vanishing lines is that the only distinct localisation functors for slopes
<1
are the
L^{f}_{k/k+1}
, which assemble into a tower

\mathbb{M}\longrightarrow\cdots\longrightarrow L^{f}_{3/4}(\mathbb{M})\longrightarrow L^{f}_{2/3}(\mathbb{M})\longrightarrow L^{f}_{1/2}(\mathbb{M})\longrightarrow 0,

letting us define the monochromatic objects

M^{f}_{k/k+1}(\mathbb{M}):=\mathrm{fib}(L^{f}_{k/k+1}(\mathbb{M})\to L^{f}_{k-1/k}(\mathbb{M})).

These objects have a slope
\tfrac{k-1}{k}
vanishing line, and enjoy periodicities of slope exactly
\tfrac{k-1}{k}
, governed by the endomorphisms
\alpha_{s},\ldots,\alpha_{t}
of this slope: we call this
Adams periodicity
. The most general formulation is quite technical, and applying it involves producing Smith–Toda complexes
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{s-1},\alpha_{s}^{p^{M_{s}}},\ldots,\alpha_{t}^{p^{M_{t}}})
and in particular making sense of this expression. We will explain this in Section
4.2
, but for now give the following representative example.

Example 1.5
.

Suppose an
E_{2}
-algebra
\mathbb{R}
has a
\sigma\in\pi_{1,0}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{R}/\sigma)=0
for
d<\tfrac{2}{3}n
, and a
\varphi:S^{3,2}\otimes\mathbb{R}/\sigma\to\mathbb{R}/\sigma
such that
\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{R}/(\sigma,\varphi))=0
for
d<\tfrac{3}{4}n
. (This is the case for
\mathbb{R}=\mathbf{MCG}
for example.) For some
N\in\mathbb{N}
suppose furthermore that
\mathbb{R}/\sigma^{N}
has an endomorphism
\varphi_{r}:S^{3r,2r}\otimes\mathbb{R}/\sigma^{N}\to\mathbb{R}/\sigma^{N}
such that
\mathbb{R}/(\sigma^{N},\varphi_{r})
has a slope
\tfrac{3}{4}
vanishing line (
\varphi_{r}
will usually be related to the
r
fold iterate of
\varphi
, though it is an endomorphism of a different object so work is needed to make this precise). Then Adams periodicity, which is Theorem
4.5
, provides isomorphisms

(\varphi_{r})_{*}:\pi_{n-3r,d-2r}(M^{f}_{3/4}(\mathbb{R}))\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\pi_{n,d}(M^{f}_{3/4}(\mathbb{R}))

defined for
d<\tfrac{2n+2N-6}{3}
.

By the methods used to prove Theorem
A
, when
\mathbbm{k}
is a field of positive characterstic and
\mathbb{R}
satisfies axioms (C), (SCE), and (F) then for each
N
one can indeed find an endomorphism
\varphi_{r}
of
\mathbb{R}/\sigma^{N}
for some
r=r(N)
such that
\mathbb{R}/(\sigma^{N},\varphi_{r})
has a slope
\tfrac{3}{4}
vanishing line. The above implies that each homotopy class
x\in\pi_{n,d}(M^{f}_{3/4}(\mathbb{R}))
lies in some “
\varphi
-periodic family”, but the period of this family depends on
d-\tfrac{2}{3}n
: the further
x
is from the vanishing line of
\pi_{*,*}(M^{f}_{3/4}(\mathbb{R}))
, the longer the period.

1.6.
Determining the kinds of stability

Theorem
A
guarantees that higher-order stabilisation maps
\alpha_{i}
exist, but does not say, for a given
\mathbb{R}
, what they are. Concretely: what slopes arise as the slope of an
\alpha_{i}
, or more generally how many
\alpha_{i}
’s there are of a given slope? For example:

(i)

In the example
\mathbf{RB}
we must form
\mathbf{RB}/(r,b)
to get a vanishing line of slope
\tfrac{1}{2}
, and it not possible to achieve this with
\mathbf{RB}/\sigma
for any single
\sigma
of slope 0.

(ii)

In the example
\mathbf{MCG}
the object
\mathbf{MCG}/\sigma
has a vanishing line of slope
\tfrac{2}{3}
, and we did not need to kill any endomorphism of slope
\tfrac{1}{2}
to achieve this.

What part of the structure of
\mathbf{RB}
versus
\mathbf{MCG}
accounts for these differences?

The object

\mathbbm{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{k}\simeq\mathrm{Bar}(\mathbb{R})

inherits an
E_{1}
-coalgebra structure, being the Koszul dual of the
E_{1}
-algebra
\mathbb{R}
, but as
\mathbb{R}
is in fact
E_{2}=E_{1}\otimes E_{1}
there is also a residual
E_{1}
-algebra structure, making
\mathbbm{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{k}
into an
E_{1}
-bialgebra. Axiom (SCE) says that the bigraded homotopy groups of this object below the diagonal vanish, and we collect its diagonal homotopy groups together to form

\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}:=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\pi_{n,n}(\mathbbm{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{k}).

This has the structure of a graded connected bialgebra, and so a Hopf algebra: we call it the
stability Hopf algebra
associated to
\mathbb{R}
. Perhaps the central message of this paper is the slogan:

\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}
precisely controls the kinds of stability that
\mathbb{R}
has.

We will make this slogan precise in Sections
8
and
9
, but the rough idea is as follows.

Taking cobar constructions gives an
E_{2}
-algebra map

\mathbb{R}\simeq\mathrm{Cobar}(\mathbbm{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{k})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Cobar}(\Delta_{\mathbb{R}})=:\mathbf{r},

and base-change along this map converts a Smith–Toda complex for
\mathbb{R}
to one for
\mathbf{r}
. We will provide a collection of tools which allow one to reverse this process: from the existence of a Smith–Toda complex for
\mathbf{r}
one may find a Smith–Toda complex of a similar form for
\mathbb{R}
. The most important of these tools is that
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbf{r}
satisfies a limited kind of
Nilpotence Theorem
: if
\psi
is a slope
\lambda<1
endomorphism of an
\mathbb{R}
-module
\mathbb{M}
which has a slope
\lambda
vanishing line, then
\psi
is nilpotent if and only if
\mathbf{r}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\psi
is.

The
E_{2}
-algebra
\mathbf{r}=\mathrm{Cobar}(\Delta_{\mathbb{R}})
has homotopy groups

\pi_{n,d}(\mathbf{r})=\mathrm{Cotor}^{n-d}_{\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}}(\mathbbm{k},\mathbbm{k})_{n},

i.e. the cohomology of the Hopf algebra
\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}
. Conversely, for any connected graded Hopf algebra
A
of finite type over a field
\mathbbm{k}
,
\mathbf{a}:=\mathrm{Cobar}(A)
is an object of
\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{2}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})
satisfying our axioms (C), (SCE), and (F), so the methods of this paper apply to it. In this sense any improvement beyond the slogan must take place purely in the realm of cohomology of connected graded Hopf algebras.

When
\mathbb{R}\simeq\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}C_{*}(G_{n};\mathbbm{k})
arises from a sequence of groups, axiom (SCE) is usually established by showing that certain
G_{n}
-complexes
T^{E_{1}}(n)
associated to these groups are wedges of
n
-spheres
[
14
, Definition 17.6]
. In this case there are
G_{n}
-modules
\mathrm{St}^{E_{1}}(n):=\widetilde{H}_{n}(T^{E_{1}}(n);\mathbbm{k})
, the
E_{1}
-Steinberg modules
, and there is a concrete description of the stability Hopf algebra as:

\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}H_{0}(G_{n};\mathrm{St}^{E_{1}}(n)).

For such examples one should try to understand
\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}
by analysing these coinvariants. There are very few
E_{2}
-algebras
\mathbb{R}
occurring “in nature” (e.g. those discussed in Section
1.3
) for which we have complete knowledge of
\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}
, and when we do it is usually because this Hopf algebra is an exterior algebra
\Lambda_{\mathbbm{k}}[\bar{\sigma}]
on one generator (which implies that
\mathbb{R}/\sigma
has a slope 1 vanishing line). But there are many examples where we have partial information about
\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}
. In the companion paper
[
48
]
we will discuss these from the point of view taken here.

Acknowledgements
.
Arriving at the formulation presented here has been a long process, with many extensive false turns: work of Palmieri
[
45
]
and of Krause
[
32
]
was valuable for convincing me that I was eventually on the right track. A conversation with Robert Burklund was extremely useful, and I particularly acknowledge the influence of his way of thinking on this work. Discussions with Søren Galatius, Jeremy Hahn, Maxime Ramzi, Dhruv Ranganathan, Jan Steinebrunner, and Kelly Wang have been clarifying, even though much of what was discussed has not made it in to this paper. I was partially supported by the ERC under the European Union’s Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme (grant agreement No. 756444), and by the Danish National Research Foundation through the Copenhagen Centre for Geometry and Topology (DNRF151).

2.
Foundations

The ideas we are trying to explain can no doubt be implemented in many possible frameworks, in a way that should make no essential difference to the underlying ideas. For concreteness we will work in an
\infty
-categorical setting, relying on Lurie’s
[
38
]
for technical support. It will sometimes be convenient to import results from
[
14
]
, which is written in model-categorical terms: we comment on this in Section
2.5
below. For readers familiar with that source we try to use compatible notation, with the significant exception that here an
E_{k}
-algebra
\mathbb{R}
is by default unital, and we occasionally pass to non-unital
E_{k}
-algebras by taking augmentation ideals, whereas in
[
14
]
the symbol
\mathbb{R}
typically denoted a non-unital
E_{k}
-algebra, which was occasionally unitalised.

2.1.
\mathbbm{k}
-modules

We write
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})
for the derived
\infty
-category of the commutative ring
\mathbbm{k}
, which we choose to consider as the category of (left)
H\mathbbm{k}
-module spectra
[
38
, Remark 7.1.1.16]
: as is typical, we often just write
\mathbbm{k}
for
H\mathbbm{k}
. This is a presentable
[
38
, Corollary 4.2.3.7]
stable
\infty
-category
[
38
, Corollary 7.1.1.5]
, and we write
\mathrm{map}_{\mathbbm{k}}(X,Y):=\mathrm{map}_{\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})}(X,Y)
for the mapping spectra in
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})
. Objects
X
of this category have homotopy groups

\pi_{d}(X):=[S^{d},X]_{\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})},

where
S^{d}:=\Sigma^{d}\mathbbm{k}
, and these have the structure of modules over
\mathbbm{k}=[\mathbbm{k},\mathbbm{k}]_{\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})}
.

As
\mathbbm{k}
is a commutative algebra object in spectra, the symmetric monoidal structure on spectra
[
38
, §4.8.2]
induces a symmetric monoidal structure
\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}
on
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})
, whose values may be computed by the two-sided bar construction
[
38
, Theorem 4.5.2.1]
. This monoidal structure preserves colimits in each variable
[
38
, Corollary 4.4.2.15]
. The homotopy groups of a tensor product may be approached by a (strongly convergent) Künneth spectral sequence
[
38
, Proposition 7.2.1.19]
:

(2.1)

E^{2}_{p,q}=\bigoplus_{q^{\prime}+q^{\prime\prime}=q}\mathrm{Tor}_{p}^{\mathbbm{k}}(\pi_{q^{\prime}}(X),\pi_{q^{\prime\prime}}(Y))\Longrightarrow\pi_{p+q}(X\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}Y).

For us
\mathbbm{k}
will usually be a field, or a PID, in which case this simplifies as usual.

The right adjoint to
-\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}X
defines internal mapping objects
\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbbm{k}}(X,Y)
. These are
\mathbbm{k}
-module spectra, with underlying spectra

\mathrm{map}_{\mathbbm{k}}(\mathbbm{k},\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbbm{k}}(X,Y))\simeq\mathrm{map}_{\mathbbm{k}}({\mathbbm{k}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}X},Y)\simeq\mathrm{map}_{\mathbbm{k}}(X,Y).

To not be overwhelmed by notation, we will write
\mathrm{map}_{\mathbbm{k}}(X,Y)
for these internal mapping
\mathbbm{k}
-module spectra.

2.2.
\mathbb{Z}
-graded objects

Our underlying category will usually be the presentable stable
\infty
-category

\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}=\mathsf{Fun}(\mathbb{Z},\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k}))

of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded objects in
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})
. For an object
X
of this category we write
X(n)\in\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})
for its value at
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Such objects have bigraded homotopy groups

\pi_{n,d}(X):=\pi_{d}(X(n))=[S^{n,d},X]_{\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}},

where
S^{n,d}
is the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded object which is
S^{d}
in grading
n
and zero otherwise.

This category is equipped with the Day convolution symmetric monoidal structure
[
38
, §2.2.6]
which we continue to write as
\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}
: explicitly

(X\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}Y)(n)\simeq\bigoplus_{n^{\prime}+n^{\prime\prime}=n}X(n^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}Y(n^{\prime\prime}).

From this formula it is clear that this monoidal structure also preserves colimits in each variable. The Künneth spectral sequence (
2.1
) yields a similar spectral sequence for computing the bigraded homotopy groups
\pi_{*,*}(X\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}Y)
.

The right adjoint to
-\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}X
defines internal mapping objects
\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbbm{k}}(X,Y)
, having values
\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbbm{k}}(X,Y)(n)\simeq\mathrm{map}_{\mathbbm{k}}(S^{n,0}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}X,Y)
.

2.3.
E_{k}
-algebras

Let
(\mathsf{C},\otimes,\mathbbm{1})
be a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category which is presentable, has all small limits, and such that the tensor product commutes with colimits in each variable. We write
\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{k}}(\mathsf{C})
for the
\infty
-category of (unital)
E_{k}
-algebras
[
38
, §5.1]
in
\mathsf{C}
, which is again presentable using
[
38
, Corollary 3.2.3.5]
. Similarly, we write
\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{k}}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\mathsf{C})
for the
\infty
-category of
E_{k}
-algebras
\mathbb{R}
equipped with an augmentation
\epsilon:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbbm{1}
. We usually write
\mathbb{I}:=\mathrm{fib}(\epsilon:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbbm{1})
, a non-unital
E_{k}
-algebra, calling it the augmentation ideal of
\mathbb{R}
.

Dunn–Lurie additivity
[
38
, §5.1.2]
allows us to identify

\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{k+\ell}}(\mathsf{C})\simeq\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{k}}(\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{\ell}}(\mathsf{C})),

similarly with augmentations.

If
\mathsf{C}
is stable (so that
\mathbb{R}\simeq\mathbbm{1}\oplus\mathbb{I}
) then taking augmentation ideals identifies
\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{k}}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\mathsf{C})
with the
\infty
-category
\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{k}^{\text{nu}}}(\mathsf{C})
of non-unital
E_{k}
-algebras. The various forgetful functors

\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{k}}(\mathsf{C})\to\mathsf{C}_{\mathbbm{1}/}\to\mathsf{C}\quad\quad\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{k}}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\mathsf{C})\to\mathsf{C}_{\mathbbm{1}//\mathbbm{1}}\to\mathsf{C}\quad\quad\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{k}^{\text{nu}}}(\mathsf{C})\to\mathsf{C},

for which we generically write
U
, all have left adjoint “free
E_{k}
-algebra” functors for which we write
\mathbb{E}_{k}^{(\text{nu})}(-)
, taking a little care with units and augmentations. We will often use F. Cohen’s description of
\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{E}_{k}(X))
for
X\in\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
when
\mathbbm{k}
is a field, in terms of the induced product, Browder bracket, and Dyer–Lashof operations. Our reference for this will be
[
14
, Section 16]
.

If
\mathsf{C}
is pointed then any object of
\mathsf{C}
may be considered as a non-unital
E_{k}
-algebra, defining a “trivial algebra” functor
Z^{E_{k}^{\text{nu}}}:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{k}^{\text{nu}}}(\mathsf{C})
. (This is inconvenient to implement in the way that operads are handled in
[
38
]
. Treating operads as monoids in symmetric sequences in
\mathsf{C}
—which has been compared with Lurie’s definition in
[
21
,
20
]
or in
[
50
, Section 5.2.5]
with our presentability hypothesis—it is simple: as
\mathsf{C}
is pointed there is a unique map
\epsilon:E_{k}^{\text{nu}}\to\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{SSeq}(\mathsf{C})}
of operads in
\mathsf{C}
, restricting along which gives the required functor
Z^{E_{k}^{\text{nu}}}=\epsilon^{*}:\mathsf{C}=\mathsf{Alg}_{\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{SSeq}(\mathsf{C})}}(\mathsf{C})\to\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{k}^{\text{nu}}}(\mathsf{C})
.) The trivial algebra functor has a left adjoint

Q^{E_{k}^{\text{nu}}}:\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{k}^{\text{nu}}}(\mathsf{C})\longrightarrow\mathsf{C},

the (derived) indecomposables. As
U^{E_{k}^{\text{nu}}}Z^{E_{k}^{\text{nu}}}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathsf{C}}
, on left adjoints we obtain
Q^{E_{k}^{\text{nu}}}\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nu}}(X)\simeq X
.

2.4.
Modules over an
E_{k}
-algebra

For
\mathbb{R}\in\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{k}}(\mathsf{C})
with
k\geq 1
, there is an
\infty
-category
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
of left
\mathbb{R}
-modules, obtained by forgetting down from
E_{k}
-algebras to
E_{1}
-algebras, using that the latter are equivalent to associative algebras
[
38
, Example 5.1.0.7]
, and invoking
[
38
, Definition 4.2.1.13]
. By the discussion in
[
38
, §4.2.3]
,
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
is presentable, has all small limits, and the forgetful map
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}\to\mathsf{C}
preserves limits and colimits. In particular, if
\mathsf{C}
is stable then so is
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
. A morphism
\phi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{S}
of unital
E_{1}
-algebras induces a functor

\mathrm{Res}^{\mathbb{S}}_{\mathbb{R}}:\mathbb{S}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod},

which preserves limits and colimits. It has a left adjoint
\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathbb{S}}_{\mathbb{R}}
, extension of scalars, which may be computed by the relative tensor product
\mathbb{S}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}-

[
38
, Proposition 4.6.2.17]
. A commonly-used example of this this construction is to apply it to the unit map
\mathbbm{k}\to\mathbb{R}
to obtain the free
\mathbb{R}
-module functor

\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}-:\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}.

Using Dunn–Lurie additivity and
[
38
, Corollary 4.8.5.20]
, if
k\geq 2
then the category
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
is equipped with an
E_{k-1}
-monoidal structure
\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}
, and if
\phi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{S}
is a map of
E_{k}
-algebras then extension of scalars
\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathbb{S}}_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{S}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}-:\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}\to\mathbb{S}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
obtains the structure of an
E_{k-1}
-monoidal functor. The underlying bifunctor

-\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}-:\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}\times\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}

may be described in terms of the relative tensor product as
A_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\mathbb{R}}(-\otimes-)
, for the
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\mathbb{R}
-bimodule
A_{\mathbb{R}}
obtained by restricting the
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathbb{R}
-bimodule
\mathbb{R}
along the pair of algebra maps
(Id,\mu):(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\mathbb{R})\to(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
, where
\mu
is the
E_{k-1}
-algebra multiplication on
\mathbb{R}
in the category of associative algebras given by Dunn–Lurie additivity. (The bimodule
A_{\mathbb{R}}
was constructed explicitly in
[
14
, §12.2.2]
and called the “adapter”.)

We will usually have a fixed
E_{2}
-algebra
\mathbb{R}
in
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
, and work in the
(
E_{1}
-)
monoidal stable
\infty
-category
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
. As this category is only monoidal, some points must be considered a little more carefully than usual. One is that it is convenient to have a supply of central objects, i.e. objects for which left and right tensoring are equivalent. The most common central objects are free
\mathbb{R}
-modules, via

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}X)\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}-

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Ind}_{\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\mathbb{R}}^{\mathbb{R}}[(\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}X)\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}-]\simeq\mathrm{Ind}_{\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\mathbb{R}}^{\mathbb{R}}[\mathrm{Ind}_{\mathbbm{k}}^{\mathbb{R}}(X)\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\mathrm{Ind}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathbb{R}}(-)]

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathbb{R}}_{\mathbbm{k}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\mathbb{R}}[X\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}-]

and the same showing
-\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}X)\simeq\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathbb{R}}_{\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\mathbbm{k}}[-\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}X]
; the symmetry of
\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}
shows these are the same as
\mathbb{R}
-modules. Another kind of central object is as follows.

Lemma 2.1
.

If
\phi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{S}
is a map of
E_{k}
-algebras with
k\geq 2
, then there is a natural equivalence
\mathbb{S}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}-\simeq-\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{S}:\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}\to\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
.

Proof.

If
k\geq 3
then
\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}
is
E_{2}
-monoidal which gives such an isomorphism. If
k=2
then let
A_{\mathbb{R}}
be the
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathbb{R}\otimes\mathbb{R}
-bimodule discussed above, and consider

A_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}\otimes\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{R})

as an
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathbb{R}
-bimodule (i.e. neglect the right
\mathbb{S}
-module structure). We claim this is equivalent to
\mathbb{S}
as a
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathbb{R}
-bimodule, and the same for
A_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}\otimes\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{R}\otimes\mathbb{S})
, which together proves the lemma.

Functoriality of the construction of the bimodules
A
gives a map of
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathbb{R}\otimes\mathbb{R}
-bimodules
A_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathrm{Res}^{\mathbb{S}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{S}}_{\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathbb{R}\otimes\mathbb{R}}A_{\mathbb{S}}\simeq\mathrm{Res}^{\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{R}}_{\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathbb{R}\otimes\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{Res}^{\mathbb{S}\text{-}\mathbb{S}}_{\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{S}
, which is adjoint to a map

A_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}\otimes\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{R})\simeq\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{R}}_{\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathbb{R}\otimes\mathbb{R}}A_{\mathbb{R}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Res}^{\mathbb{S}\text{-}\mathbb{S}}_{\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{S}.

This is an equivalence: when we neglect the module structures the target is
\mathbb{S}
, the source is
\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{S}\simeq\mathbb{S}
, and the map is easily checked to be the identity. Restricting the right module structure along
\mathbb{R}\simeq\mathbbm{1}\otimes\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{R}
it remains an equivalence, and the target becomes
\mathbb{S}
as an
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathbb{R}
-bimodule.
∎

A further subtlety of the (
E_{1}
-)monoidal setting concerns internal mapping objects. The functor
-\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{M}
has a right adjoint
\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{r}(\mathbb{M},-)
, and similarly the functor
\mathbb{M}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}-
has a right adjoint
\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{l}(\mathbb{M},-)
. These two internal mapping objects will generally be different as
\mathbb{R}
-modules, but neglecting their
\mathbb{R}
-module structures they are equivalent as objects of
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
, via

\mathrm{map}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}S^{n,0},\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{r}(\mathbb{M},-))\simeq\mathrm{map}_{\mathbb{R}}((\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}S^{n,0})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{M},-)\simeq\mathrm{map}_{\mathbb{R}}(S^{n,0}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\mathbb{M},-)

\mathrm{map}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}S^{n,0},\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{l}(\mathbb{M},-))\simeq\mathrm{map}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{M}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}S^{n,0}),-)\simeq\mathrm{map}_{\mathbb{R}}(S^{n,0}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\mathbb{M},-).

We therefore write
\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{M},\mathbb{N})\in\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
for the underlying object of either of these mapping
\mathbb{R}
-modules, which is equivalently the object corepresenting
X\mapsto\mathrm{map}_{\mathbb{R}}((\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}X)\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{M},\mathbb{N})
.

A special case of this is the
endomorphism object

\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{M})
, an
E_{1}
-algebra in
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
universal among
E_{1}
-algebras acting on
\mathbb{M}
on the left
[
38
, Section 4.7.1]
. The above discussion identifies its underlying
\mathbb{R}
-module with
\underline{\mathrm{map}}^{r}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{M},\mathbb{M})
.

2.5.
Referring to “Cellular
E_{k}
-algebras”

Occasionally we cite results from
[
14
]
, which is developed with model category theoretic foundations instead of the
\infty
-categorical ones used here. We briefly explain how those results may be safely imported into the setting used here. For the underlying model category
\mathsf{S}
we take
H\mathbbm{k}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
, modules over the Eilenberg–MacLane spectrum for
\mathbbm{k}
, equipped with the projectively transferred model structure from symmetric spectra equipped with the absolute projective stable model structure. The discussion in Sections 7.2.4 and 7.3.3 of
[
14
]
shows that this satisfies the axioms of
[
14
, Section 7.1]
. We then take
\mathsf{C}=(H\mathbbm{k}\text{-}\mathsf{mod})^{\mathbb{N}}
, with the projective model structure.

To compare with the setting here, we can use Haugseng’s rectification theorem
[
20
, Theorem 1.1]
for algebras over an operad. The symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathsf{Sp}
of spectra can be presented as the
\infty
-categorical localisation at the weak equivalences of the 1-category of symmetric spectra
[
38
, Proposition 4.1.7.4]
, so by Haugseng’s theorem the
\infty
-categorical localisation of the 1-category
H\mathbbm{k}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
is equivalent to
H\mathbbm{k}
-modules in the
\infty
-category
\mathsf{Sp}
, and hence to
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})
by
[
38
, Remark 7.1.1.16]
. Similarly, the
\infty
-categorical localisation at the weak equivalences of the 1-categories
\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{2}}((H\mathbbm{k}\text{-}\mathsf{mod})^{\mathbb{N}})
used in
[
14
]
are the
\infty
-categories
\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{2}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{N}})
of
\mathbb{N}
-graded
E_{2}
-algebras, which can then be included into
\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{2}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})
(by axiom (C) all the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
E_{2}
-algebras we consider come from
\mathbb{N}
-graded ones). Similarly, an associative algebra object
A
in
(H\mathbbm{k}\text{-}\mathsf{mod})^{\mathbb{N}}
(such as the strictifications
\overline{\mathbb{R}}
constructed in
[
14
, Section 12.2]
) presents an associative (equivalently
E_{1}
-) algebra
A^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{N}}
, and the localisation at the weak equivalences of the 1-category of
A
-modules is equivalent to the
\infty
-category of
A^{\prime}
-modules in
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{N}}
, i.e. the non-negatively graded
A^{\prime}
-modules in
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
.

By this mechanism the results of
[
14
]
may be freely imported: we will not comment on it further.

2.6.
Bar, Cobar, and Koszul duality

We follow
[
38
, Section 5.2]
. The category of
E_{1}
-coalgebras in a symmetric monoidal category
\mathsf{C}
is
\mathsf{coAlg}_{E_{1}}(\mathsf{C}):=\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{1}}(\mathsf{C}^{op})^{op}
, and similarly for (co)augmented coalgebras. As long as e.g.
\mathsf{C}
has all limits and colimits, there are adjoint functors
[
38
, Theorem 5.2.2.17, Remark 5.2.2.19]

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Bar}:{\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{1}}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\mathsf{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{coAlg}_{E_{1}}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\mathsf{C})}:\mathrm{Cobar}.}

\dashv

(We change notation from
[
38
]
slightly by writing
\mathrm{Bar}
and
\mathrm{Cobar}
for the functors taking values in (co)algebras, rather than their underlying functors to
\mathsf{C}
.) For an augmented algebra
\mathbb{A}\to\mathbbm{1}
the underlying object of
\mathrm{Bar}(\mathbb{A})
may be calculated as the relative tensor product
\mathbbm{1}\otimes_{\mathbb{A}}\mathbbm{1}
, and hence as the colimit of a simplicial object of the form
[p]\mapsto\mathbb{A}^{\otimes p}
. Dually, for a coaugmented coalgebra
\mathbbm{1}\to\mathbb{C}
the underlying object of
\mathrm{Cobar}(\mathbb{C})
may be calculated as the relative cotensor product
\mathbbm{1}\square_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbbm{1}
(i.e. the relative tensor product in the opposite category), and hence as the limit of a cosimplicial object of the form
[p]\mapsto\mathbb{C}^{\otimes p}
. For these, see
[
38
, Remark 5.2.2.8]
.

By Dunn–Lurie additivity, taking
E_{k-1}
-algebras on both sides gives an adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Bar}:{\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{k}}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\mathsf{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{k-1}}(\mathsf{coAlg}_{E_{1}}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\mathsf{C}))}:\mathrm{Cobar}.}

\dashv

When
k=2
we will usually refer to objects on the right-hand side as (augmented)
E_{1}
-bialgebras. This also allows us to iterate
\mathrm{Bar}
, giving an adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Bar}^{(k)}:{\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{k}}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\mathsf{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{coAlg}_{E_{k}}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\mathsf{C})}:\mathrm{Cobar}^{(k)}.}

\dashv

We will write

B^{E_{k}}:\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{k}}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\mathsf{C})\overset{\mathrm{Bar}^{(k)}}{\longrightarrow}\mathsf{coAlg}_{E_{k}}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\mathsf{C})\longrightarrow\mathsf{C}

for the underlying object of the iterated bar construction; it is given by the
k
-fold iterated relative bar construction. For an augmented
E_{k}
-algebra
\epsilon:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbbm{1}
with augmentation ideal
\mathbb{I}
, there is a functorial equivalence

(2.2)

B^{E_{k}}(\mathbb{R})\simeq\mathbbm{1}\oplus\Sigma^{k}Q^{E_{k}^{\text{nu}}}(\mathbb{I}).

This is
[
13
, Theorem 2.41]
, in different notation. A model-categorical proof was given in
[
14
, Theorem 13.7]
.

The
\mathrm{Bar}
and
\mathrm{Cobar}
functors tend to yield an equivalence of categories when one restricts to suitably finitary objects on each side: see
[
22
]
for a complete analysis. For us the following simple setting will suffice. Say that an object
X\in\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
is
connected
if
\pi_{n,d}(X)=0
for
n<0
, or for
n=0
and
d\leq 0
, and let
\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{1}}^{\mathrm{aug,cn}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})
denote the full subcategory on those augmented algebras whose augmentation ideal is connected, and similarly
\mathsf{coAlg}_{E_{1}}^{\mathrm{aug,scn}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})
denote the the full subcategory on those coaugmented coalgebras whose
desuspended
coaugmentation coideal is connected (i.e. the coaugmentation coideal is “simply connected”).

Proposition 2.2
.

The functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Bar}:{\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{1}}^{\mathrm{aug,cn}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{coAlg}_{E_{1}}^{\mathrm{aug,scn}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})}:\mathrm{Cobar}}

\dashv

are adjoint equivalences.

Proof.

If
\mathbb{A}\to\mathbbm{k}
is an augmented
E_{1}
-algebra with augmentation ideal
\mathbb{I}
, then the skeletal filtration of the coaugmentation coideal of
\mathbbm{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{A}}\mathbbm{k}
has associated graded
\bigoplus_{i\geq 1}S^{0,i}\otimes\mathbb{I}^{\otimes i}
, whose desuspension is connected if
\mathbb{I}
is connected. Similarly, if
\mathbbm{k}\to\mathbb{C}
is a coaugmented
E_{1}
-coalgebra with coaugmentation coideal
\mathbb{J}
, then the coskeletal filtration of augmentation ideal of
\mathbbm{k}\square_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbbm{k}
has associated graded
\prod_{i\geq 1}S^{0,-i}\otimes\mathbb{J}^{\otimes i}
, which is connected if
S^{0,-1}\otimes\mathbb{J}
is connected. Thus
\mathrm{Bar}
and
\mathrm{Cobar}
indeed restrict to functors between the indicated categories.

To show that they are inverses, we must show that the unit
\mathbb{A}\to\mathrm{Cobar}(\mathrm{Bar}(\mathbb{A}))
and counit
\mathrm{Bar}(\mathrm{Cobar}(\mathbb{C}))\to\mathbb{C}
are equivalences. This may be done by repeating the argument of Krause
[
3
, Proposition B.4]
, though we must say something as our connectedness hypothesis is weaker than his.

If
\mathbb{C}\in\mathsf{coAlg}_{E_{1}}^{\mathrm{aug,scn}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})
has coaugmentation coideal
\mathbb{J}
then the fibre of the map
\mathrm{Tot}^{k}(\mathrm{Cobar}(\mathbb{C}))\to\mathrm{Tot}^{k-1}(\mathrm{Cobar}(\mathbb{C}))
is
S^{0,-k}\otimes\mathbb{J}^{\otimes k}=(S^{0,-1}\otimes\mathbb{J})^{\otimes k}
so has trivial
\pi_{n,d}
if either
n<0
,
d\leq 0
and
n<k
, or
0\leq d\leq k
and
d+n<k
. It follows that
\mathrm{Cobar}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathrm{Tot}^{k}(\mathrm{Cobar}(\mathbb{C}))
is an isomorphism on
\pi_{n,d}
for all large enough
k
. From this
[
3
, Lemma B.6]
follows.

The role of
[
3
, Lemma B.5]
can be played by applying
[
14
, Corollary 11.6]
as follows. If
f:X\to Y
is a map between connected (in the sense we are using here) objects such that
\pi_{n,d}(Y,X)=0
for
n<N
and for
n=N
and
d<D
, then
[
14
, Corollary 11.6]
implies that
f^{\otimes k}:X^{\otimes k}\to Y^{\otimes k}
has
\pi_{n,d}(Y^{\otimes k},X^{\otimes k})=0
for
n<N
and for
n=N
and
d<N+k-1
.

For
[
3
, Lemma B.7]
, if a map
\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{D}
in
\mathsf{coAlg}_{E_{1}}^{\mathrm{aug,scn}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})
is given on coaugmentation coideals by
f:\mathbb{J}\to\mathbb{K}
, and
\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C})=\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{J})=0
for
n<N
or for
n=N
and
d<D
, then it follows from the above that the maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{N,D}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C})=\pi_{N,D}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{J})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{N,D-1}(\mathrm{Cobar}(\mathbb{D}),\mathrm{Cobar}(\mathbb{C}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{N,D-1}(\mathrm{Tot}^{1}(\mathrm{Cobar}(\mathbb{D})),\mathrm{Tot}^{1}(\mathrm{Cobar}(\mathbb{C})))}

are isomorphisms. It follows that
\mathrm{Cobar}
detects equivalences; the case of
\mathrm{Bar}
is similar.
∎

In particular, if
\mathbb{R}
satisfies axiom (C) then
\mathbb{R}\to\mathrm{Cobar}(\mathrm{Bar}(\mathbb{R}))
is an equivalence.

2.7.
Filtered objects and spectral sequences

Write
\mathbb{Z}_{\leq}
for the poset of integers with its usual ordering, and let

\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}):=\mathsf{Fun}(\mathbb{Z}_{\leq},\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})

be the category of filtered objects in
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
. This is again stable, as limits and colimits are computed pointwise, and Day convolution endows it with a symmetric monoidal structure. We write objects of this category as
\mathrm{fil}_{*}X
, and the value of such a functor at
f\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq}
as
\mathrm{fil}_{f}X
. There are functors

(2.3)

(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})^{\mathbb{Z}}\overset{\mathrm{gr}}{\longleftarrow}\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})\overset{\operatorname*{colim}}{\longrightarrow}\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}

given by colimit and associated graded: the latter has components
\mathrm{gr}_{f}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}X)\simeq\mathrm{fil}_{f}X/\mathrm{fil}_{f-1}X
. Both functors are (strong) symmetric monoidal left adjoints.

We write
X:=\operatorname*{colim}\mathrm{fil}_{*}X\in\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
, and think of
\mathrm{fil}_{*}X
as a filtration of the object
X
. We say it is
complete
if
\lim\mathrm{fil}_{*}X\simeq 0
. In this case there is a conditionally convergent (in the sense of Boardman
[
7
]
) spectral sequence

(2.4)

E^{1}_{n,d,f}=\pi_{n,d}(\mathrm{gr}_{f}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}X))\Longrightarrow\pi_{n,d}(X)

with differentials
d^{r}:E^{r}_{n,d,f}\to E^{r}_{n,d-1,f-r}
. When we wish to emphasise the filtered object from which the spectral sequence is derived, we write
E^{r}_{n,d,f}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}X)
. We apologise for the indexing, which does not match anybody else’s.

2.7.1.
Filtered homotopy groups and
\tau

We find it convenient to work with filtered objects using some of the language which has been developed in the subject of synthetic spectra, but is really just about filtered objects. The following discussion is intended to be self-contained, but
[
9
, Section 11, Appendix A]
will be useful.

Evaluation at
f\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq}
defines a functor
f^{*}:\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})\to\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
which has a left adjoint
f_{*}:\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}\to\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})
. The filtered object
f_{*}Y
is 0 in filtrations
<f
and constantly
Y
in filtrations
\geq f
. We in particular have the filtered spheres

S^{n,d,f}:=f_{*}S^{n,d},

using which we define the (trigraded)
filtered homotopy groups

\pi_{n,d,f}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}X):=[S^{n,d,f},\mathrm{fil}_{*}X]_{\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})}=\pi_{n,d}(\mathrm{fil}_{f}X).

The identity map
S^{0,0}\to 1^{*}0_{*}S^{0,0}
is adjoint to a map

\tau:S^{0,0,1}\longrightarrow S^{0,0,0}.

We write
C\tau
for its cofibre. Tensoring with this map gives maps
\tau:S^{0,0,1}\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}X\to\mathrm{fil}_{*}X
and hence induced maps
\tau:\pi_{n,d,f}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}X)\to\pi_{n,d,f+1}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}X)
,
which we use to consider the filtered homotopy groups
\pi_{n,d,*}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}X)
as a graded
\mathbbm{k}[\tau]
-module.

There is an alternative perspective on filtered objects which can be useful. The lax symmetric monoidal functor
\prod_{f\in\mathbb{Z}}f^{*}:\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})\to(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})^{\mathbb{Z}}
has left adjoint
\bigoplus_{f\in\mathbb{Z}}f_{*}
and this adjunction is monadic (by
[
38
, Theorem 4.7.0.3]
, as the right adjoint detects equivalences and preserves all colimits, because these are both pointwise), identifying
\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})
with modules in
(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})^{\mathbb{Z}}
over the commutative algebra
(\prod_{f\in\mathbb{Z}}f^{*})(S^{0,0,0})
. This algebra can be identified as
\mathbbm{k}[\tau]
, the free commutative algebra in the 1-category
((\mathbbm{k}\text{-}\mathsf{mod})^{\mathbb{Z}})^{\mathbb{Z}}
, of bigraded
\mathbbm{k}
-modules, on a single generator of bigrading
(0,1)
. In this way
\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})
is nothing but the derived category of bigraded
\mathbbm{k}[\tau]
-modules. From this point of view the functor
\mathrm{gr}
is identified with base-change along
\mathbbm{k}[\tau]\to\mathbbm{k}=\mathbbm{k}[\tau]/\tau\simeq C\tau
, i.e.
(C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}X)(f)\simeq\mathrm{gr}_{f}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}X)
, and in particular
C\tau
obtains the structure of a commutative algebra in
\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})
. Similarly, the functor
\operatorname*{colim}
is identified with base-change along
\mathbbm{k}[\tau]\to\mathbbm{k}[\tau^{\pm 1}]
(the category of
\mathbbm{k}[\tau^{\pm 1}]
-modules in
(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})^{\mathbb{Z}}
is identified with
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
), so we allow ourselves to denote it by
\tau^{-1}
.

There are two canonical objects in
\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}))
associated to
\tau
. The first is

(
\beta
)

\cdots\longrightarrow S^{0,0,2}\overset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}S^{0,0,1}\overset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}S^{0,0,0}\overset{Id}{\longrightarrow}S^{0,0,0}\overset{Id}{\longrightarrow}S^{0,0,0}\longrightarrow\cdots

where the leftmost
S^{0,0,0}
is the value at
0\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq}
. Tensoring with
\mathrm{fil}_{*}X
it gives a filtered object in
\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})
whose colimit is
\mathrm{fil}_{*}X
, and whose limit has components

\left(\lim_{p\leq 0}S^{0,0,-p}\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}X\right)(f)\simeq\lim_{p\leq 0}\mathrm{fil}_{f+p}X,

so is trivial if and only if
\mathrm{fil}_{*}X
is complete. Its associated graded in grading
p\leq 0
is
S^{0,0,-p}\otimes C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}X
. Assuming
\mathrm{fil}_{*}X
is complete this gives a conditionally convergent spectral sequence

(2.5)

\prescript{\beta}{}{E}^{1}_{n,d,f,p}=\pi_{n,d,f+p}(C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}X)\Longrightarrow\pi_{n,d,f}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}X),

with differentials
\prescript{\beta}{}{d}^{r}:\prescript{\beta}{}{E}^{r}_{n,d,f,p}\to\prescript{\beta}{}{E}^{r}_{n,d-1,f,p-r}
. We can consider this as a spectral sequence of
\mathbbm{k}[\tau]
-modules starting with
\pi_{*,*,*}(C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}X)\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\mathbbm{k}[\tau]
, where the first factor has grading
(*,*,*,0)
and
\tau
has grading
(0,0,1,-1)
, and converging to the filtered homotopy groups
\pi_{*,*,*}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}X)
. It is called the
\tau
-Bockstein spectral sequence
.

The second is

(
I\beta
)

\cdots\longrightarrow S^{0,0,2}\overset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}S^{0,0,1}\overset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}S^{0,0,0}\overset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}S^{0,0,-1}\overset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}S^{0,0,-2}\longrightarrow\cdots

where
S^{0,0,0}
is the value at
0\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq}
. Tensoring with
\mathrm{fil}_{*}X
it gives a filtered object in
\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})
whose colimit is called
\tau^{-1}\mathrm{fil}_{*}X
, and whose limit is again trivial if and only if
\mathrm{fil}_{*}X
is complete. In this case it gives a conditionally convergent spectral sequence

(2.6)

\prescript{I\beta}{}{E}^{1}_{n,d,*,*}=\pi_{n,d,*}(C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}X)\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\mathbbm{k}[\tau^{\pm 1}]\Longrightarrow\pi_{n,d,*}(\tau^{-1}\mathrm{fil}_{*}X),

with differentials
\prescript{I\beta}{}{d}^{r}:\prescript{I\beta}{}{E}^{r}_{n,d,f,p}\to\prescript{I\beta}{}{E}^{r}_{n,d-1,f,p-r}
. It is called the
inverted
\tau
-Bockstein spectral sequence
.

The relation between the structure of the filtered homotopy groups
\pi_{*,*,*}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}X)
and these spectral sequences has been studied in depth by Burklund–Hahn–Senger
[
9
, Section 11, Appendix A]
, in the case of filtered objects arising from synthetic spectra. But much of their analysis goes through in the general setting of filtered objects. The three spectral sequences are related as follows. On one hand (
2.6
) is obtained by inverting
\tau
in the spectral sequence (
2.5
). On the other hand
\pi_{n,d,*}(\tau^{-1}\mathrm{fil}_{*}X)=\tau^{-1}\pi_{n,d,*}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}X)=\pi_{n,d}(X)\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\mathbbm{k}[\tau^{\pm 1}]
and (
2.6
) is obtained from (
2.4
) by tensoring with
\mathbbm{k}[\tau^{\pm 1}]
. By specialising to
\tau=1
it follows that (
2.6
) is interchangeable with (
2.4
). Finally, whenever there is a differential
d^{r}([x])=[y]
in the spectral sequence (
2.4
), there is a corresponding differential
\prescript{\beta}{}{d}^{r}([x\otimes 1])=[y\otimes\tau^{r}]
in the spectral sequence (
2.5
), and furthermore all differentials in the latter spectral sequence arise in this way: this may be seen by adapting
[
9
, Theorem A.8 (3)]
.

Finally, let us comment on multiplicative aspects of these spectral sequences. Both (

β

) and (

⁢
I
β

) can be given the structure of commutative monoids in
\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}))
, compatibly with the natural morphism
\beta\to I\beta
in this category. This is most readily seen by observing that they both arise in an evident way from filtered objects in the 1-category of
\mathbbm{k}[\tau]
-modules in
((\mathbbm{k}\text{-}\mathsf{mod})^{\mathbb{Z}})^{\mathbb{Z}}
, where the claim is quickly verified. It follows that

\beta\otimes-:\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})\longrightarrow\beta\text{-modules in }\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}))

is strong symmetric monoidal, and similarly for
I\beta
, and hence that the functors

\beta\otimes-,I\beta\otimes-:\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})\longrightarrow\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}))

are both lax symmetric monoidal. From this it follows that a pairing
\mathrm{fil}_{*}X\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}Y\to\mathrm{fil}_{*}Z
of filtered objects induces a pairing

E^{r}_{*,*,*}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}X)\otimes E^{r}_{*,*,*}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}Y)\longrightarrow E^{r}_{*,*,*}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}Z)

of spectral sequences, for each of (
2.5
) and (
2.6
) (and hence also (
2.4
)). In particular, if
\mathrm{fil}_{*}X
has a multiplicative structure then they are all multiplicative spectral sequences. Furthermore, if
\mathrm{fil}_{*}X
has the structure of an
E_{k}
-algebra in
\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})
then these spectral sequences have a Browder bracket, Dyer–Lashof operations, and so on: see
[
14
, Section 16.6]
for how these operations interact with differentials.

2.7.2.
The canonical multiplicative filtration

If
\mathbb{R}
is an
E_{k}
-algebra in
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
equipped with an augmentation
\epsilon:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbbm{k}
, then it has a canonical filtration
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}
to be thought of as the filtration by powers of the augmentation ideal

\mathbb{I}_{\mathbb{R}}:=\mathrm{fib}(\epsilon:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbbm{k}).

It has been discussed in various contexts by several authors under several names:
[
19
]
,
[
33
]
,
[
14
, Section 5.4.2]
.
Following the latter it may be constructed as follows. We first consider non-unital
E_{k}
-algebras in
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
, and consider the functor

(2.7)

(-1)^{*}:\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{k}^{\text{nu}}}(\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}))\longrightarrow\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{k}^{\text{nu}}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})

given by evaluating at filtration
-1
. (This induces a map on non-unital
E_{k}
-algebras, as
(-1)^{*}:\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})\to\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
is lax symmetric monoidal, because
(-1)+(-1)\leq-1\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq}
.) This has a left adjoint

(-1)_{*}^{E_{k}}:\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{k}^{\text{nu}}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{k}^{\text{nu}}}(\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})).

This is constructed and discussed 1-categorically in
[
14
, Section 5.4.1]
;
\infty
-categorically it may be obtained by the adjoint functor theorem
[
37
, Corollary 5.5.2.9]
, using that (
2.7
) is a functor between presentable categories which preserves limits and filtered colimits (because such (co)limits are formed underlying in
E_{k}
-algebras). The canonical multiplicative filtration of a non-unital
E_{k}
-algebra
\mathbb{I}
is

\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{E_{k}}\mathbb{I}:=(-1)^{E_{k}}_{*}(\mathbb{I}).

Its associated graded is calculated in terms of the indecomposables by

\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{E_{k}}\mathbb{I})\simeq\mathbb{E}_{k}^{\text{nu}}((-1)_{*}(Q^{E_{k}^{\text{nu}}}(\mathbb{I}))),

which follows from the corresponding identity between right adjoints of all these functors (see
[
14
, Section 5.4.3]
for a 1-categorical treatment).

We extend this to the setting of an augmented
E_{k}
-algebra
\epsilon:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbbm{k}
by

\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{E_{k}}\mathbb{R}:=S^{0,0,0}\oplus\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{E_{k}}\mathbb{I}_{\mathbb{R}},

having associated graded
\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{E_{k}}\mathbb{R})=S^{0,0,0}\oplus\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{E_{k}}\mathbb{I}_{\mathbb{R}})\simeq\mathbb{E}_{k}((-1)_{*}(Q^{E_{k}^{\text{nu}}}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathbb{R}})))
. As long as
\mathbb{R}
satisfies axiom (C) the canonical multiplicative filtration
\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{E_{k}}\mathbb{R}
is complete, by the Claim in the proof of
[
14
, Theorem 10.20]
. In fact,
\pi_{n,d}(\mathrm{fil}_{-a}^{E_{k}}\mathbb{R})=0
for
d\leq a-n
.

Proposition 2.3
.

There is a natural isomorphism

B^{E_{\ell}}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{E_{k}}-)\simeq\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{E_{k-\ell}}B^{E_{\ell}}(-):\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{k}}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{k-\ell}}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})).

Proof.

We establish the corresponding identity among the right adjoints to all these functors. Firstly, we claim that the right adjoint
\Omega^{E_{\ell}}
to

B^{E_{\ell}}:\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\mathsf{D})\longrightarrow\mathsf{D}

is natural in
\mathsf{D}
a symmetric monoidal category and lax monoidal functors
\phi:\mathsf{D}\to\mathsf{D}^{\prime}
. To see this, we must show that the Beck–Chevalley morphism
\phi\circ\Omega^{E_{\ell}}\Rightarrow\Omega^{E_{\ell}}\circ\phi
is an equivalence. We may test this after applying the forgetful functor
U^{E_{\ell}}:\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\mathsf{D}^{\prime})\to\mathsf{D}^{\prime}
, as this is conservative. But
U^{E_{\ell}}
and
U^{E_{\ell}}(\Omega^{E_{\ell}}(-))\simeq\mathbbm{1}\oplus\Sigma^{-\ell}(-)
commute with
\phi
, so it is indeed an equivalence.

We apply this to the lax symmetric monoidal functor

\phi=S^{0,0}\oplus(-1)^{*}(-):\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{k-\ell}}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}))\longrightarrow\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{k-\ell}}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})

to obtain the required commutative square

\hbox to255.04pt{\vbox to55.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 127.51627pt\lower-27.29283pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

We will consider
E_{k}
-algebras with their canonical multiplicative filtration unless otherwise emphasised, so we simplify notation to

\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}:=\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{E_{k}}\mathbb{R}.

2.8.
Some
t
-structures

The stable
\infty
-category
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})
has the standard Postnikov
t
-structure
[
38
, Definition 1.3.5.16, Proposition 1.3.5.21]
, whose connective (resp. coconnective) objects are those whose homotopy groups are supported in positive (resp. negative) degrees. Its heart is the abelian 1-category of
\mathbbm{k}
-modules. It is compatible with filtered colimits (i.e. the coconnective objects are closed under filtered colimits) and with the symmetric monoidal structure (i.e. the unit is connective and the connective objects are closed under tensor products). Any object
X
obtains a Postnikov (or Whitehead) filtration
\tau_{\geq-*}X

\cdots\longrightarrow\tau_{\geq 1}X\longrightarrow\tau_{\geq 0}X\longrightarrow\tau_{\geq-1}X\longrightarrow\tau_{\geq-2}X\longrightarrow\cdots

which is complete and whose colimit is
X
. Its associated graded has
\mathrm{gr}_{f}(\tau_{\geq-*}X)\simeq\Sigma^{-f}\pi_{f}(X)
, where we consider
\pi_{f}(X)
as an object of (the heart of)
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})
.

We obtain a Postnikov
t
-structure on
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
by doing the above in each grading. Its heart is the abelian 1-category of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathbbm{k}
-modules. It is again compatible with filtered colimits and with the (Day convolution) symmetric monoidal structure.

If
\mathbb{R}\in\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{1}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})
is connective (i.e.
\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{R})=0
for
d<0
) then
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
has a
t
-structure whose connective (resp. coconnective) objects are those which are connective (resp. coconnective) in
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
(adapt
[
38
, Proposition 7.1.1.13]
to the graded setting). Its heart is the abelian 1-category of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\pi_{*,0}(\mathbb{R})
-modules. In particular the Postnikov filtration
\tau_{\geq-*}\mathbb{M}\in\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})
of an
\mathbb{R}
-module
\mathbb{M}
promotes to an object of
\mathsf{fil}(\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod})
. Its associated graded has
\mathrm{gr}_{f}(\tau_{\geq-*}\mathbb{M})\simeq\Sigma^{-f}\pi_{*,f}(\mathbb{M})
, where
\pi_{*,f}(\mathbb{M})
is an
\mathbb{R}
-module via
\mathbb{R}\to\tau_{\leq 0}\mathbb{R}=\pi_{*,0}(\mathbb{R})
.

We will also have cause to consider a sheared
t
-structure on
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
, the
diagonal
t
-structure
, in which an object
X
is connective (resp. coconnective) if each
\Sigma^{-n}X(n)
is connective (resp. coconnective) in the Postnikov
t
-structure on
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})
. It is elementary to see that this is indeed a
t
-structure, that its heart is again the abelian 1-category of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathbbm{k}
-modules, and that it is compatible with the (Day convolution) symmetric monoidal structure. We write
\tau^{\text{diag}}
for its truncation functors.

If
(\mathsf{C}_{\geq 0},\mathsf{C}_{\leq 0})
is a
t
-structure on a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathsf{C},\otimes,\mathbbm{1})
which is compatible with with a symmetric monoidal structure, then by
[
38
, Example 2.2.1.10]
the heart
\mathsf{C}^{\heartsuit}=(\mathsf{C}_{\geq 0})_{\leq 0}
has a symmetric monoidal structure such that the truncation functor
\tau_{\leq 0}:\mathsf{C}_{\geq 0}\to\mathsf{C}^{\heartsuit}
is strong symmetric monoidal (so the symmetric monoidal structure on the heart is calculated as
\tau_{\leq 0}(-\otimes-)
). We already used this implicitly above when discussing the map of
E_{1}
-algebras
\mathbb{R}\to\tau_{\leq 0}\mathbb{R}=\pi_{*,0}(\mathbb{R})
. In Section
8
we will further use it as follows: if
A
is connective with respect to the diagonal
t
-structure on
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
, and has the structure of an
E_{1}
-bialgebra, then

\tau_{\leq 0}^{\text{diag}}A\simeq\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{n,n}(A)

has the structure of a bialgebra object in
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathbbm{k}
-modules.

Warning 2.4
.

In this setting the inclusion
\iota:\mathsf{C}^{\heartsuit}\to\mathsf{C}_{\geq 0}
is right adjoint to the strong symmetric monoidal functor
\tau_{\leq 0}
and so is lax symmetric monoidal. In particular, it preserves
E_{1}
-algebra objects, but it need not preserve
E_{1}
-coalgebra objects! This is because for a coalgebra
C\in\mathsf{C}^{\heartsuit}
to give a coalgebra in
\mathsf{C}
one needs a map
\iota(C)\to\iota(C)\otimes\iota(C)
, but what one has is a map
\iota(C)\to\iota(C\otimes C)=\tau_{\leq 0}(\iota(C)\otimes\iota(C))
. However
\iota
becomes strong symmetric monoidal if we restrict to the subcategory of
\mathsf{C}^{\heartsuit}
consisting of flat objects.

Returning to the example, and heeding the warning, as long as each
\pi_{n,n}(A)
is a flat
\mathbbm{k}
-module then the
E_{1}
-bialgebra
\tau_{\leq 0}^{\text{diag}}A
in the heart of
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
is also an
E_{1}
-bialgebra in
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
itself. We will usually enforce this flatness by taking
\mathbbm{k}
to be a field.

2.9.
Thick subcategories

In the stable categories
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
or
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
(or indeed
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
or
\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})
) we say that a full subcategory
\mathsf{C}
is
thick
whenever:

(i)

If
A\to B\to C
is a cofibre sequence and two of these objects are in
\mathsf{C}
, then so is the third.

(ii)

If
A
is in
\mathsf{C}
then so is
S^{n,d}\otimes A
(or
S^{n,d,f}\otimes A
in the case of filtered objects).

(iii)

If
A
is in
\mathsf{C}
then so is any retract of it.

Our second axiom differs slightly from the norm: usually
\mathsf{C}
is only assumed closed under (de)suspensions, but we wish to allow shifts by all bigraded (filtered) spheres. We will say
the thick subcategory generated by
X
to mean the smallest thick subcategory containing
X
. We collect some elementary facts about them as follows.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
U
be in the thick subcategory of the monoidal unit and
f:U\otimes X\to X
be a morphism: form its iterates
f^{k}
by precomposing
f
with
U\otimes f
and so on.

(i)

X/f
is in the thick subcategory generated by
X
.

(ii)

For any
k\geq 1
,
X/f^{k}
is in the thick subcategory generated by
X/f
.

(iii)

If
f^{k}\simeq 0
then
X
is in the thick subcategory generated by
X/f
.

(iv)

X/f
and
X/f^{k}
generate the same thick subcategories.

Proof.

As
U
is in the thick subcategory of the monoidal unit, for any
Y
it follows that
U\otimes Y
is in the thick subcategory generated by
\mathbbm{1}\otimes Y=Y
. We will use this principle often.

(
i
) The cofibre sequence
U\otimes X\overset{f}{\to}X\to X/f
shows that
X/f
is in the thick subcategory generated by
X
.

(
ii
) The factorisation
f^{k}:U\otimes(U^{\otimes k-1})\overset{A\otimes f^{k-1}}{\to}U\otimes X\overset{f}{\to}X
gives a cofibre sequence
U\otimes X/f^{k-1}\to X/f^{k}\to X/f
. By induction
X/f^{k-1}
is in the thick subcategory generated by
X/f
, so
U\otimes X/f^{k-1}
is too, and hence by the cofibre sequence
X/f^{k}
is too.

(
iii
) If
f^{k}\simeq 0
then
X/f^{k}\simeq X\oplus\Sigma(U\otimes X)
contains
X
as a retract. So
X
is in the thick subcategory generated by
X/f^{k}
, so by (
ii
) is in the thick subcategory generated by
X/f
.

(
iv
) Consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\otimes k+1}\otimes X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\otimes k}\otimes X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\otimes 2}\otimes X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\otimes X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\otimes k}\otimes X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\otimes k-1}\otimes X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\otimes X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\otimes k}\otimes f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\otimes k}\otimes f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\otimes k-1}\otimes f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\otimes f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\otimes f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\otimes k-1}\otimes f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

and the total homotopy cofibre
C
of the outer rectangle. By taking horizontal cofibres in the outer rectangle first there is a cofibre sequence
U\otimes X/f^{k}\to X/f^{k}\to C
, so
C
is in the thick subcategory generated by
X/f^{k}
. On the other hand taking vertical cofibres first gives a cofibre sequence
U^{\otimes k}\otimes X/f\overset{\bar{f}^{k}}{\to}X/f\to C
. We will show that
\bar{f}^{k}
is null when
k>1
, so that
X/f
is a retract of
C
and so is in the thick subcategory generated by
X/f^{k}
.

To see this, note that
\bar{f}^{k}
is indeed the
k
-fold iterate of the map
\bar{f}:U\otimes X/f\to X/f
induced between vertical cofibres of the right-hand square (supposing of course that the homotopies making the other squares commute are obtained by applying
U^{\otimes i}\otimes-
to this one). By a well-known principle such a map satisfies
\bar{f}^{2}\simeq 0
: see e.g.
[
46
]
for an extensive discussion.
∎

2.10.
Finiteness

Recall that an object
X
of an
\infty
-category
\mathsf{C}
is called
compact
if
\mathrm{Map}_{\mathsf{C}}(X,-):\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{Spaces}
commutes with filtered colimits. If
\mathsf{C}
is stable, so has mapping spectra, then we may instead ask that
