\mathrm{map}_{\mathsf{C}}(X,-):\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{Sp}
commutes with filtered colimits. As
\mathrm{Map}_{\mathsf{C}}(X,-)\simeq\Omega^{\infty}\mathrm{map}_{\mathsf{C}}(X,-)
the latter implies the former, but as
\pi_{n}\mathrm{map}_{\mathsf{C}}(X,-)=\pi_{0}\mathrm{Map}_{\mathsf{C}}(\Sigma^{n}X,-)=\pi_{0}\mathrm{Map}_{\mathsf{C}}(X,\Sigma^{-n}-)
and
\Sigma^{-n}
preserves colimits the former implies the latter too.

In the stable categories
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
or
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
an object is called
finite
if it is in the thick subcategory generated by the monoidal unit.

Lemma 2.6
.

In
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
or
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
the finite objects are compact.

Proof.

By their definition the compact objects clearly have the 2-out-of-3 property for cofibre sequences, and are closed under retracts and tensoring with
\otimes
-invertible objects such as
S^{n,d}
or
S^{n,d,f}
. Thus they form a thick subcategory in the sense we gave in Section
2.9
. It suffices therefore to show that the monoidal units
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}
are compact.

If
L
is a left adjoint functor whose right adjoint preserves filtered colimits, then
L
preserves compact objects. Each of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{sSet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{k}\text{-chains}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\otimes-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}\otimes-}

is such a left adjoint, and the compact object
*\in\mathsf{sSet}
is sent to
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}
.
∎

Lemma 2.7
.

If
\mathbb{F}
is a finite object in
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
, and
\mathbb{M}
is an object in this category, then
\mathbb{F}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{M}
,
\underline{\mathrm{map}}^{r}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{F},\mathbb{M})
, and
\underline{\mathrm{map}}^{l}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{F},\mathbb{M})
are all in the thick subcategory generated by
\mathbb{M}
. Similarly in the category
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
.

Proof.

The functors
-\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{M}
,
\underline{\mathrm{map}}^{r}_{\mathbb{R}}(-,\mathbb{M})
, and
\underline{\mathrm{map}}^{l}_{\mathbb{R}}(-,\mathbb{M})
all preserve (co)fibre sequences, send shifts to shifts, and (being functors) preserve retracts, so the class of
\mathbb{R}
-modules which they send into the thick subcategory of
\mathbb{M}
is itself a thick subcategory. It certainly contains the monoidal unit, so contains all finite objects.
∎

2.11.
Duality

When
\mathbb{R}
is an
E_{2}
-algebra the category
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
(or
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
) is only
E_{1}
-monoidal, so as discussed in Section
2.4
it has left and right internal mapping objects: thus we must speak of left and right duals as in
[
38
, Section 4.6.1]
. By definition of the right mapping object there is an equivalence

\mathrm{map}_{\mathbb{R}}(-\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{M},-)\simeq\mathrm{map}_{\mathbb{R}}(-,\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{r}(\mathbb{M},-)).

Using this one may produce a natural map

(-)\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{r}(\mathbb{M},\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{r}(\mathbb{M},-),

and when
\mathbb{M}
is finite this is an equivalence (it is when evaluated at
\mathbb{R}
, and both sides preserve colimits when
\mathbb{M}
is finite and hence compact). This exhibits
D^{r}(\mathbb{M}):=\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{r}(\mathbb{M},\mathbb{R})
as right dual to
\mathbb{M}
; a similar calculation exhibits
D^{l}(\mathbb{M}):=\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{l}(\mathbb{M},\mathbb{R})
as left dual to
\mathbb{M}
.

Lemma 2.8
.

If
\mathbb{M}
and
\mathbb{N}
are dual finite objects in
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
, then they generate the same thick subcategory.

Proof.

The duality data

ev:\mathbb{N}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{M}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\quad\quad\quad coev:\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{N}

gives a factorisation
Id_{\mathbb{M}}:\mathbb{M}\overset{\mathbb{M}\otimes coev}{\to}\mathbb{M}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{N}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{M}\overset{ev\otimes\mathbb{M}}{\to}\mathbb{M}
which expresses
\mathbb{M}
as a retract of
\mathbb{M}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{N}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{M}
. As
\mathbb{M}
is a finite
\mathbb{R}
-module, so is in the thick subcategory generated by
\mathbb{R},

\mathbb{M}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{N}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{M}
is in the thick subcategory generated by
\mathbb{N}
, so
\mathbb{M}
is too. Similarly for the converse.
∎

The same discussion holds in
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
.

3.
Stable homology is Bousfield localisation

For an
\mathbb{R}
-module
\mathbb{M}
, we wish to develop the notion of the “best approximation to
\mathbb{M}
up to slope
\lambda
”. As we are mainly interested in
finite

\mathbb{R}
-modules, we propose that this be implemented as Bousfield localisation away from the (essentially small) class

\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}:=\{\text{finite $\mathbb{R}$-modules with a slope $\lambda$ vanishing line}\}.

We spell this out, closely following Miller
[
42
]
.

Definition 3.1
.

(i)

A
\mathbb{R}
-module
\mathbb{L}
is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-local
if
[\mathbb{T},\mathbb{L}]_{\mathbb{R}}=0
for all
\mathbb{T}\in\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
.

(ii)

A
\mathbb{R}
-module
\mathbb{A}
is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-acyclic
if
[\mathbb{A},\mathbb{L}]_{\mathbb{R}}=0
for every
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-local
\mathbb{L}
.

(iii)

An
\mathbb{R}
-module map
\phi:\mathbb{M}\to\mathbb{L}
is an
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-localisation
if
\mathbb{L}
is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-local and the fibre of
\phi
is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-acyclic.

As
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
is closed under shifts (i.e.
S^{n,d}\otimes-
with
n,d\in\mathbb{Z}
), in (i) it is equivalent to ask for the mapping object
\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{L})\in\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
to be trivial. As the
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-local objects are closed under shifts, in (ii) it is equivalent to ask for
\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{L})\simeq 0
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Any
\mathbb{R}
-module has an
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-localisation, which is unique up to homotopy equivalence.

In view of this proposition, we denote “the” localisation map by
\mathbb{M}\to L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M})
, and its fibre by
C_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M})
.

Proof of Proposition
3.2
.

Let us first discuss uniqueness. Let

\mathbb{A}_{i}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}\overset{\phi_{i}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{L}_{i}\quad\quad i=1,2

be fibre sequences with
\mathbb{L}_{i}

\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-local and
\mathbb{A}_{i}

\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-acyclic. As
[S^{n,d}\otimes\mathbb{A}_{1},\mathbb{L}_{2}]_{\mathbb{R}}=0
for all
(n,d)
, because
\mathbb{L}_{2}
is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-local and
\mathbb{A}_{1}
is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-acyclic, the long exact sequence obtained by applying
[-,\mathbb{L}_{2}]_{\mathbb{R}}
to the fibre sequence with
i=1
gives a bijection

(3.1)

-\circ\phi_{1}:[\mathbb{L}_{1},\mathbb{L}_{2}]_{\mathbb{R}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}[\mathbb{M},\mathbb{L}_{2}]_{\mathbb{R}}.

Under this bijection
\phi_{2}
corresponds to a map
\psi_{12}:\mathbb{L}_{1}\to\mathbb{L}_{2}
such that
\psi_{12}\circ\phi_{1}\simeq\phi_{2}
. The same with the indices reversed gives a map
\psi_{21}:\mathbb{L}_{2}\to\mathbb{L}_{1}
such that
\psi_{21}\circ\phi_{2}\simeq\phi_{1}
. Combining them gives
\psi_{21}\circ\psi_{12}\circ\phi_{1}\simeq\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{L}_{1}}\circ\phi_{1}:\mathbb{M}\to\mathbb{L}_{1}
which with the fact that (
3.1
) is injective shows
\psi_{21}\circ\psi_{12}\simeq\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{L}_{1}}
; the other composition is treated similarly.

For existence, first write
\mathbb{M}_{0}:=\mathbb{M}
. Define maps
\mathbb{M}_{0}\to\mathbb{M}_{1}\to\mathbb{M}_{2}\to\cdots
inductively by the cofibre sequences

(3.2)

\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\mathbb{T}]\in\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}/\sim\\
f:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{M}_{i}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{T}\overset{ev}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{M}_{i}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}_{i+1},

where the sum is over pairs of a homotopy type
[\mathbb{T}]
of object in
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}^{f}
(there is only a set of homotopy types of finite
\mathbb{R}
-modules) and a map
f:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{M}_{i}
from a representative of this homotopy type; the map
ev
on the
([\mathbb{T}],f)
-summand is given by
f
. We claim that the map
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}_{0}\to\mathbb{M}_{\infty}:=\operatorname*{colim}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{M}_{i}
is an
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-localisation.

To see that
\mathbb{M}_{\infty}
is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-local, let
\mathbb{T}\in\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
and consider a map
g:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{M}_{\infty}
. As
\mathbb{T}
is a finite
\mathbb{R}
-module it is compact, so
g
factors as
g:\mathbb{T}\overset{f}{\to}\mathbb{M}_{i}\to\mathbb{M}_{i+1}\to\mathbb{M}_{\infty}
. But the composition
\mathbb{T}\overset{f}{\to}\mathbb{M}_{i}\to\mathbb{M}_{i+1}
is nullhomotopic by definition of
\mathbb{M}_{i+1}
, and so
g
is nullhomotopic too.

To see that the fibre of
\mathbb{M}\to\mathbb{M}_{\infty}
is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-acyclic, let
\mathbb{L}
be
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-local and apply
\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(-,\mathbb{L})
to the cofibre sequences (
3.2
). As this sends sums to products and annihilates objects of
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
, it gives a sequence of equivalences

\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{M}_{0},\mathbb{L})\overset{\sim}{\longleftarrow}\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{M}_{1},\mathbb{L})\overset{\sim}{\longleftarrow}\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{M}_{2},\mathbb{L})\overset{\sim}{\longleftarrow}\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{M}_{3},\mathbb{L})\overset{\sim}{\longleftarrow}\cdots

and so, taking the limit, shows that

\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{M}_{\infty},\mathbb{L})\simeq\lim_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{M}_{i},\mathbb{L})\longrightarrow\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{M}_{0},\mathbb{L})=\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{M},\mathbb{L})

is an equivalence, for all
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-local
\mathbb{L}
. This is precisely the same as saying that the fibre of
\mathbb{M}\to\mathbb{M}_{\infty}
is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-acyclic.
∎

Its main properties are summarised as follows: again we follow Miller
[
42
]
.

Proposition 3.3
.

(i)

The class
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
is closed under left or right tensoring with finite
\mathbb{R}
-modules.

(ii)

The class of
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-acyclic
\mathbb{R}
-modules is closed under left or right tensoring with arbitrary
\mathbb{R}
-modules.

(iii)

The localisation
L^{f}_{\lambda}
is smashing, i.e.
L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{M})\simeq L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{M}\simeq\mathbb{M}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})
. Thus
L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R})
is an idempotent
\mathbb{R}
-algebra, and
L^{f}_{\lambda}
is Bousfield localisation with respect to it.

Proof.

For (
i
), note that left or right tensoring an object
\mathbb{T}
by a finite
\mathbb{R}
module gives an object in the thick subcategory generated by
\mathbb{T}
, as finite
\mathbb{R}
-modules are in the thick subcategory generated by the monoidal unit
\mathbb{R}
. If
\mathbb{T}
has a vanishing line of slope
\lambda
then anything in the thick subcategory it generates does too.

For (
ii
), let
\mathbb{A}
be
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-acyclic,
\mathbb{M}
be an
\mathbb{R}
-module, and
\mathbb{L}
be
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-local. Then

\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{M}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{A},\mathbb{L})\simeq\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{M},\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{r}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{L}))

is trivial as
\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{L})\simeq 0
by definition of
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-acyclic so the
\mathbb{R}
-module
\underline{\mathrm{map}}^{r}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{L})
is trivial too. This holds for all
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-local
\mathbb{L}
, so
\mathbb{M}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{A}
is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-acyclic. The same argument goes through for
\mathbb{A}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{M}
using the left internal mapping object.

For (
iii
) we verify that the map
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{M}\to L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{M}
is an
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-localisation. Its fibre is
C_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{M}
, which is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-acyclic by (
ii
) as
C_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})
is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-acyclic by definition. It remains to show that
L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{M}
is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-local. To see this, express
\mathbb{M}
as a filtered colimit
\operatorname*{colim}_{\alpha}\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
with
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
finite
\mathbb{R}
-modules, and let
\mathbb{T}\in\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
. Then as
\mathbb{T}
is compact we have

\displaystyle\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T},L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{M})

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname*{colim}_{\alpha}\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T},L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{M}_{\alpha})

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname*{colim}_{\alpha}\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}D^{r}(\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}),L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R}))

which is trivial as
\mathbb{T}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}D^{r}(\mathbb{M}_{\alpha})
is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-acyclic by (
ii
), and
L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})
is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-local. The analogous argument goes through for
\mathbb{M}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})
, using left duals.
∎

It is sometimes convenient to have a more
\infty
-categorical perspective on the localisations
L^{f}_{\lambda}(-)
. The full subcategory
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}^{f}\text{-}\mathsf{loc}\subseteq\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
of
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-local objects is closed under homotopy limits. Furthermore, as
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
consists of finite
\mathbb{R}
-modules, which are in particular compact objects of
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
, the subcategory
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}\text{-}\mathsf{loc}
is closed under filtered homotopy colimits. Using that
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
is presentable, it follows using the adjoint functor theorem
[
37
, Corollary 5.5.2.9]
(or alternatively from
[
37
, Proposition 5.5.4.15]
) that the inclusion of this full subcategory has a left adjoint

L_{\lambda}^{f}:\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}\text{-}\mathsf{loc}.

We momentarily overload our earlier notation by writing
\eta_{\mathbb{M}}:\mathbb{M}\to L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M})
for the unit of this adjunction, and
C_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M})
for its fibre. If
\mathbb{L}
is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-local then in the fibre sequence

\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M}),\mathbb{L})\overset{-\circ\eta_{\mathbb{M}}}{\longrightarrow}\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{M},\mathbb{L})\longrightarrow\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(C_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M}),\mathbb{L})

the left-hand map is an equivalence by definition of
L_{\lambda}^{f}
as a left adjoint, so
\mathrm{map}_{\mathbb{R}}(C_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M}),\mathbb{L})\simeq 0
. This holds for all
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-local
\mathbb{L}
’s, so
C_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M})
is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-acyclic. Thus
\eta_{\mathbb{M}}:\mathbb{M}\to L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M})
is an
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-localisation in the sense of Definition
3.1
.

Remark 3.4
.

Nothing we have said so far is very particular to
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}^{f}
: we have only used that it is an essentially small class of compact objects.

3.1.
Examples

In this section we describe some calculations of
L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R})
for examples such as those discussed in Section
1.3
. The examples described in
1.3.1
satisfy the hypotheses of the following proposition (usually with
\lambda=\frac{1}{2}
).

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
\mathbb{R}\in\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{2}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})
, and suppose that there is a
\sigma\in\pi_{1,0}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\mathbb{R}/\sigma
has a vanishing line of slope
\lambda
. Then there is an equivalence

\sigma^{-1}\mathbb{R}\simeq L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R})

of
\mathbb{R}
-modules.

Proof.

Here
\sigma^{-1}\mathbb{R}
means the colimit of the telescope

\mathbb{R}\overset{\sigma}{\longrightarrow}S^{-1,0}\otimes\mathbb{R}\overset{\sigma}{\longrightarrow}S^{-2,0}\otimes\mathbb{R}\overset{\sigma}{\longrightarrow}S^{-3,0}\otimes\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\cdots

in
\mathbb{R}
-modules. We wish to recognise the map
\mathbb{R}\to\sigma^{-1}\mathbb{R}
given by the inclusion of the initial term as
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-localisation, so must verify that its fibre is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-acyclic and that
\sigma^{-1}\mathbb{R}
is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-local.

Considering
\mathbb{R}
as the colimit of the constant telescope, we see that the cofibre of
\mathbb{R}\to\sigma^{-1}\mathbb{R}
may be expressed as the colimit of

0\longrightarrow S^{-1,0}\otimes\mathbb{R}/\sigma\longrightarrow S^{-2,0}\otimes\mathbb{R}/\sigma^{2}\longrightarrow S^{-3,0}\otimes\mathbb{R}/\sigma^{3}\longrightarrow\cdots.

The cofibres of these map all have the form
S^{-i,0}\otimes\mathbb{R}/\sigma
so are
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-acyclic, and as
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-acyclic objects are closed under colimits it follows that the cofibre of
\mathbb{R}\to\sigma^{-1}\mathbb{R}
is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-acyclic, and hence so is its fibre.

Let
\mathbb{T}\in\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
and
f:\mathbb{T}\to\sigma^{-1}\mathbb{R}
be a map. Then the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\sigma^{-1}\mathbb{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{-1}\mathbb{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{-1}\mathbb{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{-1}\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{-1}\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\sigma^{-1}\mathbb{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{-1}\mathbb{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{-1}\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}f}

shows that
f
factors through
\sigma^{-1}\mathbb{T}
up to homotopy. As
\sigma
is a class of slope 0, and
\mathbb{T}
has a vanishing line of slope
>0
, we have
\sigma^{-1}\mathbb{T}\simeq 0
and hence
f=0
. Thus
\sigma^{-1}\mathbb{R}
is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-local.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

The proof of this proposition shows a little more. Namely, the cofibre of
\mathbb{R}\to\sigma^{-1}\mathbb{R}
has a filtration with associated graded
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{\infty}S^{-i,0}\otimes\mathbb{R}/\sigma
, so if
\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{R}/\sigma)=0
for
d<\lambda n+\mu
then the spectral sequence for this filtration shows that
\pi_{n,d}(\sigma^{-1}\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})=0
for
d<\lambda(n+1)+\mu
. So the fibre of the map
\mathbb{R}\to L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})
has a vanishing line of slope
\lambda
, and hence this map induces an isomorphism on homotopy groups below a line of slope
\lambda
: in other words, this map is a
good
approximation in such a range. In Section
4.1
we will give a general form of this argument.

The example
\mathbf{RB}
of configuration spaces of red-or-blue points in the plane described in Section
1.3.2
satisfies the hypotheses of the following proposition, with
\lambda=\tfrac{1}{2}
. We will spell this out in Example
3.9
below.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
\mathbb{R}\in\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{2}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})
, and suppose that there are elements
r,b\in\pi_{1,0}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\mathbb{R}/(r,b)
has a vanishing line of slope
\lambda
. Then there is a homotopy cartesian square

(3.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{-1}\mathbb{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{-1}\mathbb{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{-1}b^{-1}\mathbb{R}}

of
\mathbb{R}
-modules.

Proof.

Write
\mathbb{P}
for the homotopy pullback of the punctured square, so that the localisation maps
\mathbb{R}\to r^{-1}\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{R}\to b^{-1}\mathbb{R}
assemble to a map
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{P}
. We show that this map is an
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-localisation, by checking that the fibre of
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{P}
is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-acyclic and that
\mathbb{P}
is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-local.

The fibre of
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{P}
is the total homotopy fibre of the square obtained by replacing the top left corner of (
3.3
) by
\mathbb{R}
. This square is obtained by tensoring together the arrows
\mathbb{R}\to r^{-1}\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{R}\to b^{-1}\mathbb{R}
, so its total homotopy fibre is obtained by tensoring together the fibres
\mathbb{F}_{r}
and
\mathbb{F}_{b}
of these two maps. As in the proof of the previous proposition,
\mathbb{F}_{r}
is a telescope with all cofibres given by shifts of
\mathbb{R}/r
, and similarly
\mathbb{F}_{b}
is a telescope with all cofibres given by shifts of
\mathbb{R}/b
. From this we can express
\mathbb{F}_{r}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{F}_{b}
as a telescope with all cofibres given by sums of shifts of
\mathbb{R}/r\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{R}/b\simeq\mathbb{R}/(r,b)
, which lies in
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
. Thus
\mathbb{F}_{r}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{F}_{b}
is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-acyclic.

To verify that the pullback
\mathbb{P}
is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-local, for
\mathbb{T}\in\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
we obtain a cartesian square of mapping objects

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T},r^{-1}\mathbb{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T},b^{-1}\mathbb{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T},r^{-1}b^{-1}\mathbb{R}).}

As in the proof of the previous proposition we have

\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T},r^{-1}\mathbb{R})\simeq\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(r^{-1}\mathbb{T},r^{-1}\mathbb{R})\simeq 0,

because
r^{-1}\mathbb{T}\simeq 0
(as
r
has slope 0 and
\mathbb{T}
has a vanishing line of slope
>0
). Similarly
\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T},b^{-1}\mathbb{R})
and
\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T},r^{-1}b^{-1}\mathbb{R})
vanish, so as the square is cartesian it follows that
\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{P})\simeq 0
. This holds for all
\mathbb{T}\in\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
, so
\mathbb{P}
is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-local. (More succinctly, each of
r^{-1}\mathbb{R}
,
b^{-1}\mathbb{R}
, and
r^{-1}b^{-1}\mathbb{R}
is
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-local as inverting
r
or
b
kills all objects in
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
, and the
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-local modules are closed under limits.)
∎

Remark 3.8
.

(i)

Proposition
3.7
admits an evident generalisation: to a sequence
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{r}\in\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{R})
of elements such that
\mathbb{R}/(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{r})
has a vanishing line of slope
\lambda
, and such that each
\sigma_{i}
has slope
<\lambda
. Then
L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})
is described via the
r
-cube obtained by tensoring together the maps
\mathbb{R}\to\sigma_{i}^{-1}\mathbb{R}
.

(ii)

In the setting of (i) our discussion has a lot to do with local and Čech cohomology in their homotopical incarnations introduced by Greenlees and May
[
18
]
, namely homotopical
I
-power torsion and homotopical localisation away from
I
. Concretely, in the setting of (i) and with
I:=(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{r})\subset\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{R})
, the description in (i) shows that the fibre sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{I}(\mathbb{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}[I^{-1}]}

are identical.

The object
\mathbb{R}[I^{-1}]
may also be described as Bousfield localisation with respect to
\mathbb{R}/(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{r})
(cf.
[
12
, Proposition 6.14]
), which lies in
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
and so gives a factorisation
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}[I^{-1}]\to L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R})
. The fact that the latter map is an equivalence reflects the fact that
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}^{f}
is generated as a thick subcategory by
\mathbb{R}/(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{r})
, which it is not too hard to prove manually. We will discuss a generalised form of this in Section
4.3
.

(iii)

Typical instances of higher-order homological stability are more complicated than the setting of (i), in that the higher-order stabilisation maps are usually only defined after taking cofibres for the lower-order ones: in particular one cannot “invert them in
\mathbb{R}
” as they are not defined in
\mathbb{R}
, so one cannot even formulate something like Proposition
3.7
. In Section
4.4
we will discuss an analogue of the constructions of this section in this more general setting.

Example 3.9
.

The notation in Proposition
3.7
is inspired by the example
\mathbf{RB}=\mathbb{E}_{2}(S^{1,0}r\oplus S^{1,0}b)
discussed in Section
1.3.2
, which may be considered as the
\mathbbm{k}
-chains on the spaces of configurations of distinct unordered red-or-blue points in
[0,1]^{2}
. We mentioned the fact that
\mathbf{RB}/(r,b)
has a vanishing line of slope
\tfrac{1}{2}
. In fact, the work of F. Cohen shows that as a
\mathbbm{k}
-algebra we have

\pi_{*,*}(\mathbf{RB})\cong\mathbbm{k}[r,b]\otimes\mathrm{Sym}^{*}[\text{generators of slope $\geq\tfrac{1}{2}$}],

where
\mathrm{Sym}^{*}[-]
denotes the free graded-commutative algebra. This shows that there is an isomorphism of bigraded
\mathbbm{k}
-modules

\pi_{*,*}(\mathbf{RB}/(r,b))\cong\pi_{*,*}(\mathbf{RB})/(r,b)\cong\mathrm{Sym}^{*}[\text{generators of slope $\geq\tfrac{1}{2}$}],

from which one immediately sees the vanishing line of slope
\tfrac{1}{2}
. Using this, Proposition
3.7
gives a cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{f}_{1/2}(\mathbf{RB})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{-1}\mathbf{RB}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{-1}\mathbf{RB}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{-1}b^{-1}\mathbf{RB},}

mentioned in Example
1.4
. We have
\pi_{*,*}(\mathbf{RB})\cong\mathbbm{k}[r,b]\otimes\pi_{*,*}(\mathbf{RB}/(r,b))
as a
\mathbbm{k}[r,b]
-module. The homotopy pullback
P
of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{k}[r^{\pm 1},b]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{k}[r,b^{\pm 1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{k}[r^{\pm 1},b^{\pm 1}]}

in the derived category of
\mathbbm{k}[r,b]
-modules is no longer a discrete
\mathbbm{k}[r,b]
-module, but rather has

\displaystyle\pi_{*,0}(P)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbbm{k}[r,b]

\displaystyle\pi_{*,-1}(P)

\displaystyle\cong r^{-1}b^{-1}\mathbbm{k}[r^{-1},b^{-1}],

where the latter has the evident
\mathbbm{k}[r,b]
-module structure. This leads to the formula

\pi_{*,*}(L^{f}_{1/2}(\mathbf{RB}))\cong\pi_{*,*}(\mathbf{RB})\oplus r^{-1}b^{-1}\mathbbm{k}[r^{-1},b^{-1}]\otimes\pi_{*,*+1}(\mathbf{RB}/(r,b))

as a bigraded
\mathbbm{k}
-module. The second summand, which is
\pi_{*,*}(S^{0,1}\otimes C^{f}_{1/2}(\mathbf{RB}))
, is seen to vanish in bidegrees
(n,d)
with
d<-1
or
d<\tfrac{1}{2}n
.

4.
Applications of Smith–Toda complexes

By a
Smith–Toda complex
we mean an
\mathbb{R}
-module
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
obtained as the iterated cofibre of a sequence of endomorphisms

\alpha_{j}:S^{n_{j},d_{j}}\otimes\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j-1})\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j-1}).

We will call
\tfrac{d_{j}}{n_{j}}
the
slope
of
\alpha_{j}
. We will usually adopt some mild axioms and standardised notation for Smith–Toda complexes, as follows.

Definition 4.1
.

A Smith–Toda complex
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
is
admissible
if

(i)

The slopes
\tfrac{d_{1}}{n_{1}},\tfrac{d_{2}}{n_{2}},\ldots
are non-decreasing, and

(ii)

it has a vanishing line of slope
\lambda
with
\tfrac{d_{r}}{n_{r}}<\lambda
.

We then write
\lambda^{\prime}:=\tfrac{d_{r}}{n_{r}}
, and let
\alpha_{k},\ldots,\alpha_{r}
denote the
\alpha
’s of slope precisely
\lambda^{\prime}
.

In Section
5
we will prove that admissible Smith–Toda complexes exist for any
\lambda<1
, as long as
\mathbbm{k}
is a field of positive characteristic. In this section we explain what one can do with admissible Smith–Toda complexes, whether they are produced by the argument of Section
5
or otherwise.

Proposition 4.2
.

If
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
is an admissible Smith–Toda complex then
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1})
is an admissible Smith–Toda complex with a vanishing line of slope
\lambda^{\prime}
.

This follows by downwards induction using the following general lemma.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a finite
\mathbb{R}
-module, and
\phi:S^{N,D}\otimes\mathbb{M}\to\mathbb{M}
be an endomorphism of non-zero bidegree with
\lambda:=\tfrac{D}{N}
. If
\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{M}/\phi)=0
for
d<\lambda n+\kappa
, then
\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{M})=0
for
d<\lambda n+\kappa
too.

Proof.

Consider the tower

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots S^{3N,3D}\otimes\mathbb{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{2N,2D}\otimes\mathbb{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{N,D}\otimes\mathbb{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{2N,2D}\otimes\mathbb{M}/\phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{N,D}\otimes\mathbb{M}/\phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{M}/\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

If
x\in\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{M})
with
d<\lambda n+\kappa
then it vanishes when mapped to
\mathbb{M}/\phi
so
x=\phi(x^{\prime})
for some
x^{\prime}\in\pi_{n-N,d-D}(\mathbb{M})
, and its bidegrees also satisfy
d-D<\lambda(n-N)+\kappa
as
D=\lambda N
. Thus
x^{\prime}=\phi(x^{\prime\prime})
for
x^{\prime\prime}\in\pi_{n-2N,d-2D}(\mathbb{M})
, and so on. As
\mathbb{M}
is a finite
\mathbb{R}
-module and
(N,D)\neq(0,0)
,
\pi_{n-kN,d-kD}(\mathbb{M})=0
for
k\gg 0
, so
x=0
.
∎

4.1.
Quality of the approximation

One application of the existence of an admissible Smith–Toda complex with a slope
\lambda
vanishing line is that it guarantees the high-connectivity of the map
\mathbb{R}\to L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R})
, as follows.

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
\mathbb{R}\in\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{2}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})
. Let
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
be an admissible Smith–Toda complex such that
\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r}))=0
for
d<\lambda n+\kappa
. Then the fibre
C_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})
of

\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})

has
\pi_{n,d}(C_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R}))=0
for
d<\lambda n+\kappa+\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\lambda n_{i}-d_{i}-1)
, and also for
d<\lambda^{\prime}n+\tfrac{\lambda^{\prime}}{\lambda}\kappa+\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\lambda^{\prime}n_{i}-d_{i}-1)
.

n
d
slope
\lambda
slope
\lambda^{\prime}

Note that the
\mathbb{R}
-module
C_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})
need not be finite, so having a vanishing line of slope
\lambda^{\prime}
does not follow from having one of slope
\lambda
: as depicted in the adjacent figure, it is more information.

Proof of Theorem
4.4
.

For any
\mathbb{R}
-module
\mathbb{M}
write
\mathbb{M}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j-1}):=\mathbb{M}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j-1})
, so there are induced maps

\alpha_{j}=\mathbb{M}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\alpha_{j}:S^{n_{j},d_{j}}\otimes\mathbb{M}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j-1})\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j-1}).

If
\mathbb{T}\in\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
then
\mathbb{T}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j-1})\in\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
as it is in the thick subcategory generated by
\mathbb{T}
. As
\tfrac{d_{j}}{n_{j}}<\lambda
it follows that

\pi_{*,*}(\alpha_{j}^{-1}\mathbb{T}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j-1}))=\alpha_{j}^{-1}\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{T}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j-1}))=0,

so
\alpha_{j}^{-1}\mathbb{T}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j-1})=0
.

Abbreviate
\mathbb{C}:=C_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})
. By its construction in Proposition
3.2
,
\mathbb{C}
is a telescope of maps whose cofibres are sums of elements of
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
. The construction
\alpha_{j}^{-1}(-)/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j-1})=(-)\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\alpha^{-1}_{j}\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j-1})
commutes with colimits, so from the above discussion it follows that
\alpha_{j}^{-1}\mathbb{C}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j-1})\simeq 0
.

From the directed colimit

\mathbb{C}\overset{\alpha_{1}}{\longrightarrow}S^{-n_{1},-d_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{C}\overset{\alpha_{1}}{\longrightarrow}S^{-2n_{1},-2d_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\cdots\longrightarrow\alpha_{1}^{-1}\mathbb{C}\simeq 0

we see that
(\alpha_{1}^{-1}\mathbb{C})/\mathbb{C}\simeq S^{0,1}\otimes\mathbb{C}
has a filtration whose associated graded is

\bigoplus_{i_{1}=1}^{\infty}S^{-i_{1}n_{1},-i_{1}d_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{C}/\alpha_{1}.

Similarly, from the directed colimit

\mathbb{C}/\alpha_{1}\overset{\alpha_{2}}{\longrightarrow}S^{-n_{2},-d_{2}}\otimes\mathbb{C}/\alpha_{1}\overset{\alpha_{2}}{\longrightarrow}S^{-2n_{2},-2d_{2}}\otimes\mathbb{C}/\alpha_{1}\longrightarrow\cdots\longrightarrow\alpha_{2}^{-1}\mathbb{C}/\alpha_{1}\simeq 0

we see that
(\alpha_{2}^{-1}\mathbb{C}/\alpha_{1})/(\mathbb{C}/\alpha_{1})\simeq S^{0,1}\otimes\mathbb{C}/\alpha_{1}
has a filtration whose associated graded is

\bigoplus_{i_{2}=1}^{\infty}S^{-i_{2}n_{2},-i_{2}d_{2}}\otimes\mathbb{C}/(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}).

Continuing in this way, we see that
S^{0,1}\otimes\mathbb{C}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j-1})
has a filtration whose associated graded is

\bigoplus_{i_{j}=1}^{\infty}S^{-i_{j}n_{j},-i_{j}d_{j}}\otimes\mathbb{C}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j})

for each
j\leq r
.

As
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
is a finite
\mathbb{R}
-module with a vanishing line of slope
\lambda
we have that
L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r}))=0
and so
\mathbb{C}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})\simeq\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
, which we have assumed has trivial homotopy groups in bidegrees
(n,d)
satisfying
d<\lambda n+\kappa
. The spectral sequence for the final filtration obtained takes the form

E^{1}_{n,p,q}=\pi_{n,p+q}(S^{-pn_{r},-pd_{r}}\otimes\mathbb{C}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r}))\Rightarrow\pi_{n,p+q}(S^{0,1}\otimes\mathbb{C}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r-1})),

defined for
p\geq 1
. This shows that

\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{C}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r-1}))=0\text{ for }d<\lambda n+\kappa+(\lambda n_{r}-d_{r}-1).

Running the series of analogous spectral sequences for the earlier filtrations gives

\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{C})=0\text{ for }d<\lambda n+\kappa+\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\lambda n_{i}-d_{i}-1).

As
\mathbb{C}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})\simeq\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
is a
finite

\mathbb{R}
-module with a slope
\lambda
vanishing line, it also has a slope
\lambda^{\prime}
vanishing line. Specifically, using that its homotopy groups vanish in negative homological degree we also have
\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r}))=0
for
d<\lambda^{\prime}n+\tfrac{\lambda^{\prime}}{\lambda}\kappa
. Running the same series of spectral sequences as above using this vanishing line shows first that

\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{C}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r-1}))=0\text{ for }d<\lambda^{\prime}n+\tfrac{\lambda^{\prime}}{\lambda}\kappa+(\lambda^{\prime}n_{r}-d_{r}-1)

and then by the same series of spectral sequences that

\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{C})=0\text{ for }d<\lambda^{\prime}n+\tfrac{\lambda^{\prime}}{\lambda}\kappa+\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\lambda^{\prime}n_{i}-d_{i}-1).\qed

4.2.
Adams periodicity

In Theorem
4.4
we have seen that the existence of an admissible Smith–Toda complex
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
with a slope
\lambda
vanishing line means the fibre of
\mathbb{R}\to L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R})
has a certain vanishing line. If
\lambda^{\prime}:=\tfrac{d_{r}}{n_{r}}
and
\alpha_{k},\ldots,\alpha_{r}
are the
\alpha
’s of slope precisely
\lambda^{\prime}
, then
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1})
has a slope
\lambda^{\prime}
vanishing line by Proposition
4.2
and so the fibre of
\mathbb{R}\to L^{f}_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R})
also has a certain vanishing line. Writing

M^{f}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}):=\mathrm{fib}(L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R})\to L^{f}_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R})),

we will show below that this has a slope
\lambda^{\prime}
vanishing line, but moreover that the
\alpha_{k},\ldots,\alpha_{r}
induce certain periodicities on the homotopy groups of
M^{f}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R})
, in a range of degrees.

To formulate the “in a range of degrees” part of this result, for an object
X\in\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
we write
\tau^{\lambda^{\prime}}_{<b}(X)\in\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
for the object given in terms of Postnikov truncation by

\tau^{\lambda^{\prime}}_{<b}(X)(n):=\tau_{<\lfloor b+\lambda^{\prime}n\rfloor}(X(n)).

(This is not truncation with respect to a
t
-structure unless
\lambda^{\prime}=1
, in which case it is truncation with respect to the diagonal
t
-structure introduced in Section
2.8
.)

Theorem 4.5
.

Let
\mathbb{R}\in\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{2}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})
. Let
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
be an admissible Smith–Toda complex with a slope
\lambda
vanishing line, and write
\lambda^{\prime}:=\tfrac{d_{r}}{n_{r}}
and let
\alpha_{k},\ldots,\alpha_{r}
be the
\alpha
’s of slope precisely
\lambda^{\prime}
. Then

(i)

M^{f}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R})
has a slope
\lambda^{\prime}
vanishing line, and

(ii)

if
\pi_{n,d}(M^{f}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}))=0
for
d<\lambda^{\prime}n+\kappa^{\prime}
, then setting

b:=\kappa^{\prime}+\min_{S\subsetneq\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\}}\left[\sum_{s\not\in S}(\lambda^{\prime}n_{s}-d_{s}-1)\right]

there are maps

\beta_{i}:S^{n_{i},d_{i}}\otimes\tau_{<b}^{\lambda^{\prime}}M_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}^{f}(\mathbb{R})/(\beta_{k},\ldots,\beta_{i-1})\longrightarrow\tau_{\leq b}^{\lambda^{\prime}}M_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}^{f}(\mathbb{R})/(\beta_{k},\ldots,\beta_{i-1})

for
k\leq i\leq r
with
\tau_{<b}^{\lambda^{\prime}}M_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}^{f}(\mathbb{R})/(\beta_{k},\ldots,\beta_{r})\simeq 0
.

The meaning of this theorem is clearest when there is a single
\alpha
of slope
\lambda^{\prime}
, namely
\alpha_{r}
. It then says that there is a periodicity isomorphism

\beta_{r}:\pi_{n-n_{r},d-d_{r}}(M_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}^{f}(\mathbb{R}))\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\pi_{n,d}(M_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}^{f}(\mathbb{R}))

defined for
d<\lambda^{\prime}n+b
. This is only non-vacuous in the range where these homotopy groups are not known to vanish, i.e. for
b>\kappa^{\prime}
, i.e. for

\min_{S\subsetneq\{1,2,\ldots,r-1\}}\left[\sum_{s\not\in S}((\lambda^{\prime}n_{s}-d_{s})-1)\right]>0.

As
\tfrac{d_{s}}{n_{s}}<\lambda^{\prime}
for
s\in\{1,2,\ldots,r-1\}
, by the assumption that only
\alpha_{r}
has slope
\lambda^{\prime}
, we have
\lambda^{\prime}n_{s}-d_{s}>0
for each
s
, but a little more is required for the conclusion to be non-vacuous. Namely, we wish to find Smith–Toda complexes where these values are arbitrarily large: that will yield periodicity isomorphisms in arbitrarily wide bands of degrees parallel to the vanishing line (although the wider one wishes this band of homotopy groups to be, the longer one must typically take the period of the periodicity to be). When we establish the existence of Smith–Toda complexes in Section
5
we will explain how this property may also be achieved.

Remark 4.6
.

We call this “Adams periodicity” by analogy with his periodicity theorem
[
2
, Theorem 1.2]
for
\mathrm{Ext}
over the Steenrod algebra. Indeed, as we will discuss in the companion paper
[
48
]
, Adams’ theorem may be obtained as an instance of this one.

When there are several
\alpha
’s of slope
\lambda^{\prime}
it seems more difficult to give a concrete interpretation, unless one knows that the stabilisation maps
\alpha_{k},\ldots,\alpha_{r}
of slope
\lambda^{\prime}
can be defined simultanously as endomorphisms
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1})
, and arranged to form a coherently commutative cube. In Section
9.3
we will show that this is possible, at least when
\mathbb{R}
is an
E_{3}
-algebra.

Addendum 4.7
.

If
\alpha_{k},\ldots,\alpha_{r}
can all be defined on
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1})
so as to yield a coherently homotopy commutative
(r-k)
-cube, then
\beta_{k},\ldots,\beta_{r}
can all be defined on
\tau_{\leq b}^{\lambda^{\prime}}M_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}^{f}(\mathbb{R})
so as to yield a coherently commutative
(r-k)
-cube. In this case the conclusion of the Theorem is that this cube is homotopy (co)cartesian.

Proof of Theorem
4.5
and Addendum
4.7
.

For (
i
), it follows from Theorem
4.4
that
C_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})
has a vanishing line of slope
\lambda
as well as a vanishing line of slope
\lambda^{\prime}
, and
C_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{f}(\mathbb{R})
has a vanishing line of slope
\lambda^{\prime}
, so it follows that
M^{f}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R})\simeq\Sigma\mathrm{fib}(C_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})\to C_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{f}(\mathbb{R}))
has a vanishing line of slope
\lambda^{\prime}
.

For (
ii
), note that the object
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j-1})
has a (skeletal) filtration with associated graded

\bigoplus_{S\subseteq\{1,2,\ldots,j-1\}}S^{\sum_{s\in S}n_{s},\sum_{s\in S}(d_{s}+1)}\otimes\mathbb{R},

so the fibre of the projection
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j-1})\to S^{\sum_{s=1}^{j-1}n_{s},\sum_{s=1}^{j-1}(d_{s}+1)}\otimes\mathbb{R}
to the top cell has a filtration with associated graded given by the analogous sum but over proper subsets
S\subsetneq\{1,2,\ldots,j-1\}
. It follows that

\mathrm{fib}(S^{-\sum_{s=1}^{k-1}n_{s},-\sum_{s=1}^{k-1}(d_{s}+1)}\otimes M_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}^{f}(\mathbb{R})/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1})\to M_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}^{f}(\mathbb{R}))

has a filtration with associated graded

\bigoplus_{S\subsetneq\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\}}S^{-\sum_{s\not\in S}n_{s},-\sum_{s\not\in S}(d_{s}+1)}\otimes M^{f}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R})

and therefore has trivial
(n,d)
-th homotopy group as long as

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle<\min_{S\subsetneq\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\}}\left[\lambda^{\prime}(n+\sum_{s\not\in S}n_{s})-\sum_{s\not\in S}(d_{s}+1)+\kappa^{\prime}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad=\lambda^{\prime}n+\kappa^{\prime}+\min_{S\subsetneq\{1,2,\ldots,k-1\}}\left[\sum_{s\not\in S}((\lambda^{\prime}n_{s}-d_{s})-1)\right].

In particular the homotopy groups of this fibre vanish for
d<\lambda^{\prime}n+b
, so there is an equivalence

\tau_{<b}^{\lambda^{\prime}}M_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}^{f}(S^{-\sum_{s=1}^{k-1}n_{s},-\sum_{s=1}^{k-1}(d_{s}+1)}\otimes\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1}))\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\tau_{<b}^{\lambda^{\prime}}M_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}^{f}(\mathbb{R}).

Given this equivalence, the maps
\beta_{k},\ldots,\beta_{r}
on
\tau_{<b}^{\lambda^{\prime}}M_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}^{f}(\mathbb{R})
are just those induced by applying
\tau_{<b}^{\lambda^{\prime}}M_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}^{f}(-)
to the sequence of maps
\alpha_{k},\ldots,\alpha_{r}
starting with
S^{-\sum_{s=1}^{k-1}n_{s},-\sum_{s=1}^{k-1}(d_{s}+1)}\otimes\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1})
. The conclusion follows as

\tau_{<b}^{\lambda^{\prime}}M_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}^{f}(\mathbb{R})/(\beta_{k},\ldots,\beta_{r})\simeq\tau_{<b}^{\lambda^{\prime}}M_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}^{f}(S^{-\sum_{s=1}^{k-1}n_{s},-\sum_{s=1}^{k-1}(d_{s}+1)}\otimes\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r}))

and
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
has a vanishing line of slope
\lambda
so is annihilated by
M_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}^{f}(-)
.

For the Addendum, apply
\tau_{<b}^{\lambda^{\prime}}M_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}^{f}(-)
to the coherently commutative
(r-k)
-cube given by the endomorphisms
\alpha_{k},\ldots,\alpha_{r}
of
S^{-\sum_{s=1}^{k-1}n_{s},-\sum_{s=1}^{k-1}(d_{s}+1)}\otimes\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1})
, to obtain a coherently commutative
(r-k)
-cube of endomorphisms
\beta_{k},\ldots,\beta_{r}
of
\tau_{<b}^{\lambda^{\prime}}M_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}^{f}(\mathbb{R})
. As above the total homotopy cofibre of this
(r-k)
-cube is identified with
\tau_{<b}^{\lambda^{\prime}}M_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}^{f}(S^{-\sum_{s=1}^{k-1}n_{s},-\sum_{s=1}^{k-1}(d_{s}+1)}\otimes\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r}))
and vanishes.
∎

4.3.
Monogenicity and Quantisation

A further application of the existence of an admissible Smith–Toda complex with a slope
\lambda
vanishing line is that it implies that
L^{f}_{\lambda}(-)
is given by localisation away from a single object (namely the Smith–Toda complex), and also that there are only finitely-many different localisation functors at slopes
\leq\lambda
, as follows.

Theorem 4.8
.

If
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
is an admissible Smith–Toda complex with a slope
\lambda
vanishing line and
\bar{\lambda}
satisfies
\tfrac{d_{r}}{n_{r}}<\bar{\lambda}\leq\lambda
, then any finite
\mathbb{R}
-module with a slope
\bar{\lambda}
vanishing line lies in the thick subcategory generated by
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
. In particular:

(i)

Finite
\mathbb{R}
-modules with a slope
\bar{\lambda}
vanishing line have a slope
\lambda
vanishing line.

(ii)

\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
generates the same thick subcategory as
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}^{f}
, so
L_{\lambda}^{f}(-)
agrees with Bousfield localisation away from
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{T}\in\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\bar{\lambda}}
, and consider the finite
\mathbb{R}
-module

\mathbb{T}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r}):=\mathbb{T}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r}).

As
\mathbb{T}
has a slope
\bar{\lambda}
vanishing line it follows that for each
i-1\leq r
the
\mathbb{R}
-module
\mathbb{T}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{i-1})
has a slope
\bar{\lambda}
vanishing line, as it is in the thick subcategory generated by
\mathbb{T}
. Thus

\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{i-1}))\simeq\mathbb{T}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{i-1})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}D^{r}(\mathbb{T}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{i-1}))\in\ \mathcal{A}^{f}_{\bar{\lambda}}

too, as
D^{r}(\mathbb{T}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{i-1}))
is a finite
\mathbb{R}
-module. As
\tfrac{d_{i}}{n_{i}}<\lambda
it follows that the

\alpha_{i}\in\pi_{n_{i},d_{i}}(\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{i-1})))

are nilpotent for all
i\leq r
. Applying Lemma
2.5
(
iii
) it follows for each
i\leq r
that
\mathbb{T}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{i-1})
is in the thick subcategory generated by
\mathbb{T}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{i})
, so in particular
\mathbb{T}
lies in the thick subcategory generated by
\mathbb{T}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{i})
.

On the other hand as
\mathbb{T}
is finite, i.e. in the thick subcategory of the monoidal unit,
\mathbb{T}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{i})=\mathbb{T}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
lies in the thick subcategory generated by
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{i})
.
∎

Corollary 4.9
.

If
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
is an admissible Smith–Toda complex with a slope
\lambda
vanishing line and
\bar{\lambda}
satisfies
\tfrac{d_{r}}{n_{r}}<\bar{\lambda}\leq\lambda
, then
L^{f}_{\lambda}(-)=L^{f}_{\bar{\lambda}}(-)
.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.8
the inclusion
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\bar{\lambda}}
is in fact an equality.
∎

4.4.
An explicit model for localisation

A consequence of Theorem
4.8
is a somewhat explicit formula for
L^{f}_{\lambda}
. To give the formula, let
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
be an admissible Smith–Toda complex with a slope
\lambda
vanishing line. By admissibility the bidegrees
|\alpha_{j}|=(n_{j},d_{j})
satisfy
\tfrac{d_{j}}{n_{j}}<\lambda
, but we will request the stronger property

(4.1)

\min_{\emptyset\neq S\subseteq\{1,2,\ldots,r\}}\left[1+\sum_{s\in S}(\lambda n_{j}-d_{j}-1)\right]>0.

When we show, in Section
5
, that admissible Smith–Toda complexes exist over fields of positive characteristic, we will also be able to arrange this property.

By Theorem
4.8
(
ii
) the localisation
L^{f}_{\lambda}(-)
agrees with Bousfield localisation away from the single compact object
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
, and we will show that this localisation can be constructed by the following recipe. Define an
\mathbb{R}
-module
\mathbb{F}
by the fibre sequence

\mathbb{F}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})

where the right-hand map is the inclusion of the bottom cell. Mapping out of this cofibre sequence to some
\mathbb{R}
-module
\mathbb{M}
gives a fibre sequence

\underline{\mathrm{map}}^{r}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r}),\mathbb{M})\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}\overset{\epsilon_{\mathbb{M}}}{\longrightarrow}\underline{\mathrm{map}}^{r}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{F},\mathbb{M})=:\tau(\mathbb{M}).

Tautologically, the
\mathbb{R}
-module
\mathbb{M}
is
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
-local if and only if the map
\epsilon_{\mathbb{M}}:\mathbb{M}\to\tau(\mathbb{M})
is an equivalence. We define

T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M}):=\operatorname*{colim}(\mathbb{M}\overset{\epsilon_{\mathbb{M}}}{\to}\tau(\mathbb{M})\overset{\epsilon_{\tau(\mathbb{M})}}{\to}\tau^{2}(\mathbb{M})\overset{\epsilon_{\tau^{2}(\mathbb{M})}}{\to}\cdots).

Proposition 4.10
.

The map
\mathbb{M}\to T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M})
is equivalent to Bousfield localisation away from
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
, so is equivalent to the map
\mathbb{M}\to L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M})
.

Remark 4.11
.

The construction of the functor
T_{\lambda}^{f}(-)
is deliberately reminiscent of Weiss’ definition of the Taylor approximation functor
T_{n}(-)
in orthogonal calculus
[
52
,
53
]
. Indeed, orthogonal calculus (at least when valued in a stable category) may be set up in a manner which is highly parallel to the discussion we are giving here: we hope to explain this elsewhere.

Before giving the proof of Proposition
4.10
, we establish a basic lemma.

Lemma 4.12
.

If
\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{M})=0
for
d<\lambda n+\kappa
then
T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M})=0
.

Proof.

The defining cofibre sequence
\mathbb{F}\to\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
and the standard
\mathbb{R}
-module cell structure on
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
shows that
\mathbb{F}
has a finite filtration with associated graded given by

\bigoplus_{\emptyset\neq S\subseteq\{1,2,\ldots,r\}}S^{\sum_{j\in S}n_{j},-1+\sum_{j\in S}(d_{j}+1)}\otimes\mathbb{R}.

Thus
\tau(\mathbb{M})=\underline{\mathrm{map}}^{r}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{F},\mathbb{M})
has a finite filtration with associated graded

\bigoplus_{\emptyset\neq S\subseteq\{1,2,\ldots,r\}}S^{-\sum_{j\in S}n_{j},1-\sum_{j\in S}(d_{j}+1)}\otimes\mathbb{M},

so
\pi_{n,d}(\tau(\mathbb{M}))=0
as long as

d<\lambda n+\kappa+\min_{\emptyset\neq S\subseteq\{1,2,\ldots,r\}}\left[1+\sum_{s\in S}(\lambda n_{j}-d_{j}-1)\right].

Writing
\epsilon\in\mathbb{R}
for this minimum, the assumption (
4.1
) says that
\epsilon>0
. In other words the above shows that
\pi_{n,d}(\tau(\mathbb{M}))=0
for
d<\lambda n+(\kappa+\epsilon)
with
\epsilon>0
. Iterating this, it follows that
\pi_{n,d}(\tau^{s}(\mathbb{M}))=0
for
d<\lambda n+(\kappa+s\epsilon)
, and as
\epsilon>0
taking the colimit gives that
\pi_{n,d}(T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M}))=0
for all
n
and
d
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.10
.

The fibre of
\epsilon_{\tau^{s}(\mathbb{M})}:\tau^{s}(\mathbb{M})\to\tau^{s+1}(\mathbb{M})
is

(4.2)

\underline{\mathrm{map}}^{r}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r}),\tau^{s}(\mathbb{M}))\simeq\tau^{s}(\mathbb{M})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}D^{r}(\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})).

By Lemma
2.8
the right dual
D^{r}(\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r}))
is in the thick subcategory generated by
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
so is
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
-acyclic, and hence by the proof of Proposition
3.3
(
ii
) the object (
4.2
) is
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
-acyclic too. Thus the fibre of
\mathbb{M}\to T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M})
is also
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
-acyclic.

It remains to show that
T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M})
is
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
-local. For this we follow Weiss
[
53
]
extremely closely. We must show that the map

\epsilon_{T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M})}:T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M})\longrightarrow\tau(T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M}))=\underline{\mathrm{map}}^{r}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{F},T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M}))

is an equivalence. As
\mathbb{F}
is a finite
\mathbb{R}
-module the functor
\tau(-)
preserves filtered colimits, so
\epsilon_{T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M})}
is the map induced on horizontal colimits in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau(\mathbb{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{2}(\mathbb{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau(\mathbb{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{2}(\mathbb{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{3}(\mathbb{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau(T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{\mathbb{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{\mathbb{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{\tau(\mathbb{M})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{\tau(\mathbb{M})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{\tau^{2}(\mathbb{M})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{\tau^{2}(\mathbb{M})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau(\epsilon_{\mathbb{M}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau(\epsilon_{\tau(\mathbb{M})})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau(\epsilon_{\tau^{2}(\mathbb{M})})}

We factorise the map from the
s
-th column to the last as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{map}}^{r}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{R},\tau^{s}(\mathbb{M}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{map}}^{r}_{\mathbb{R}}(T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R}),T_{\lambda}^{f}(\tau^{s}(\mathbb{M})))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{map}}^{r}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{R},T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{map}}^{r}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{F},\tau^{s}(\mathbb{M}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{map}}^{r}_{\mathbb{R}}(T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{F}),T_{\lambda}^{f}(\tau^{s}(\mathbb{M})))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{map}}^{r}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{F},T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\lambda}^{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{\tau^{s}(\mathbb{M})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\lambda}^{f}}

where the middle vertical map is given by precomposition with
T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{F})\to T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})
, and the right-hand horizontal maps involve the identity
T_{\lambda}^{f}(\tau^{s}(\mathbb{M}))\simeq T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{M})
coming from cofinality of the colimits defining them.

Now the functor
T_{\lambda}^{f}(-)
is obtained by taking a filtered colimit of maps from finite
\mathbb{R}
-modules, so it preserves cofibre sequences. But
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
has a slope
\lambda
vanishing line, so by Lemma
4.12
we have
T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r}))\simeq 0
and therefore
T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{F})\overset{\sim}{\to}T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})
. Hence the middle vertical map is an equivalence.

The right-hand vertical map is the colimit of the left-hand vertical maps as
s\to\infty
: we have shown that this map is a retract of a colimit of equivalences, so is an equivalence.
∎

Remark 4.13
.

The proof shows that rather than the full strength of Lemma
4.12
we only use
T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r}))\simeq 0
.

4.5.
A “telescope conjecture”

In Section
3
we might instead have chosen to Bousfield localise away from

\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}:=\{\text{ $\mathbb{R}$-modules with a slope $\lambda$ vanishing line}\},

i.e. remove the assumption that the modules be finite. Such a localisation still exists, but it can no longer be obtained by the telescope construction of Proposition
3.2
. Instead, one may use that
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
is presentable, so that by
[
37
, Proposition 5.5.4.15]
the inclusion

\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}\text{-}\mathsf{loc}\subseteq\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
of the full subcategory of
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
-local objects has a left adjoint
L_{\lambda}:\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}\to\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}\text{-}\mathsf{loc}\subseteq\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
. As
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
-local objects are in particular
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-local, there is a comparison map

(4.3)

L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{M})\longrightarrow L_{\lambda}(\mathbb{M}).

Theorem 4.14

(“Telescope conjecture”)
.

If an admissible Smith–Toda complex with a slope
\lambda
vanishing line and satisfying (
4.1
) exists, then (
4.3
) is an equivalence.

Proof.

It suffices to show that all
\mathbb{T}\in\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
are
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-acyclic. If
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
is such a Smith–Toda complex, then Proposition
4.10
shows that
L^{f}_{\lambda}(-)
is given by the construction
T_{\lambda}^{f}(-)
formed using this admissible Smith–Toda complex. But if
\mathbb{T}\in\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
then Lemma
4.12
shows that
T_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{T})=0
, so
L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{T})=0
and so
\mathbb{T}
is indeed
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-acyclic.
∎

4.6.
Monochromatic layers

In Section
4.2
we have already discussed a special case of the
(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})
-monochromatic objects

M^{f}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbb{M}):=\mathrm{fib}(L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{M})\to L^{f}_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbb{M})).

Let
\mathsf{Mono}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}\subset\mathbf{\mathbb{R}}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
denote the full subcategory spanned by such objects: more conceptually, it is the full subcategory on objects which are
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
-local and
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
-acyclic.

We briefly outline one way of looking at this category, omitting the details as we have not yet found a use for this perspective. If
\mathbb{R}/(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{s})
is an admissible Smith–Toda complex with a slope
\lambda^{\prime}
vanishing line, then
L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R}/(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{s}))
is
(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})
-monochromatic, and
\{S^{n,0}\otimes L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R}/(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{s}))\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
forms a set of compact generators for
\mathsf{Mono}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}
. Setting

\mathbb{E}:=\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{R}}(L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R}/(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{s}))),

an
E_{1}
-algebra in
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
and hence in
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
, it follows from an appropriate form of the Schwede–Shipley theorem that there is an equivalence of categories

\displaystyle\mathsf{Mono}_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{E}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}\longmapsto

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{map}_{\mathbb{R}}(L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R}/(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{s})),\mathbb{M})

\displaystyle L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R}/(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{s}))\otimes_{\mathbb{E}}\mathbb{X}\longmapsfrom

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{X}.

5.
Existence of Smith–Toda complexes in positive characteristic

Our goal in this section is to prove that, as long as work over a field of positive characteristic, there exist admissible Smith–Toda complexes having vanishing lines of slopes arbitrarily close to 1. A precise statement is as follows:

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
\mathbbm{k}
be a field of positive characteristic
p
, and let
\mathbb{R}\in\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{2}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})
satisfy (C), (SCE), and (F). For any given
\lambda<1
, there exists an admissible Smith–Toda complex
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
having a vanishing line of slope
\lambda
. Furthermore:

(i)

The slopes
\tfrac{d_{i}}{n_{i}}
of the
\alpha_{i}
are all of the form
\tfrac{k}{k+1}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

(ii)

It in fact has a vanishing line of slope
\min\{\tfrac{k}{k+1}\,|\,k\in\mathbb{N}\text{ with }\lambda\leq\tfrac{k}{k+1}\}
.

(iii)

If
W\in\mathbb{R}
is pre-specified, then the
\alpha_{i}
may be chosen such that
\lambda n_{i}-d_{i}-1\geq W
for
i=1,\ldots,r
.

This proves Theorem
A
(
i
) and (
ii
). The role of Theorem
5.1
(
iii
) is to ensure that the quantity “
b
” may be made arbitrarily large in Theorem
4.5
, and also that the property (
4.1
) may be achieved in Section
4.4
.

In order to prove Theorem
5.1
we must construct endomorphisms
\alpha_{i}
of inductively-defined
\mathbb{R}
-modules
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{i-1})
, and a basic difficulty is saying what these endomorphisms are supposed to achieve. To address this, we will consider the canonical multiplicative filtration
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}
of
\mathbb{R}
, and prove the analogue (Theorem
5.6
) of Theorem
5.1
in the category of
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}
-modules. The advantage of working in this category is that we can use the associated graded to describe what it is that a single endomorphism is supposed to achieve, which can be formulated simply because the associated graded of
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}
is a free
E_{2}
-algebra and so the structure of its homotopy is completely understood by the work of F. Cohen. In other words,
\pi_{*,*,*}(C\tau\otimes-)
defines a homology theory on
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}
-modules, whose coefficients
\pi_{*,*,*}(C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R})
have a quite simple structure.

5.1.
The elements
x_{i}

Recall from Section
2.7.2
that we write
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}=\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{E_{2}}\mathbb{R}
for the canonical multiplicative filtration of the augmented
E_{2}
-algebra
\mathbb{R}
. Theorem
5.1
will follow from a more precise version in the category of
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}
-modules, which we shall formulate shortly. A distinguished role will be played by certain homotopy classes on the associated graded of
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}
, which we first describe.

Recall that we write
\mathbb{I}:=\mathrm{fib}(\epsilon:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbbm{k})
for the augmentation ideal. There is an equivalence

C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}\simeq\mathbb{E}_{2}((-1)_{*}Q^{E_{2}^{\text{nu}}}\mathbb{I})

and, being a free
E_{2}
-algebra over
\mathbbm{k}
, the homotopy groups of this object may be completely described in terms of
\pi_{*,*}(Q^{E_{2}^{\text{nu}}}\mathbb{I})=:H_{*,*}^{E_{2}}(\mathbb{I})
using the work of F. Cohen
[
10
, Chapter 3]
1
1

1

Cohen considers
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-coefficients whereas we wish to consider a general field
\mathbbm{k}
of positive characteristic
p
. But as
\mathbbm{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}-:\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{F}_{p})\to\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})
is essentially surjective, any free
E_{k}
-algebra in
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
is base-changed from one in
\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{F}_{p})^{\mathbb{Z}}
, so by the Künneth theorem Cohen’s description of its homotopy continues to hold over
\mathbbm{k}
. As free algebras describe the available homology operations, it follows that
E_{k}
-algebras over
\mathbbm{k}
have the same operations as those over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. There are some new features, for example Dyer–Lashof operations are only Frobenius-linear, but this does not affect the description of free
E_{k}
-algebras.
. We will have to assume familiarity with his results; they are too intricate to give a full description here, so we refer to
[
14
, Section 16]
for an exposition. The description is

\pi_{*,*,*}(C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R})\cong W_{1}((-1)_{*}H_{*,*}^{E_{2}}(\mathbb{I})),

the free
W_{1}
-algebra on the
E_{2}
-homology of
\mathbb{I}
, placed in additional grading
-1
. Here
W_{1}(-)
is an explicitly described monad, which is recalled in
[
14
, Section 16]
. For our purposes the following features suffice:

(i)

W_{1}((-1)_{*}H_{*,*}^{E_{2}}(\mathbb{I}))
is a free graded-commutative
\mathbbm{k}
-algebra.

(ii)

Using the equivalences
\mathbbm{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{k}\simeq B^{E_{1}}(\mathbb{R})\simeq\mathbbm{k}\oplus\Sigma Q^{E_{1}^{\text{nu}}}\mathbb{I}
from (
2.2
), axiom (SCE) is equivalent to
H^{E_{1}}_{n,d}(\mathbb{I})=0
for
d<n-1
, and by
[
14
, Theorem 14.4]
this implies that
H^{E_{2}}_{n,d}(\mathbb{I})=0
for
d<n-1
too. (In fact, by
[
14
, Theorem 14.6]
the converse holds too.) Axiom (F) is equivalent to the
H^{E_{1}}_{n,n-1}(\mathbb{I})
being finite-dimensional, and it follows from the bar spectral sequence as in the proof of
[
14
, Theorem 14.4]
that the
H^{E_{2}}_{n,n-1}(\mathbb{I})
are also finite-dimensional.

It is an elementary but laborious calculation using the description of a basis for
W_{1}((-1)_{*}H_{*,*}^{E_{2}}(\mathbb{I}))
in
[
14
, Section 16.2]
that the free graded-commutative algebra generators in tridegrees
(n,d,f)
having
d<n
are given by the free restricted
\lambda_{1}
-algebra

V_{*,*,*}:=L_{1}((-1)_{*}\Big(\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}H_{n,n-1}^{E_{2}}(\mathbb{I})\Big))

on the trigraded vector space
(-1)_{*}(\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}H_{n,n-1}^{E_{2}}(\mathbb{I}))
. As the
H^{E_{2}}_{n,n-1}(\mathbb{I})
are finite-dimensional,
V_{*,*,*}
has finite type.

Definition 5.2
.

If
p=2
let
X_{*,*,*}=V_{*,*,*}
. If
p
is odd, let
X_{*,*,*}\subseteq V_{*,*,*}
be the subspace of those elements of even homological degree.

In either case,
X_{*,*,*}
consists of the generators of the free graded-commutative algebra
\pi_{*,*,*}(C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R})
of tridegrees
(n,d,f)
satisfying
d<n
and which are not nilpotent.

Lemma 5.3
.

(i)

If
p=2
then
X_{N,D,F}
vanishes unless
D=N-1
. If
p
is odd then
X_{N,D,F}
vanishes unless
D=N-1
, or
D=N-2
and
N
is even. In either case, if
X_{N,D,F}
is non-zero then
\tfrac{D}{N}
is a rational number of the form
\tfrac{k}{k+1}
.

(ii)

The formula

X_{N,D,F}\prec X_{N^{\prime},D^{\prime},F^{\prime}}\Longleftrightarrow\tfrac{D}{N}<\tfrac{D^{\prime}}{N^{\prime}},\text{ or }\tfrac{D}{N}=\tfrac{D^{\prime}}{N^{\prime}}\text{ and }\tfrac{F}{D}<\tfrac{F^{\prime}}{D^{\prime}}

defines a total order on the non-zero homogeneous components of
X_{*,*,*}
.

Proof.

To prove (
i
) we get into the details of what a free restricted
\lambda_{1}
-algebra is.

Even characteristic.
In this case a restricted
\lambda_{1}
-algebra is a restricted Lie algebra, so that

V_{*,*,*}=\mathrm{Lie}^{\text{res}}((-1)_{*}\Big(\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}H_{n,n-1}^{E_{2}}(\mathbb{I})\Big)).

As such,
V_{*,*,*}=X_{*,*,*}
is supported in tridegrees
(n,d,f)
having
d=n-1
. (This is because
\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}H_{n,n-1}^{E_{2}}(\mathbb{I})
is supported in such degrees, and the bracket satisfies
|[x,y]|=|x|+|y|+(0,1,0)
and the restriction satisfies
|\xi(x)|=2|x|+(0,1,0)
.)

Odd characteristic.
In this case a restricted
\lambda_{1}
-algebra is a restricted Lie algebra with a further operation
\zeta
, so that

V_{*,*,*}=\mathrm{Lie}^{res}((-1)_{*}\Big(\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}H_{n,n-1}^{E_{2}}(\mathbb{I})\Big))\oplus\zeta\cdot\mathrm{Lie}^{res}((-1)_{*}\Big(\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}H_{n,n-1}^{E_{2}}(\mathbb{I})\Big))_{\text{odd}}.

The first summand is supported in tridegrees
(n,d,f)
with
d=n-1
, by the same argument as above. The second summmand is supported in tridegrees with
d=n-2
and
d
even (so also
n
is even). (This is because
|\zeta(x)|=p|x|+(0,p-2,0)
, so if
|x|=(n,n-1,f)
with
n-1
odd (so
n
even) then
|\zeta(x)|=(pn,p(n-1)+p-2,pf)=(pn,pn-2,pf)
, and
pn-2
is even.) Thus
X_{*,*,*}
is also supported in such degrees.

We now prove (
ii
). It is certainly a preorder, so we first show that it is a partial order, i.e. antisymmetric. If both
X_{N,D,F}\preceq X_{N^{\prime},D^{\prime},F^{\prime}}
and
X_{N,D,F}\succeq X_{N^{\prime},D^{\prime},F^{\prime}}
then
\tfrac{D}{N}=\tfrac{D^{\prime}}{N^{\prime}}
and
\tfrac{F}{D}=\tfrac{F^{\prime}}{D^{\prime}}
. In even characteristic
D=N-1
and
D^{\prime}=N^{\prime}-1
, so the first equation implies
N=N^{\prime}
and
D=D^{\prime}
, and then the second equation implies
F=F^{\prime}
. In odd characteristic
D=N-1
or
N-2
, and
D^{\prime}=N^{\prime}-1
or
N^{\prime}-2
. The first equation shows that the same case must hold for both, and
N=N^{\prime}
and
D=D^{\prime}
, so by the second equation
F=F^{\prime}
too. Thus
\prec
is a partial order.

Given
X_{N,D,F}
and
X_{N^{\prime},D^{\prime},F^{\prime}}
then if
\tfrac{D}{N}\neq\tfrac{D^{\prime}}{N^{\prime}}
they are comparable. If
\tfrac{D}{N}=\tfrac{D^{\prime}}{N^{\prime}}
and
\tfrac{F}{D}\neq\tfrac{F^{\prime}}{D^{\prime}}
then they are also comparable. This leaves the case
\tfrac{D}{N}=\tfrac{D^{\prime}}{N^{\prime}}
and
\tfrac{F}{D}=\tfrac{F^{\prime}}{D^{\prime}}
, in which case the above shows that
X_{N,D,F}=X_{N^{\prime},D^{\prime},F^{\prime}}
so they are again comparable. Thus
\prec
is a total order
∎

We now choose bases for each
X_{N,D,F}
, which are all finite-dimensional, and so obtain a totally ordered homogeneous basis

x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots

for
X_{*,*,*}
subordinate to the total ordering of the non-zero homogeneous components of this trigraded vector space. We write

|x_{i}|=(n_{i},d_{i},f_{i})

for their tridegrees, so that these elements define homotopy classes

x_{i}\in X_{n_{i},d_{i},f_{i}}\subseteq V_{n_{i},d_{i},f_{i}}\subseteq\pi_{n_{i},d_{i},f_{i}}(C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}).

As a matter of language, we will say that
x_{i}
has
slopes

(\tfrac{d_{i}}{n_{i}},\tfrac{f_{i}}{d_{i}})
. More generally, we order slopes lexicographically, so say

\text{$x_{i}$ has slopes $<(\lambda,\mu)$}\Longleftrightarrow\tfrac{d_{i}}{n_{i}}<\lambda,\text{ or }\tfrac{d_{i}}{n_{i}}=\lambda\text{ and }\tfrac{f_{i}}{d_{i}}<\mu

and analogously for slopes
\leq(\lambda,\mu)
.

Remark 5.4
.

We will explain in Section
5.4
some simplifications to this discussion which arise if
\mathbb{R}
is an
E_{k}
-algebra with
k\geq 3
and we use the filtered
E_{k}
-algebra
\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{E_{k}}\mathbb{R}
instead.

5.2.
The filtered form of Theorem
5.1

As
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}
is an
E_{2}
-algebra, for any
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}
-module
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{M}
there is a unit map

u:\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathrm{End}_{\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{M})

landing in the centre.

Definition 5.5
.

A filtered map

\phi_{i}:S^{p^{M}n_{i},p^{M}d_{i},p^{M}f_{i}}\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{M}\longrightarrow\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{M}

is an
x_{i}
self-map
if
C\tau\otimes{\phi_{i}}=(C\tau\otimes u)_{*}(x_{i}^{p^{M}})
for some
M
.

That is,
\phi_{i}
is an
x_{i}
self-map if it induces multiplication by a
p
-th power of
x_{i}
on the associated graded. Using this notion we may formulate a—more precise—filtered form of Theorem
5.1
as follows.

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
\mathbbm{k}
be a field of positive characteristic
p
, and let
\mathbb{R}\in\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{2}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})
satisfy (C), (SCE), and (F). Then there exists a sequence of endomorphisms

\phi_{i}:S^{p^{M_{i}}n_{i},p^{M_{i}}d_{i},p^{M_{i}}f_{i}}\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{i-1})\longrightarrow\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{i-1})

for
i=1,2,\ldots
such that:

(i)

\phi_{i}
is an
x_{i}
self-map.

(ii)

\tau^{-1}\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{i})
has a vanishing line of slope
\tfrac{d_{i+1}}{n_{i+1}}
.

(iii)

If
M
is pre-specified then the
\phi_{i}
may be chosen so that
M_{i}\geq M
for all
i
.

We first explain how this implies Theorem
5.1
.

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

Given
\lambda<1
let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}
be the set of all
x_{i}
’s with
\tfrac{d_{i}}{n_{i}}<\lambda
. Given a
W\in\mathbb{R}
, choose
M
large enough that
\lambda p^{M}n_{i}-p^{M}d_{i}-1\geq W
for all
i=1,\ldots,r
, which is possible as
\tfrac{d_{i}}{n_{i}}<\lambda
. Let
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{r}
be the sequence of endomorphisms provided by Theorem
5.6
, with
M_{i}\geq M
, and set
\alpha_{i}:=\tau^{-1}\phi_{i}
, an endomorphism of
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{i-1})
. These endomorphisms have slopes
\tfrac{d_{i}}{n_{i}}
which are all rational numbers of the form
\tfrac{k}{k+1}
by Lemma
5.3
(
i
). By Theorem
5.6
(
ii
) the object
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})=\tau^{-1}\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{r})
has a vanishing line of slope
\tfrac{d_{r+1}}{n_{r+1}}
, which is
\geq\lambda
by definition of
r
. Thus
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
is an admissible Smith–Toda complex satisfying properties (
i
)–(
iii
) of Theorem
5.1
.
∎

5.3.
Proof of Theorem
5.6

We suppose, for an induction, that a sequence of
x_{j}
self-maps
\phi_{j}
has been defined for
j<i
, so that
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/({\phi}_{1},\ldots,{\phi}_{i-1})
has been constructed, and we consider its internal endomorphism object

\mathrm{End}_{\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{i-1})).

This is a filtered object, and yields a multiplicative spectral sequence of signature

\displaystyle E^{1}_{n,d,f}=\pi_{n,d,f}(C\tau\otimes\mathrm{End}_{\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{i-1})))

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\Longrightarrow\pi_{n,d}(\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{R}}(\tau^{-1}\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{i-1}))).

We begin by establishing some vanishing estimates for
\pi_{*,*,*}(C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/({\phi}_{1},\ldots,{\phi}_{i-1}))
and for this
E^{1}
-page.

Lemma 5.7
.

(i)

There is a
\kappa
such that
\pi_{n,d,f}(C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{i-1}))=0
for
d<\tfrac{d_{i}}{n_{i}}n+\kappa
.

(ii)

For each
\tilde{\kappa}
there is a
\rho
such that
\pi_{n,d,f}(C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{i-1}))=0
whenever
d=\tfrac{d_{i}}{n_{i}}n+\tilde{\kappa}
and
f<\tfrac{f_{i}}{d_{i}}d+\rho
.

(iii)

There is an
r_{0}
such that
E^{1}_{p^{N}n_{i},p^{N}d_{i}-1,p^{N}f_{i}-r}=0
for all
r\geq r_{0}
and all
N
.

Proof.

As we have discussed in Section
5.1
,
\pi_{*,*,*}(C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R})
is a free graded-commutative
\mathbbm{k}
-algebra, and its free generators can be written as
V_{*,*,*}\oplus W^{\prime}_{*,*,*}
with
W^{\prime}_{*,*,*}
being supported in tridegrees
(n,d,f)
with
d\geq n
, and
V_{*,*,*}=U_{*,*,*}\oplus X_{*,*,*}
where
X_{*,*,*}
is as in Definition
5.2
, and therefore
U_{*,*,*}
are the multiplicative generators supported in tridegrees
(n,d,f)
with
d<n
which are nilpotent (i.e. those of odd homological degree when
p
is odd, and zero if
p=2
). Let
x_{i},\ldots,x_{j}
be the
x_{k}
’s with with
i\leq k
and
\tfrac{d_{k}}{n_{k}}=\tfrac{d_{i}}{n_{i}}
, so we have
\tfrac{f_{i}}{d_{i}}\leq\tfrac{f_{i+1}}{d_{i+1}}\leq\cdots\leq\tfrac{f_{j}}{d_{j}}
by definition of the total order
\prec
. Let
W_{*,*,*}
be spanned by
W^{\prime}_{*,*,*}
together with
x_{j+1},x_{j+2},\ldots
. We may therefore write

\pi_{*,*,*}(C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R})=\Lambda^{*}_{\mathbbm{k}}[U_{*,*,*}]\otimes\mathbbm{k}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{j}]\otimes\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbbm{k}}^{*}[W_{*,*,*}].

As
\phi_{k}
induces multiplication by
x_{k}^{p^{M_{k}}}
on associated graded, and these form a regular sequence,
\pi_{*,*,*}(C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{i-1}))
is given by

(5.1)

\Lambda^{*}_{\mathbbm{k}}[U_{*,*,*}]\otimes\frac{\mathbbm{k}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{i-1}]}{(x_{1}^{p^{M_{1}}},\ldots,x_{i-1}^{p^{M_{i-1}}})}\otimes\mathbbm{k}[x_{i},\ldots,x_{j}]\otimes\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbbm{k}}^{*}[W_{*,*,*}].

To prove (
i
), we observe that the first two factors of (
5.1
) combine to give a finite-dimensional trigraded vector space, which is therefore supported in tridegrees
(n,d,f)
with
d\geq\tfrac{d_{i}}{n_{i}}n+\kappa
for some
\kappa
. It suffices to show that the remaining factors are supported in slopes
\geq\tfrac{d_{i}}{n_{i}}
, but the elements
x_{i},\ldots,x_{j}
have slopes
\tfrac{d_{i}}{n_{i}}
, and those of
W_{*,*,*}
have slopes
>\tfrac{d_{i}}{n_{i}}
.

To prove (
ii
), we consider (
5.1
) as describing a free
\mathbbm{k}[x_{i},\ldots,x_{j}]
-module, with module generators

G_{*,*,*}:=\Lambda^{*}_{\mathbbm{k}}[U_{*,*,*}]\otimes\frac{\mathbbm{k}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{i-1}]}{(x_{1}^{p^{M_{1}}},\ldots,x_{i-1}^{p^{M_{i-1}}})}\otimes\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbbm{k}}^{*}[W_{*,*,*}].

As the first two factors are finite-dimensional trigraded vector spaces, and the last is a free graded-commutative algebra on elements of slope
>\tfrac{d_{i}}{n_{i}}
, it follows that for each
\tilde{\kappa}
the vector space
\bigoplus_{d=\tfrac{d_{i}}{n_{i}}n+\tilde{\kappa}}G_{n,d,*}
is finite-dimensional. Thus

L_{\tilde{\kappa}}:=\bigoplus_{d=\tfrac{d_{i}}{n_{i}}n+\tilde{\kappa}}\pi_{n,d,*}(C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/({\phi}_{1},\ldots,{\phi}_{i-1}))

is a finitely-generated free
\mathbbm{k}[x_{i},\ldots,x_{j}]
-module, say with generators
\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{p}\}
having tridegrees
|z_{q}|=(n^{\prime}_{q},d^{\prime}_{q},f^{\prime}_{q})
.

The element
z_{q}x_{i}^{\ell_{i}}\cdots x_{j}^{\ell_{j}}
has tridegree

(n,d,f)=(n^{\prime}_{q}+\sum\ell_{r}n_{r},d^{\prime}_{q}+\sum\ell_{r}d_{r},f^{\prime}_{q}+\sum\ell_{r}f_{r})

and so has

f-\tfrac{f_{i}}{d_{i}}d=(f^{\prime}_{q}-\tfrac{f_{i}}{d_{i}}d^{\prime}_{q})+\sum_{r=i}^{j}\ell_{r}(f_{r}-\tfrac{f_{i}}{d_{i}}d_{r})\geq f^{\prime}_{q}-\tfrac{f_{i}}{d_{i}}d^{\prime}_{q}

independently of the
\ell_{r}
, as
f_{r}-\tfrac{f_{i}}{d_{i}}d_{r}=d_{r}(\tfrac{f_{r}}{d_{r}}-\tfrac{f_{i}}{d_{i}})\geq 0
for all
i\leq r\leq j
. Thus every element of the free module
L_{\tilde{\kappa}}
has tridegrees
(n,d,f)
satisfying

f-\tfrac{f_{i}}{d_{i}}d\geq\min_{1\leq q\leq p}\{f^{\prime}_{q}-\tfrac{f_{i}}{d_{i}}d^{\prime}_{q}\}=:\rho,

and so
L_{\tilde{\kappa}}
vanishes in tridegrees
(n,d,f)
satisfying
f<\tfrac{f_{i}}{d_{i}}d+\rho
.

To prove (
iii
), we first note that
\mathrm{End}_{\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{i-1}))
lies in the thick subcategory generated by
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{i-1})
, by Lemma
2.7
. It follows that
C\tau\otimes\mathrm{End}_{\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{i-1}))
lies in the thick subcategory generated by
C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{i-1})
. The property of satisfying the vanishing conditions described in parts (
i
) and (
ii
) defines a thick subcategory, so it follows from those parts that
C\tau\otimes\mathrm{End}_{\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{i-1}))
