is strong monoidal, we have

\mathbb{S}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}s}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{k}\simeq(\mathbb{S}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{k})\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}(\overline{\mathbb{S}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{k})^{\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}s}.

By (
\dagger
) we have
\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{S}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{k})=0
for
n<0
or
d<\theta n+1
except it is
\mathbbm{k}
for
(n,d)=(0,0)
, and by Lemma
7.3
we have
\pi_{n,d}(\overline{\mathbb{S}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{k})=0
for
n<1
or
d<\theta n
. Putting these together it follows that

\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{S}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}s}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{k})=0\text{ for }n<s\text{ or }d<\theta n.

By our assumption that
\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{E})=0
for
d<\lambda n+\kappa
, and that
\theta\geq\lambda
it follows that

\pi_{\ell n,\ell d+\tilde{\kappa}}(\mathbb{S}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}s}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{E})=0\text{ for }\ell d+\tilde{\kappa}<\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}a+b=n\ell\\
a\geq s\end{subarray}}(\theta a+\lambda b+\kappa)

so as
\theta\geq\lambda
it in particular vanishes for
\ell d+\tilde{\kappa}<\lambda(n\ell)+\kappa+(\theta-\lambda)s
. As
\tfrac{d}{n}=\lambda
this condition may be written as

0=\ell(d-\lambda n)<\kappa-\tilde{\kappa}+(\theta-\lambda)s

and by our assumption that
\theta>\lambda
this condition is satisfied for all
s\gg 0
, independently of
\ell
.
∎

For (
iii
), we consider the map of
\mathbb{S}
-based Adams spectral sequences for the unit
u:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{E}
. This map is central, as
\mathbb{E}
is a
\mathbb{R}
-algebra by assumption, so the maps
E_{r}^{*,*,*}(\mathbb{R})\to E_{r}^{*,*,*}(\mathbb{E})
all land in the centre. In particular the class
z:=(\mathbb{S}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}u)_{*}(x)\in\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{S}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{E})=E_{1}^{n,0,d}(\mathbb{E})
and its powers are central in
E_{1}^{*,*,*}(\mathbb{E})
, and hence also in any page of the spectral sequence they survive to. We wish to show that some
p
-th power of this class lifts along the edge homomorphism
\Phi:\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{E})\to E^{*,0,*}_{1}(\mathbb{E})
, which by convergence is the same as showing it is a permanent cycle. As in the proof of Theorem
5.6
, because
z
is central it follows that we may find
p
-th powers of it surviving until arbitrarily late pages of the spectral sequence.

Applying the Claim with
\tilde{\kappa}=-1
, there is an
s_{0}
such that
E_{1}^{\ell n,s,\ell d-1+s}(\mathbb{E})=0
for all
s\geq s_{0}
and all
\ell
. Choose
N
large enough that
z^{p^{N}}
survives until
E_{s_{0}}^{p^{N}n,0,p^{N}d}(\mathbb{E})
. A potential differential
d_{s}(z^{p^{N}})
with
s\geq s_{0}
lands in
E_{s}^{p^{N}n,s,p^{N}d-1+s}(\mathbb{E})
but these all vanish by our choice of
s_{0}
, so
z^{p^{N}}
is a permanent cycle as required.

For (
iv
), we repeat the proof of Theorem
6.7
(
i
) and (
ii
). Let
\psi\in\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{E})
be some lift of
(\mathbb{S}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}u)_{*}(x^{p^{N}})
provided by (
iii
). As
\mathbb{E}
is a finite
\mathbb{R}
-module,
\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{E})
lies in the thick subcategory generated by
\mathbb{E}
and so also satisfies the assumptions of (
ii
). The elements
\psi\circ-,-\circ\psi\in\pi_{n,d}(\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{E}))
map to
(\mathbb{S}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}u)_{*}(x^{p^{N}})\circ-,-\circ(\mathbb{S}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}u)_{*}(x^{p^{N}})\in\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{S}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{E}))
, which are equal up to a suitable sign as
(\mathbb{S}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}u)_{*}:\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{S})\to\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{S}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{E}))
lands in the graded centre. By (
ii
) it follows that the appropriately signed difference
(\psi\circ-)\pm(-\circ\psi)\in\pi_{n,d}(\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{E}))
is nilpotent, then as
\psi\circ-
and
-\circ\psi
commute it follows that
(\psi^{p^{M}}\circ-)\simeq\pm(-\circ\psi^{p^{M}})
, i.e.
\psi^{p^{M}}
lies in the graded centre of
\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{E})
. Replace
\psi
by
\psi^{p^{M}}
so that it lies in the centre. If
\psi^{\prime}\in\pi_{n^{\prime},d^{\prime}}(\mathbb{E})
is a lift of
(\mathbb{S}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}u)_{*}(x^{p^{N^{\prime}}})
then
(\psi^{\prime})^{p^{N}}-(\psi)^{p^{N^{\prime}}}\in\mathrm{Ker}(\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{E})\to\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{S}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{E}))
so by (
ii
) again this element is nilpotent. As
\psi
is in the centre, this means that
\psi^{\prime}
agrees with
\psi
after taking suitable
p
-th powers.
∎

8.
The stability Hopf algebra

Recall from Section
2.6
there there is an adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Bar}:{\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{2}}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{1}}(\mathsf{coAlg}_{E_{1}}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}))}:\mathrm{Cobar}}

\dashv

which by Proposition
2.2
restricts to an equivalence of categories when we take objects satisfying suitable connectivity hypotheses on each side. This allows us to consider the
E_{1}
-bialgebra
\mathrm{Bar}(\mathbb{R})
in place of an
E_{2}
-algebra
\mathbb{R}
. We will show that when
\mathbb{R}
satisfies (SCE) this allows us to extract a connected graded Hopf algebra
\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}
from
\mathbb{R}
, and we will explain how it controls the stability properties of
\mathbb{R}
.

8.1.
E_{2}
-algebras from Hopf algebras

In Section
2.8
we have discussed the diagonal
t
-structure on
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
, and the fact that its heart is the abelian 1-category of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathbbm{k}
-modules. If
A
is a (
\mathbb{N}
-)graded connected Hopf algebra over
\mathbbm{k}
which is a flat
\mathbbm{k}
-module in each degree, then we may consider it as an
E_{1}
-bialgebra object in
\mathbbm{k}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}^{\mathbb{Z}}=(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})^{\heartsuit}
and hence, using flatness and Warning
2.4
, as an
E_{1}
-bialgebra object in
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
. We may then form

\mathbf{a}:=\mathrm{Cobar}(A)\in\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{2}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}).

The homotopy groups of this object are tautologically given by

\pi_{n,d}(\mathbf{a})=\mathrm{Cotor}_{A}^{n-d}(\mathbbm{k},\mathbbm{k})_{n}.

Using the fact that
A
is connected, and calculating
\mathrm{Cotor}
with the reduced Cobar complex, we see that
\mathbf{a}
satisfies (C). Observing that
A
satisfies the hypothesis of Proposition
2.2
we see that
\mathrm{Bar}(\mathbf{a})\simeq\mathrm{Bar}(\mathrm{Cobar}(A))\simeq A
is supported in diagonal bidegrees, so
\mathbf{a}
satisfies (SCE). Furthermore we see that
\mathbf{a}
satisfies (F) precisely when
A
has finite type.

If
A
is commutative, then it can be considered as an
E_{1}
-coalgebra in
E_{\infty}
-algebras in
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
, and hence
\mathbf{a}=\mathrm{Cobar}(A)
promotes to an
E_{\infty}
-algebra.

Remark 8.1
.

The category
\mathbf{a}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
is equivalent to the category
\mathsf{Stable}(A)
introduced by Hovey
[
26
, Section 2.5]
,
[
27
]
, in its
\infty
-categorical incarnation
[
5
, Definition 4.9]
. To see this, note that Koszul duality identifies the category of finite
\mathbf{a}
-modules with the category of
A
-comodules in
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
whose homotopy groups are finite
\mathbbm{k}
-modules in total. These are precisely the
A
-comodules which are dualisable in
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
: in other words the dualisable objects in the category of
A
-comodules equipped with the monoidal structure
\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}
.
\mathsf{Stable}(A)
is defined as
\mathsf{Ind}
of this category, but
\mathbf{a}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
is
\mathsf{Ind}
of the category of finite
\mathbf{a}
-modules.

8.2.
The stability Hopf algebra

Let
\mathbb{R}\in\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{2}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})
satisfy (C) and (SCE). The underlying object of
\mathrm{Bar}(\mathbb{R})
is
\mathbbm{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{k}
, which by axiom (SCE) has trivial homotopy groups below the diagonal, i.e. in bidegrees
(n,d)
with
d<n
. That is, it is connective with respect to the diagonal
t
-structure. Applying the strong symmetric monoidal functor
\tau_{\leq 0}^{\mathrm{diag}}:\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\to(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})^{\heartsuit}=\mathbbm{k}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}^{\mathbb{Z}}
we obtain a bialgebra object

\Delta:=\tau^{\mathrm{diag}}_{\leq 0}(\mathrm{Bar}(\mathbb{R})).

As it lies in the heart, it is equivalent to the data of its homotopy groups

\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\pi_{n,n}(\mathbbm{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{k}),

which are equipped with the structure of a positively graded and connected bialgebra (so in fact a Hopf algebra). We identify these objects, and call them
\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Supposing that the
\mathbbm{k}
-modules
\pi_{n,n}(\mathbbm{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{k})
are all flat (cf. Warning
2.4
) then
\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}
may be considered as an augmented
E_{1}
-bialgebra in
\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}}
, and truncation

\mathrm{Bar}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\tau_{\leq 0}^{\mathrm{diag}}(\mathrm{Bar}(\mathbb{R}))=\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}

is a map of such. As
\mathbb{R}
satisfies (C) it in particular satisfies the hypothesis of Proposition
2.2
, so
\mathbb{R}\simeq\mathrm{Cobar}(\mathrm{Bar}(\mathbb{R}))
. Applying
\mathrm{Cobar}
to the map above therefore gives a map of
E_{2}
-algebras
2
2

2

I am grateful to Kelly Wang for suggesting this convenient and flexible notation.

\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Cobar}(\Delta_{\mathbb{R}})=:\mathbf{r}.

The following theorem formalises the slogan that “
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbf{r}
enjoy precisely the same kinds of homological stability in slopes
<1
”. Its purpose is that it reduces the problem of determining which kinds of homological stability
\mathbb{R}
has to a problem in the cohomology of the Hopf algebra
\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}
. We will take this principle further in Section
9
.

Theorem 8.2
.

Let
\mathbb{R}
satisfy (C) and (SCE).

(i)

The map
\bigoplus_{n}H^{E_{2}}_{n,n-1}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathbb{R}})\to\bigoplus_{n}H^{E_{2}}_{n,n-1}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathbf{r}})
is an isomorphism. In particular, when
\mathbbm{k}
is a field of positive characteristic
p
and
\mathbb{R}
satisfies (F) we may speak of the same sequence
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots
for both
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbf{r}
.

(ii)

The map
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbf{r}
satisfies assumption
(\dagger)
of Theorem
7.1
with
\theta=1
.

(iii)

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a finite
\mathbb{R}
-module with a slope
\lambda<1
vanishing line. Then for
\tfrac{d}{n}=\lambda
elements in the kernel of

\pi_{n,d}(\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{M}))\longrightarrow\pi_{n,d}(\mathrm{End}_{\mathbf{r}}(\mathbf{r}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{M}))

are nilpotent.

(iv)

Let
\mathbbm{k}
be a field of positive characteristic
p
and
\mathbb{R}
satisfy (F). Fix slopes
(\lambda,\mu)
, let
x_{i},\ldots,x_{j}
be the
x
’s of slopes
(\lambda,\mu)
, and suppose
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{M}\in\mathsf{W}(\lambda,\mu)
. Then there is an equality

I_{\lambda,\mu}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{M})=I_{\lambda,\mu}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbf{r}\otimes_{\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{M})

of ideals of
\mathbbm{k}[x_{i},\ldots,x_{j}]
, where the latter is calculated in the category of
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbf{r}
-modules.

Part (
i
) is an immediate consequence of the following.

Lemma 8.3
.

We have
H^{E_{2}}_{n,d}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathbf{r}},\mathbb{I}_{\mathbb{R}})=0
for
d<n+1
, and in particular the map
H^{E_{2}}_{n,d}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathbb{R}})\to H^{E_{2}}_{n,d}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathbf{r}})
is an isomorphism in bidegrees satisfying
d\leq n-1
.

Proof.

Recall that
\mathbbm{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{k}\simeq\mathrm{Bar}(\mathbb{R})\simeq\mathbbm{k}\oplus\Sigma Q^{E_{1}^{\text{nu}}}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathbb{R}})
by (
2.2
), and similarly for
\mathbf{r}
. By construction the map

\mathbbm{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{k}\longrightarrow\mathbbm{k}\otimes_{\mathbf{r}}\mathbbm{k}\simeq\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}

is an isomorphism on homotopy groups in bidegrees
d\leq n
, and an epimorphism in bidegrees
d\leq n+1
(as the target vanishes in degrees
(n,n+1)
). Thus
H^{E_{1}}_{n,d}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathbf{r}},\mathbb{I}_{\mathbb{R}})=0
for
d<n+1
. We also have that
H^{E_{1}}_{n,d}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathbf{r}})
and
H^{E_{1}}_{n,d}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathbb{R}})
vanish for
d<n-1
. We apply
[
14
, Proposition 14.5]
with
\rho(n)=n
and
\sigma(n)=n+1
, which satisfy
\rho*\rho=\rho
and
\rho*\sigma=\sigma
, to deduce that the
E_{2}
-homology satisfies
H^{E_{2}}_{n,d}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathbf{r}},\mathbb{I}_{\mathbb{R}})=0
for
d<n+1
.
∎

Part (
ii
) is as follows.

Corollary 8.4
.

We have
H_{n,d}^{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbf{r})=0
for
n<1
or
d<n+1
, except that it is
\mathbbm{k}
in bidegree
(0,0)
.

Proof.

By
[
14
, Theorem 15.9]
applied with
\rho(n)=n
and
\sigma(n)=n+1
it follows that there is a map

H^{\mathbb{R}}_{n,d}(\mathbf{r},\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow H^{E_{2}}_{n,d}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathbf{r}},\mathbb{I}_{\mathbb{R}})

which is an isomorphism for
d<n+1
, so in particular the domain vanishes in this range. The difference between this and the stated result is precisely
H_{*,*}^{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{R})=\mathbbm{k}[0,0]
.
∎

Then part (
iii
) follows from this and Theorem
7.1
(
ii
).

For part (
iv
), first note that the containment
I_{\lambda,\mu}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{M})\subseteq I_{\lambda,\mu}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbf{r}\otimes_{\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{M})
is immediate, as a map which has an iterate which is
\tau
-power torsion continues to have this property upon applying
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbf{r}\otimes_{\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}}-
. For the converse, let
y\in I_{\lambda,\mu}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbf{r}\otimes_{\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{M})\subset\mathbbm{k}[x_{i},\ldots,x_{j}]
and let
\psi
be a
y
self-map of
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{M}
. We wish to show that
\tau^{-1}\psi
is a nilpotent endomorphism of the
\mathbb{R}
-module
\tau^{-1}\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{M}
. As we chose
y
in the ideal
I_{\lambda,\mu}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbf{r}\otimes_{\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{M})
we know that
\mathbf{r}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\tau^{-1}\psi=\tau^{-1}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbf{r}\otimes_{\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}}\psi)
is nilpotent, but by part (
iii
) it follows that
\tau^{-1}\psi
is nilpotent, i.e. that
y\in I_{\lambda,\mu}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{M})
.

8.3.
Maps of Hopf algebras

In practice the Hopf algebra
\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}
may not be completely known, and one must work with partial information. In particular, it will often be convenient to pass to a quotient Hopf algebra
\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}\twoheadrightarrow Q
. Here we provide some tools for doing so.

If
f:A\to B
is a morphism of (
\mathbbm{k}
-flat) Hopf algebras then there is a corresponding map of
E_{2}
-algebras

\mathbf{a}:=\mathrm{Cobar}(A)\longrightarrow\mathbf{b}:=\mathrm{Cobar}(B).

This may be used to compare calculations over
\mathbf{a}
with calculations over
\mathbf{b}
, in a range of degrees, as follows.

Proposition 8.5
.

(i)

If
f
is surjective and
\mathrm{Ker}(f)
is supported in gradings
\geq N
, then this map satisfies hypothesis
(\dagger)
of Theorem
7.1
with slope
\theta=\tfrac{N-1}{N}
.

(ii)

If
f
is injective and
\mathrm{Coker}(f)
is supported in gradings
\geq N
, then this map satisfies hypothesis
(\dagger)
of Theorem
7.1
with slope
\theta=\tfrac{N-2}{N}
.

Proof.

As
H^{E_{1}}_{*,*}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathbf{a}})
is
A_{n}
in bidegree
(n,n-1)
and trivial otherwise, and similarly for
\mathbf{b}
, we see that

H^{E_{1}}_{n,d}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathbf{b}},\mathbb{I}_{\mathbf{a}})=\begin{cases}\mathrm{Coker}(A_{n}\to B_{n})&d=n-1\\
\mathrm{Ker}(A_{n}\to B_{n})&d=n\\
0&\text{else}.\end{cases}

Under assumption (i) these are supported in bidegrees
(n,n)
and vanish for
n<N
. In particular they vanish in bidegrees
(n,d)
satisfying
d<\tfrac{N-1}{N}n+1
. Applying
[
14
, Proposition 14.5]
with
\rho(n)=n
and
\sigma(n)=\tfrac{N-1}{N}n+1
, it follows that
H^{E_{2}}_{n,d}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathbf{b}},\mathbb{I}_{\mathbf{a}})
for
d<\tfrac{N-1}{N}n+1
too. Invoking
[
14
, Theorem 15.9]
with the same
\rho
and
\sigma
gives the same vanishing range for
H^{\mathbf{a}}_{n,d}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{a})
. It follows that hypothesis (
\dagger
) of Theorem
7.1
holds with
\theta=\tfrac{N-1}{N}
.

Under assumption (ii) the relative
E_{1}
-homology groups are supported in bidegrees
(n,n-1)
and vanish for
n<N
, so in particular they vanish in bidegrees
(n,d)
satisfying
d<\tfrac{N-2}{N}n+1
. The same line of reasoning gives the conclusion.
∎

If
A
is a connected graded Hopf algebra, and
{P}
is a set of primitive elements in
A
, then the algebra quotient
A\twoheadrightarrow A/({P})
by the two-sided ideal generated by
{P}
canonically obtains the structure of a Hopf algebra. Conversely, if
f:A\twoheadrightarrow B
is a surjective map of Hopf algebras, and
a
is an element of
\mathrm{Ker}(f)
of minimal degree, then
a
is primitive. Both of these are elementary to check, and it follows from the latter that a general quotient of
A
is obtained by killing a (possibly transfinite)
primitive sequence

p_{1},p_{2},\ldots
, where
p_{i}
is primitive modulo the two-sided ideal generated by the previous
p_{j}
’s.

8.4.
The underived canonical multiplicative filtration

The canonical multiplicative filtration
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbf{r}=\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{E_{2}}\mathbf{r}
has

\mathbbm{k}\otimes_{\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{E_{2}}\mathbf{r}}\mathbbm{k}=B^{E_{1}}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{E_{2}}\mathbf{r})\simeq\mathrm{fil}^{E_{1}}_{*}\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}

by Proposition
2.3
. Although
\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}
is an essentially discrete object, as it is in the heart of the diagonal
t
-structure, its canonical
E_{1}
-multiplicative filtration is usually not a filtration by subobjects. Indeed, the associated graded of this filtration is

\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{E_{1}}\Delta_{\mathbb{R}})\simeq E_{1}((-1)_{*}Q^{E_{1}^{\text{nu}}}\bar{\Delta}_{\mathbb{R}})

for
\bar{\Delta}_{\mathbb{R}}:=\mathrm{Ker}(\epsilon:\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathbbm{k})
, and the indecomposables
Q^{E_{1}^{\text{nu}}}\bar{\Delta}_{\mathbb{R}}
will only be a discrete object if
\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}
is a free associative algebra. Our purpose in this section is to compare this canonical
E_{1}
-multiplicative filtration with a filtration by subobjects, namely the filtration of
\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}
by (literal, not derived) powers of the ideal
\bar{\Delta}_{\mathbb{R}}
. To do so let us suppose that
\mathbbm{k}
is a field, to avoid having to worry about flatness.

In fact we may as well discuss the setting of a connected graded Hopf algebra
A
and its corresponding
E_{2}
-algebra
\mathbf{a}:=\mathrm{Cobar}(A)
. The augmentation ideal
\bar{A}:=\mathrm{Ker}(\epsilon:A\to\mathbbm{k})
defines a filtered
E_{1}
-algebra by

\mathrm{fil}_{p}^{\mathrm{aug}}A:=\begin{cases}A&p\geq 0\\
\bar{A}^{-p}&p<0.\end{cases}

and similarly
\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}\bar{A}
. These filtered objects are both complete. We have
\bar{A}=\mathrm{fil}_{-1}^{\mathrm{aug}}\bar{A}
, and as
(-1)_{*}^{E_{1}}
is left adjoint to evaluation at
-1
by adjunction we obtain a filtered non-unital
E_{1}
-algebra map

\mathrm{fil}^{E_{1}}_{*}\bar{A}\longrightarrow\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}\bar{A},

and, by unitalising, a filtered
E_{1}
-algebra map

\mathrm{fil}^{E_{1}}_{*}{A}\longrightarrow\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}{A}.

They both induce an equivalence on colimits. The latter may be checked to in fact be
E_{1}
-bialgebra map, using that the target is in the heart of the diagonal
t
-structure. Taking the cobar construction again gives a filtered
E_{2}
-algebra map

\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbf{a}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Cobar}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}A)=:\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}\mathbf{a},

which then also induces an equivalence on colimits.

The spectral sequence associated to
\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}\mathbf{a}
computes
\mathrm{Cotor}^{*}_{A}(\mathbbm{k},\mathbbm{k})
starting from
\mathrm{Cotor}
over the associated graded Hopf algebra

\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}A)=0_{*}\mathbbm{k}\oplus\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}(-n)_{*}\bar{A}^{n}/\bar{A}^{n+1}.

This kind of spectral sequence was first considered by Ivanovsky
[
29
]
for
A
the dual Steenrod algebra; it is in a sense dual, and in practical terms quite similar, to the May spectral sequence
[
40
]
: a further useful reference is Bajer–Sadofsky
[
4
]
. The associated graded Hopf algebra
\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}A)
is generated in grading
-1
and so is primitively generated, and is therefore the universal restricted enveloping algebra of its restricted Lie algebra of primitive elements
[
44
, Theorem 6.11]
. In particular it is cocommutative.

Supposing now that
\mathbbm{k}
is
\mathbb{Q}
or
\mathbb{F}_{p}
(or more generally a perfect field), it follows from a theorem of Borel
[
44
, Theorem 7.11]
that the dual
\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}A)^{\vee}
is isomorphic
as an algebra
to a tensor product of monogenic Hopf algebras. That is, of the connected Hopf algebras

\displaystyle\mathbbm{k}[x]

with
x
of even degree

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\mathbbm{k}}[y]

with
y
of odd degree

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}_{p}[x]/(x^{p^{\ell}})

(with
x
of even degree if
p
is odd)

with
x
or
y
primitive. Knowing
\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}A)^{\vee}
as an algebra suffices to compute

\mathrm{Ext}^{*}_{\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}A)^{\vee}}(\mathbbm{k},\mathbbm{k})\cong\mathrm{Ext}^{*}_{\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}A)\text{-}\mathsf{comod}}(\mathbbm{k},\mathbbm{k})\cong\mathrm{Cotor}^{*}_{\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}A)}(\mathbbm{k},\mathbbm{k})

as an algebra (and the result will be a commutative algebra because
\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}A)
is in fact a Hopf algebra), however it does not suffice to determine the Browder bracket or Dyer–Lashof operations, as these are encoded by the product on
\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}A)
, i.e. the coproduct on
\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}A)^{\vee}
. The monogenic Hopf algebras have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ext}^{*}_{\mathbbm{k}[x]}(\mathbbm{k},\mathbbm{k})

\displaystyle\cong\Lambda_{\mathbbm{k}}[\underline{x}^{\vee}]

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ext}^{*}_{\Lambda_{\mathbbm{k}}[y]}(\mathbbm{k},\mathbbm{k})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbbm{k}[\underline{y}^{\vee}]

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ext}^{*}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}[x]/(x^{p^{\ell}})}(\mathbb{F}_{p},\mathbb{F}_{p})

\displaystyle\cong\Lambda_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}[\underline{x}^{\vee}]\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}[t]\quad\text{$p$ odd or $p=2$ and $\ell>1$}

where we write
\underline{z}^{\vee}\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1}
for the dual to the indecomposable element
z
, and in the final case
t
can be described as the
p^{\ell}
-fold Massey product
\langle\underline{x}^{\vee},\underline{x}^{\vee},\ldots,\underline{x}^{\vee}\rangle
: in particular, in our usual grading we have
|\underline{z}^{\vee}|=(|z|-1,|z|)
and
|t|=(p^{\ell}|x|-2,p^{\ell}|x|)
.

If the Hopf algebra
\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}A)
is in addition commutative—which will certainly be the case if
A
is commutative, which in turn will certainly be the case if it is
\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}
for
\mathbb{R}
an
E_{3}
-algebra—then by the classification of connected primitively generated abelian Hopf algebras over a perfect field
[
44
, Theorem 7.16]
it is isomorphic to a locally finite tensor product of monogenic Hopf algebras
as a Hopf algebra
. The monogenic Hopf algebras have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cotor}^{*}_{\mathbb{Q}[x]}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle\cong\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}[\underline{x}]

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cotor}^{*}_{\Lambda_{\mathbbm{k}}[y]}(\mathbbm{k},\mathbbm{k})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbbm{k}[\underline{y}]

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cotor}^{*}_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[x]/(x^{2^{\ell}})}(\mathbb{F}_{2},\mathbb{F}_{2})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}[\underline{x},\xi(\underline{x}),\xi^{2}(\underline{x}),\ldots\xi^{\ell-1}(\underline{x})]

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cotor}^{*}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}[x]/(x^{p^{\ell}})}(\mathbb{F}_{p},\mathbb{F}_{p})

\displaystyle\cong\Lambda_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}[\underline{x},\xi(\underline{x}),\ldots\xi^{\ell-1}(\underline{x})]\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}[\zeta(\underline{x}),\zeta(\xi(\underline{x}),\ldots,\zeta(\xi^{\ell-1}(\underline{x})))]

where we write
\underline{z}\in\mathrm{Cotor}^{1}
for the class of a primitive element
z
, and the case of polynomial algebras over finite fields is included by formally allowing
\ell=\infty
. This calculation is expressed as the homology of an
E_{2}
-algebra, and the operations
\xi
and
\zeta
have their usual meanings from this context. In fact, as the monogenic Hopf algebras are all commutative, their cobar constructions are all
E_{\infty}
-algebras so we may express the result in terms of Dyer–Lashof operations by the notational substitutions
\xi(t)=Q^{1+|t|}(t)
for
p=2
, and
\xi(t)=Q^{(1+|t|)/2}(t)
and
\zeta(t)=\beta Q^{(1+|t|)/2}(t)
for
p
odd. (This also accounts for why no Browder brackets arise.)

In either case this allows us to fully describe the
E_{1}
-page of the spectral sequence

\pi_{*,*,*}(\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}\mathbf{a}))=\mathrm{Cotor}^{*}_{\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}A)}(\mathbbm{k},\mathbbm{k})\Longrightarrow\mathrm{Cotor}^{*}_{A}(\mathbbm{k},\mathbbm{k})=\pi_{*,*}(\mathbf{a})

as a commutative algebra, and similarly for the spectral sequence associated to a filtered Smith–Toda complex
\mathrm{fil}^{\mathrm{aug}}_{*}\mathbf{a}/(\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{s})
considered as a module over the above. Furthermore, being a spectral sequence associated to a purely algebraic filtration it is in principle algorithmically computable: methods developed to study the May spectral sequence
[
40
,
51
,
36
]
will surely be useful here.

8.5.
Detecting periodic families

There is a further kind of application of the Hopf algebra
\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}
, not directly to establishing homological stability properties of
\mathbb{R}
but rather to finding periodic families of elements in
\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{R})
. The idea is very simple: if
\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}\to Q
is a (quotient) map of Hopf algebras, then there are maps of
E_{2}
-algebras

\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbf{r}=\mathrm{Cobar}(\Delta_{\mathbb{R}})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Cobar}(Q)=:\mathbf{q},

which we may try to use to detect elements of
\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{R})
. This will be pursued elsewhere.

9.
Existence of Smith–Toda complexes revisited

In this section we wish to explain another construction of Smith–Toda complexes, building on the Hopf algebra methods introduced in the previous section. The method is more
ad hoc
, but is somewhat stronger in that it is able to construct all stabilisation maps of a given slope at once. That is, for a finite
\mathbb{R}
-module
\mathbb{M}
with a slope
\lambda
vanishing line, it can construct slope
\lambda
endomorphisms
\delta_{s},\ldots,\delta_{t}
of
\mathbb{M}
which coherently homotopy commute with each other, and such that
\mathbb{M}/(\delta_{s},\ldots,\delta_{t})
has a vanishing line of slope
>\lambda
.

We will describe this method when
\mathbb{R}
is an
E_{3}
-algebra (what will will say in fact works whenever the Hopf algebra
\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}
is known to be commutative), and comment in Remark
9.3
on what will need to be done to extend this to
E_{2}
-algebras.

9.1.
Commutative Hopf algebras

We collect here the facts about graded connected commutative Hopf algebras that we will use.

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
A
be a graded connected Hopf algebra over a field of positive characteristic, such that its indecomposables and primitives are both finite-dimensional. Suppose that
A
is commutative (or cocommutative). Then
A
is finite-dimensional.

Proof.

By dualising if necessary, we may suppose that
A
is commutative. We will show that every element of
A
of strictly positive degree is nilpotent: in particular the finitely-many algebra generators are nilpotent, from which it follows that
A
is finite-dimensional.

Supposing the conclusion did not hold, so there are non-nilpotent elements of strictly positive degree, let
x\in A
be one of minimal degree. Then
\psi(x)=1\otimes x+\sum_{i}x_{i}^{\prime}\otimes x_{i}^{\prime\prime}+x\otimes 1
where
x_{i}^{\prime},x_{i}^{\prime\prime}\in A
have strictly positive degrees which are strictly smaller than the degree of
x
. Thus
x_{i}^{\prime}
and
x_{i}^{\prime\prime}
are nilpotent, so for
N\gg 0

\psi(x^{p^{N}})=1\otimes x^{p^{N}}+x^{p^{N}}\otimes 1
, and hence
x^{p^{N}}
is primitive. But if
x
is non-nilpotent then
\{x^{p^{M}}\}_{M\geq N}
is an infinite linearly-independent set of non-zero primitive elements, so the primitives of
A
are not finite-dimensional: this is a contradiction.
∎

The following combines
[
54
, Theorem A]
and
[
4
, Theorem 1.2]
.

Theorem 9.2

(Wilkerson, Bajer–Sadofsky)
.

Let
A
be a finite-dimensional graded connected Hopf algebra over a field
\mathbbm{k}
of positive characteristic. Suppose that
A
is commutative. Then

(i)

\mathrm{Cotor}_{A}^{*}(\mathbbm{k},\mathbbm{k})_{*}
is finitely-generated as a
\mathbbm{k}
-algebra.

(ii)

The spectral sequence
\mathrm{Cotor}_{\mathrm{gr}(A)}^{*}(\mathbbm{k},\mathbbm{k})_{*}\Rightarrow\mathrm{Cotor}_{A}^{*}(\mathbbm{k},\mathbbm{k})_{*}
, associated to the filtration of
A
by powers of the augmentation ideal, collapses at a finite page.

∎

Remark 9.3
.

To extend the methods we are about to describe to
E_{2}
-algebras, one needs Theorems
9.1
and
9.2
with the assumption “Suppose that
A
is commutative” removed.

For Theorem
9.1
experts in Hopf algebras tell me that it is plausible, but I have not succeeded in making progress.

For Theorem
9.2
the problem is merely technical. What is needed is to prove
[
54
, Proposition 3.2]
, which Wilkerson does using power operations in the Cartan–Eilenberg spectral sequence for a conormal extension of commutative Hopf algebras. But the operations required are those that exist in a spectral sequence of
E_{2}
-algebras, and as
\mathrm{Cobar}(A)
is an
E_{2}
-algebra for any Hopf algebra
A
it seems extremely likely that the Cartan–Eilenberg filtration can be lifted to a filtered
E_{2}
-algebra.

9.2.
The method

Let
\mathbb{R}
be an
E_{3}
-algebra satisfying (C), (SCE), and (F). Fix a slope
\lambda<1
. Let
x_{s},\ldots,x_{t}
be the
x
’s of
\tfrac{d}{n}
-slope precisely
\lambda
. Choose an integer
\delta
large enough that
n_{1},\ldots,n_{t}<\delta
and
\lambda<\tfrac{\delta-1}{\delta+1}
. Form the Hopf algebra quotient

\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}\longrightarrow Q

by killing all primitives of grading
>\delta
, and repeating this infinitely often: thus
Q
has no primitives of grading
>\delta
. Taking
\mathrm{Cobar}
, there are maps of filtered
E_{3}
-algebras

\rho:\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbf{r}\longrightarrow\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbf{q}\longrightarrow\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}\mathbf{q}.

Lemma 9.4
.

There are filtered homotopy classes
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{t}\in\pi_{*,*,*}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}\mathbf{q})
such that
C\tau\otimes\xi_{i}
is a
p
-th power of
(C\tau\otimes\rho)_{*}(x_{i})
.

The object
\tau^{-1}\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}\mathbf{q}/(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{t})
has a vanishing line of slope
>\lambda
.

Proof.

Form the Hopf subalgebra
A\to Q
generated by
\bigoplus_{n\leq\delta}Q_{n}
(in other words, the quotient
Q^{\vee}\to A^{\vee}
is obtained by iteratedly killing all primitives of grading
>\delta
). There are maps of filtered
E_{3}
-algebras
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}\overset{\rho}{\to}\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}\mathbf{q}\leftarrow\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}\mathbf{a}
.
As
\mathbb{R}
is an
E_{3}
-algebra,
\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}
is a commutative Hopf algebra and hence so are
Q
and
A
.
The commutative Hopf algebra
A
has finite-dimensional primitives and indecomposables, so is finite-dimensional by Theorem
9.1
. Thus by Theorem
9.2
the spectral sequence

\mathrm{Cotor}_{\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}^{\mathrm{aug}}_{*}A)}^{d-n}(\mathbbm{k},\mathbbm{k})_{n}\Longrightarrow\mathrm{Cotor}_{A}^{d-n}(\mathbbm{k},\mathbbm{k})_{n}=\pi_{n,d}(\mathbf{a})

collapses at a finite page.

On associated graded, the maps above induce

\pi_{*,*,*}(\mathbb{E}_{3}((-1)_{*}Q^{E_{3}^{\text{nu}}}(\mathbb{I}_{\mathbb{R}})))\overset{\rho_{*}}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{Cotor}_{\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}^{\mathrm{aug}}_{*}Q)}^{*}(\mathbbm{k},\mathbbm{k})_{*}\longleftarrow\mathrm{Cotor}_{\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}^{\mathrm{aug}}_{*}A)}^{*}(\mathbbm{k},\mathbbm{k})_{*}.

The
x_{1},\ldots,x_{t}
map to elements
x_{1}^{Q},\ldots,x_{t}^{Q}\in\mathrm{Cotor}_{\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}^{\mathrm{aug}}_{*}Q)}^{*}(\mathbbm{k},\mathbbm{k})_{*}
, and as
A\to Q
is an isomorphism in gradings
<\delta
, so
\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}^{\mathrm{aug}}_{*}A)\to\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}^{\mathrm{aug}}_{*}Q)
is too, these lift uniquely to classes
x_{1}^{A},\ldots,x_{t}^{A}\in\mathrm{Cotor}_{\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}^{\mathrm{aug}}_{*}A)}^{*}(\mathbbm{k},\mathbbm{k})_{*}
. As the spectral sequence for
\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}\mathbf{a}
collapses at a finite page, the
p^{M}
-th power of these are permanent cycles for some
M\gg 0
, detecting classes
\xi_{1}^{A},\ldots,\xi_{t}^{A}\in\pi_{*,*,*}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}\mathbf{a})
: these map to the required classes in
\pi_{*,*,*}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}\mathbf{q})
.

To prove the second part, first note that as
A
is a finite-dimensional commutative Hopf algebra,
\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}^{\mathrm{aug}}_{*}A)
is a tensor product of monogenic Hopf algebras and so
\mathrm{Cotor}_{\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}^{\mathrm{aug}}_{*}A)}^{*}(\mathbbm{k},\mathbbm{k})_{*}
is a tensor product of a polynomial and perhaps an exterior algebra. The elements
x_{1}^{A},\ldots,x_{t}^{A}
can be taken as the polynomial generators of
\tfrac{d}{n}
-slope
<\lambda
, so that

\mathrm{Cotor}_{\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{fil}^{\mathrm{aug}}_{*}A)}^{*}(\mathbbm{k},\mathbbm{k})_{*}=\mathbbm{k}[x_{1}^{A},\ldots,x_{r}^{A}]\otimes\Lambda_{\mathbbm{k}}[x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}].

The spectral sequence for
\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}\mathbf{a}/(\xi_{1}^{A},\ldots,\xi_{t}^{A})
therefore takes the form

\frac{\mathbbm{k}[x_{1}^{A},\ldots,x_{r}^{A}]}{((x_{1}^{A})^{p^{M}},\ldots,(x_{t}^{A})^{p^{M}})}\otimes\Lambda_{\mathbbm{k}}[x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}]\Longrightarrow\pi_{*,*}(\tau^{-1}\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}\mathbf{a}/(\xi_{1}^{A},\ldots,\xi_{s}^{A})),

showing that
\tau^{-1}\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{\mathrm{aug}}\mathbf{a}/(\xi_{1}^{A},\ldots,\xi_{t}^{A})
has a slope
>\lambda
vanishing line. The claim now follows by applying
\mathbf{q}\otimes_{\mathbf{a}}-
, and appealing to Theorem
7.1
(
i
) via Proposition
8.5
(
ii
). The latter applies to the map of Hopf algebras
A\to Q
with
N=\delta+1
, yielding
\theta=\tfrac{\delta-1}{\delta+1}
which is
>\lambda
by our choice of
\delta
.
∎

The
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{t}
produced in the proof of this lemma are not very canonical, but we choose them once and for all. We use the same notation for the images of these classes in
\pi_{*,*}(\mathbf{q})
given by inverting
\tau
. The idea is now to repeat the kind of argument from Sections
5
and
6
, but instead of constructing self-maps
\phi_{i}
for
s\leq i\leq t
characterised by
C\tau\otimes\phi_{i}
being a
p
-th power of
(C\tau\otimes u)_{*}(x_{i})
, we will construct self-maps
\gamma_{i}
characterised by
\mathbf{q}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\gamma_{i}
being a
p
-th power of
u_{*}(\xi_{i})
.

To formalise this, for a
\beta\in\pi_{*,*}(\mathbf{q})
let us say that an endomorphism
\psi
of a
\mathbb{R}
-module
\mathbb{M}
is a
\beta
self-map
if

\mathbf{q}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\psi=u_{*}(\beta^{p^{M}})\in\pi_{*,*}(\mathrm{End}_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{q}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{M}))

for some
M
. We will prove an analogue of Theorems
6.6
and
6.7
on the existence of of
\beta
self-maps, and their uniqueness and so on. The class of objects to which the argument applies is the following.

Definition 9.5
.

Let
\mathsf{W}(\lambda)\subseteq\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
be the full subcategory consisting of the finite
\mathbb{R}
-modules
\mathbb{M}
for which there is a
\kappa
such that
\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{M})=0
for all
d<\lambda n+\kappa
. (The objects of this category are precisely
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
from Section
3
.)

Just as in Proposition
6.5
, this is a thick subcategory, and there are containments
\mathsf{W}(\lambda)\subseteq\mathsf{W}(\bar{\lambda})
when
\lambda\geq\bar{\lambda}
.

Theorem 9.6
.

Let
\beta\in\pi_{N,D}(\mathbf{q})
be given, with
\tfrac{D}{N}=\lambda
.

(i)

All objects
\mathbb{M}\in\mathsf{W}(\lambda)
admit a
\beta
self-map.

(ii)

If
\psi
is a
\beta
self-map of
\mathbb{M}\in\mathsf{W}(\lambda)
then after perhaps taking a
p
-th power it lies in the graded centre of
\pi_{*,*}(\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{M}))
.

(iii)

If
\psi
and
\psi^{\prime}
are
\beta
self-maps of
\mathbb{M}\in\mathsf{W}(\lambda)
then they agree after perhaps taking
p
-th powers.

(iv)

If
\mathbb{M}
and
\mathbb{N}
are in
\mathsf{W}(\lambda)
and have
\beta
self-maps
\psi^{\mathbb{M}}
and
\psi^{\mathbb{N}}
of the same tridegrees, then after perhaps taking
p
-th powers they intertwine all
\mathbb{R}
-module maps
f:S^{n,d}\otimes\mathbb{M}\to\mathbb{N}
.

(v)

If
\mathbb{M}\overset{f}{\to}\mathbb{N}\overset{g}{\to}\mathbb{P}\overset{\partial}{\to}\Sigma\mathbb{M}
is a distinguished triangle of
\mathbb{R}
-modules, and
\mathbb{M}
and
\mathbb{N}
are in
\mathsf{W}(\lambda)
, then so is
\mathbb{P}
. Furthermore they admit
\beta
self-maps
\psi^{\mathbb{M}}
,
\psi^{\mathbb{N}}
, and
\psi^{\mathbb{P}}
which assemble into a morphism of distinguished triangles.

Proof.

The map
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbf{q}
satisfies property (
\dagger
) of Theorem
7.1
with
\theta=\tfrac{\delta}{\delta+1}
by Theorem
8.2
(
ii
) and Proposition
8.5
, and we had
\lambda<\tfrac{\delta-1}{\delta+1}<\tfrac{\delta}{\delta+1}
by assumption.

To prove (
i
) we apply Theorem
7.1
(
iii
) with
\mathbb{E}:=\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{M})
and
x=\beta
, which precisely gives a
\beta
self-map
\psi
. Then (
ii
) and (
iii
) follow from Theorem
7.1
(
iv
), and (
iv
) and (
v
) are then deduced in the same way as the corresponding parts of Theorem
6.7
.
∎

9.3.
Simultaneous stabilisation

If
\mathbb{M}\in\mathsf{W}(\lambda)
then by Theorem
9.6
(i) there are
\xi_{i}
self-maps
\gamma_{i}
of
\mathbb{M}
for
s\leq i\leq t
, and by Theorem
9.6
(ii) they can be chosen to be central in the endomorphism ring, so in particular to commute with each other up to homotopy. There is a strengthening of this, which was in fact our motivation for this method: they can be chosen to simultaneously commute up to coherent homotopy, in the following sense.

Proposition 9.7
.

For a sequence
\beta_{i}\in\pi_{N_{i},D_{i}}(\mathbf{q})
with
1\leq i\leq r
, having
\tfrac{D_{i}}{N_{i}}=\lambda
, and
\mathbb{M}\in\mathsf{W}(\lambda)
, there are
\beta_{i}
-self maps
\psi_{i}
and a commutative cube

[1]^{r}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}

whose restriction to each 1-morphism
\{\epsilon_{1}\}\times\cdots\times\{\epsilon_{i-1}\}\times[1]\times\{\epsilon_{i+1}\}\times\cdots\times\{\epsilon_{r}\}
is (a shift of)
\psi_{i}
, for all
\epsilon
’s in
\{0,1\}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
r
, so suppose that
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{r-1}
and the required
(r-1)
-cube
c:[1]^{r-1}\to\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
has been constructed. The category
\mathsf{Fun}([1]^{r-1},\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod})
of such cubes is (left) tensored over the presentable category
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
and hence admits mapping objects in that category. We then let

\mathbb{E}:=\mathrm{End}_{\mathsf{Fun}([1]^{r-1},\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod})}(c)\in\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod};

composition equips this with the structure of a unital associative algebra in the homotopy category of
\mathbb{R}
-modules (which could be promoted to an
E_{1}
-structure, but this suffices). This object may be expressed as the limit of a finite diagram whose entries are of the form
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}(S^{n,d}\otimes\mathbb{M},S^{n^{\prime},d^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbb{M})
. All such objects are in the thick subcategory
\mathsf{W}(\lambda)
, so a finite limit of them is too: thus
\mathbf{E}\in\mathsf{W}(\lambda)
. By Theorem
9.6
(i) there is a
\psi_{r}\in\pi_{*,*}(\mathbf{E})
such that

\mathbf{q}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\psi_{r}=u_{*}(\beta_{r}^{p^{M}})\in\pi_{*,*}(\mathrm{End}_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{q}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{E})).

Interpreted using the definition of this endomorphism object, this
\psi_{r}
is exactly a morphism
(S^{p^{M}N_{r},p^{M}D_{r}}\otimes\mathbb{R})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}c\to c
in
\mathsf{Fun}([1]^{r-1},\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod})
, i.e. an
r
-cube in
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
, which by naturality induces a
\beta_{r}
self-map when restricted to each vertex of
[1]^{r-1}
, as required.
∎

By the discussion so far, for
\mathbb{M}\in\mathsf{W}(\lambda)
we may find
\xi_{i}
self-maps
\gamma_{i}
for
s\leq i\leq t
, by Proposition
9.7
we may arrange these into a commutative
(s-t)
-cube, and we may then let

\mathbb{M}/(\gamma_{s},\ldots,\gamma_{t})

denote the total homotopy cofibre of this cube.

Proposition 9.8
.

\mathbb{M}/(\gamma_{s},\ldots,\gamma_{t})
lies in
\mathsf{W}(\bar{\lambda})
for some
\bar{\lambda}>\lambda
.

Proof.

By Theorem
7.1
(i) it suffices to check this after applying
\mathbf{q}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}-
. The
\mathbf{q}
-module
\mathbb{N}:=\mathbf{q}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{M}
has a slope
\lambda
vanishing line also by Theorem
7.1
(i), and

\mathbf{q}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{M}/(\gamma_{s},\ldots,\gamma_{t}))\simeq\mathbb{N}\otimes_{\mathbf{q}}\mathbf{q}/(\xi_{s}^{p^{M_{s}}},\ldots,\xi_{t}^{p^{M_{t}}}).

The latter is in the thick subcategory of
\mathbb{N}\otimes_{\mathbf{q}}\mathbf{q}/(\xi_{s},\ldots,\xi_{t})
, so it suffices to show that this has a slope
>\lambda
vanishing line.

We also have available the classes
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{s-1}\in\pi_{*,*}(\mathbf{q})
, letting us consider

\mathbb{N}\otimes_{\mathbf{q}}\mathbf{q}/(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{t}).

For
1\leq i<s
the endomorphism
\xi_{i}\cdot-
of
\mathbb{N}\otimes_{\mathbf{q}}\mathbf{q}/(\gamma_{i+1},\ldots,\gamma_{t})
is nilpotent, as
\xi_{i}
has slope
<\lambda
but this object has a slope
\lambda
vanishing line as it is in the thick subcategory generated by
\mathbb{N}
. It follows by downwards induction that
\mathbb{N}\otimes_{\mathbf{q}}\mathbf{q}/(\xi_{s},\ldots,\xi_{t})
is in the thick subcategory of
\mathbb{N}\otimes_{\mathbf{q}}\mathbf{q}/(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{t})
, and as
\mathbb{N}
is a finite
\mathbf{q}
-module
\mathbb{N}\otimes_{\mathbf{q}}\mathbf{q}/(\xi_{s},\ldots,\xi_{t})
is therefore in the thick subcategory of
\mathbf{q}/(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{t})
. The latter has a vanishing line of slope
>\lambda
by Lemma
9.4
, so
\mathbb{N}\otimes_{\mathbf{q}}\mathbf{q}/(\xi_{s},\ldots,\xi_{t})
does too.
∎

9.4.
Efficiency

Just as in Sections
6.3
and
6.4
we can often get away with fewer stabilisation maps. By Lemma
9.4
the object
\mathbf{q}/(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{t})
has a vanishing line of slope
>\lambda
, so choose polynomials

\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{r}\in\mathbbm{k}[\xi_{s},\ldots,\xi_{t}]

such that

(i)

\mathbf{q}/(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{s-1},\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{r})
has a vanishing line of slope
>\lambda
, and

(ii)

this cannot be achieved using fewer than
r

\zeta
’s.

If
\mathbb{M}\in\mathsf{W}(\lambda)
then by Theorem
9.6
(i) there are
\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{r}
self-maps
\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{r}
of
\mathbb{M}
, which can again be arranged into a homotopy commutative cube with total homotopy cofibre
\mathbb{M}/(\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{r})
.

Proposition 9.9
.

\mathbb{M}/(\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{r})
lies in
\mathsf{W}(\bar{\lambda})
for some
\bar{\lambda}>\lambda
.

Proof.

By the same method as the proof of Proposition
9.8
, we reduce to showing that the
\mathbf{q}
-module
\mathbf{q}/(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{s-1},\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{r})
has a vanishing line of slope
>\lambda
, but this is true by assumption.
∎

Finally, we provide an analogue of Theorem
6.12
.

Proposition 9.10
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{M}\in\mathsf{W}(\lambda)
generates the same thick subcategory as
\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{s-1})
. Then the endomorphism
\delta_{i}
of
\mathbb{M}/(\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{i-1})
is not nilpotent.

Proof.

The subcategory of
\mathsf{W}(\lambda)
of those objects whose
\zeta_{i}
self-map
\delta_{i}
is nilpotent is thick by Theorem
9.6
(
v
), so it suffices to show that
\delta_{i}
is non-nilpotent on
\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{s-1},\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{i-1})
. By Theorem
7.1
(
ii
) it suffices to show that
\zeta_{i}\cdot-
is non-nilpotent on
\mathbf{q}/(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{s-1},\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{i-1})
. If it were, then the
\mathbf{q}
-module
\mathbf{q}/(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{s-1},\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{i-1},\zeta_{i+1},\ldots,\zeta_{r})
would lie in the thick subcategory of
\mathbf{q}/(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{s-1},\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{r})
. Then
\mathbf{q}/(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{s-1},\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{i-1},\zeta_{i+1},\ldots,\zeta_{r})
would have a vanishing line of slope
>\lambda
, but only use
(r-1)

\zeta
’s which contradicts our minimality assumption.
∎

Remark 9.11
.

In practice one might have
\mathbf{q}/(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{s-1},\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{r})
with a vanishing line of slope
>\lambda
, but find it hard to know that there is no possible shorter sequence of
\zeta
’s achieving this. If one checks that each
\mathbf{q}/(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{s-1},\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{i-1},\zeta_{i+1},\ldots,\zeta_{r})
obtained by omitting a single
\zeta
does not have a vanishing line of slope
>\lambda
, then the argument and conclusion of Proposition
9.10
still holds.

In particular, we can always find a subsequence of
\xi_{s},\ldots,\xi_{t}
inducing a sequence of non-nilpotent self-maps and achieving a vanishing line of slope
>\lambda
, but as in Example
6.16
such a sequence will not always have the minimal length possible.

10.
Miscellany

We collate here some extended examples and more speculative discussion which would have been distracting in the middle of the text.

10.1.
Some features of homological multi-stability

The following example shows that in the setting of multi-stability described in Section
1.3.2
one must not be too ambitious with what one tries to say about the structure of
\pi_{*,d}(\mathbb{R})
as a
\pi_{*,0}(\mathbb{R})
-module.

Example 10.1
.

Let
\mathbbm{k}=\mathbb{Q}
, let
\ell\geq 2
and
N\gg 0
be given, and set

\mathbb{R}:=\mathbb{E}_{\infty}(S^{1,0}r\oplus S^{1,0}b\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}S^{\ell+1,\ell}x_{i})\cup^{E_{\infty}}D^{\ell+2,\ell+1}y_{1}\cup^{E_{\infty}}\cdots\cup^{E_{\infty}}D^{\ell+2,\ell+1}y_{N+1}

where
y_{1}
is attached along
r\cdot x_{1}
,
y_{j}
is attached along
r\cdot x_{j}-b\cdot x_{j-1}
for
1<j<N+1
, and
y_{N+1}
is attached along
-b\cdot x_{N}
. This
E_{\infty}
-algebra satisfies (C), (SCE), and (F), and one calculates that
\pi_{n,d}(\mathbb{R}/(r,b))=0
for
d<\tfrac{\ell}{\ell+1}n
, as
\mathbb{R}/(r,b)
is equivalent to
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}S^{\ell+1,\ell}x_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{j=1}^{N+1}S^{\ell+2,\ell+1}y_{j})
.

Now
\pi_{*,0}(\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{Q}[r,b]
, and we calculate that

\displaystyle\pi_{*,\ell}(\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Q}[r,b]\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\}/(r\cdot x_{1},r\cdot x_{i}-b\cdot x_{i-1},-b\cdot x_{N})

\displaystyle\pi_{*,\ell+1}(\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Q}[r,b]\{b^{N}\cdot y_{1}+b^{N-1}r\cdot y_{2}+\cdots+br^{N-1}\cdot y_{N}+r^{N}\cdot y_{N+1}\}

as
\mathbb{Q}[r,b]
-modules, with the next non-trivial homotopy group
\pi_{*,2\ell}(\mathbb{R})
. The calculation will look the same for
\mathbb{Q}
replaced by
\mathbb{F}_{p}
with
p\gg\ell
. We see several sobering features, all manifestations of the same point.

Firstly, while
\pi_{*,\ell+1}(\mathbb{R})
is a free
\mathbb{Q}[r,b]
-module on one generator, the grading of this generator is
\ell+2+N
. So whatever one might mean by “stability” for a graded
\mathbb{Q}[r,b]
-module the stable range here can be made arbitrarily bad by increasing
N
.

Secondly, we see that while
\pi_{*,\ell}(\mathbb{R})
is generated in gradings
\leq\ell
and presented in gradings
\leq\ell+1
, it has
\mathrm{Tor}_{2}^{\mathbb{Q}[r,b]}(\pi_{*,\ell}(\mathbb{R}),\mathbb{Q})
non-trivial in grading
\ell+2+N
. (At one time Castelnuovo–Mumford regularity had seemed like a good measure of the “stable range” of a graded
\mathbb{Q}[r,b]
-module, but here we see that the regularity is
\ell+N
, so can be made arbitrarily bad by changing the
number
of
E_{\infty}
-cells in
\mathbb{R}
but not the
bidegrees
in which they sit. Indeed it is a well-known feature of Castelnuovo–Mumford regularity over polynomial rings in more than one variable that one cannot estimate regularity only in terms of the gradings of generators and of relations: one must use the numbers of them too.)

Thirdly, we see that in the highly cartesian square analogous to (
1.1
) the Mayer–Vietoris connecting map

\partial:\pi_{N+\ell+2,\ell+1}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\pi_{N+\ell,\ell}(\mathbb{R}),

which exists because for
N\gg 0
these groups are in the range in which the square is cartesian, is non-trivial. Indeed, one may check that

\partial(b^{N}\cdot y_{1}+b^{N-1}r\cdot y_{2}+\cdots+br^{N-1}\cdot y_{N}+r^{N}\cdot y_{N+1})=[b^{N-1}\cdot x_{1}]\neq 0.

Remark 10.2
.

This example cannot arise by
\mathbb{Q}
-linearising an
E_{2}
-algebra in
\mathsf{Top}
. If it did then as
\pi_{*,0}(\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{Q}[r,b]
it would follow that it can be given a
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
-grading in which
r
and
b
have gradings
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
respectively. But one can easily check that for
N>\ell+2
the
x_{i}
and
y_{j}
cannot be given
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
-gradings which make all the attaching maps homogeneous.

In fact, if
\mathbb{R}
is an
E_{2}
-algebra in
\mathbb{N}^{k}
-graded chain complexes having
\pi_{*,\ldots,*,0}(\mathbb{R})=\mathbbm{k}[\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{k}]
, with
\sigma_{i}
of grading
(0,\ldots,0,1,0,\ldots,0)
with 1 in the
i
-th position, then assuming (SCE) there is a stability range (of slope
\tfrac{1}{2}
) for each
\sigma_{i}\cdot-
expressed in terms of the
i
-th grading. This may be proved analogously to
[
14
, Theorem 18.1]
.

10.2.
Going beyond slope 1

The following example shows that for an
E_{2}
-algebra
\mathbb{R}
satisfying (C), (SCE) and (F) one cannot generally find a Smith–Toda complex
\mathbb{R}/(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
having a vanishing line of slope
\geq 1
.

Example 10.3
.

Let
\mathbbm{k}=\mathbb{F}_{2}
and
\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{E}_{2}(S^{1,0}\sigma)
. We will show that a finite
\mathbb{R}
-module
\mathbb{M}
with a slope 1 vanishing line must be trivial.

The finite
\mathbb{R}
-module
\mathbb{M}
may be filtered by skeleta, giving a spectral sequence

E^{1}_{*,*}=\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{R})\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}V_{*,*}\Longrightarrow\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{M})

of
\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{R})
-modules, with
V_{*,*}
a finite-dimensional bigraded
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-vector space. This spectral sequence collapses at a finite stage, and
\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{M})
is obtained from
E^{\infty}_{*,*}
by finitely-many extensions, as the skeletal filtration is finite. We have

(10.1)

\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{F}_{2}[\sigma,Q^{1}(\sigma),Q^{2}Q^{1}(\sigma),Q^{4}Q^{2}Q^{1}(\sigma),\ldots],

with the class
Q^{2^{s-1}}\cdots Q^{2}Q^{1}(\sigma)
having bidegree
(2^{s},2^{s}-1)
. We now make use of the theory of coherent modules and rings:
[
11
, Chapter I]
is a good reference for the audience of this paper. The ring (
10.1
) is coherent, for example by
[
11
, Proposition 1.5]
. Therefore a
\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{R})
-module is coherent if and only if it is finitely-presented
[
11
, Proposition 1.4]
. As
V_{*,*}
is finite-dimensional, it follows that
E^{1}_{*,*}
is a coherent
\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{R})
-module. As kernels and cokernels of maps between coherent modules are coherent
[
11
, Proposition 1.3]
, it follows that each page
E^{r}_{*,*}
is a coherent
\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{R})
-module. As the spectral sequence collapses at a finite stage with finitely-many extensions, and extensions of coherent modules are coherent
[
11
, Proposition 1.2]
, it follows that
\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{M})
is a coherent
\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{R})
-module. Therefore it is a finitely-presented
\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{R})
-module.

As
\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{M})
has a slope 1 vanishing line, each element
Q^{2^{s-1}}\cdots Q^{2}Q^{1}(\sigma)
acts nilpotently on it, because they have slope
\tfrac{2^{s}-1}{2^{s}}<1
. As it is a finitely-presented and so in particular finitely-generated
\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{R})
-module, it follows that
\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{M})
is in fact finite-dimensional over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
.

To finish the argument we will show that a non-zero finite-dimensional module over the ring (
10.1
) cannot be finitely-presented, giving a contradiction. If
N_{*,*}
is such a module then each element
Q^{2^{s-1}}\cdots Q^{2}Q^{1}(\sigma)
with
s
large enough acts trivially on
N_{*,*}
, and so the module structure is induced along the quotient map

\mathbb{F}_{2}[\sigma,Q^{1}(\sigma),Q^{2}Q^{1}(\sigma),\ldots]\longrightarrow\frac{\mathbb{F}_{2}[\sigma,Q^{1}(\sigma),Q^{2}Q^{1}(\sigma),\ldots]}{(Q^{2^{s-1}}\cdots Q^{2}Q^{1}(\sigma):s\geq K)}=:T_{*,*}

for some
K
. Thus

\mathrm{Tor}_{1}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}[\sigma,Q^{1}(\sigma),Q^{1}Q^{2}(\sigma),\ldots]}(\mathbb{F}_{2},N_{*,*})\cong\mathrm{Tor}_{1}^{T_{*,*}}(\mathbb{F}_{2},N_{*,*})\otimes\mathbb{F}_{2}\{Q^{2^{s-1}}\cdots Q^{1}(\sigma)\,:\,s\geq K\}.

By considering the right-hand side we see that if this is non-zero then it is infinite-dimensional: but it is non-zero, because the non-zero finite-dimensional
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-vector space
N_{*,*}
is certainly not a free module. As the left-hand side measures minimal relations in a resolution of
N_{*,*}
, this module is indeed not finitely-presented.

10.3.
A counterexample in characteristic zero

Theorem
A
, and its proof, requires
\mathbbm{k}
to have positive characteristic. The following example, which we learnt from Robert Burklund, shows that this assumption is essential at least if we wish to keep Theorem
A
(
iii
).

Let
\mathbbm{k}=\mathbb{Q}
and consider the
E_{\infty}
-algebra

\mathbb{R}:=\mathbb{E}_{\infty}(S^{1,0}\sigma\oplus S^{2,1}z)\cup^{E_{\infty}}_{\sigma z}D^{3,2}x\cup^{E_{\infty}}_{\sigma^{2}}D^{2,1}y.

This satisfies (C), (F), and (SCE). The homotopy groups of
\mathbb{R}
are given by the homology of the bigraded cdga

(\mathrm{Sym}^{*}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\sigma_{1,0},z_{2,1},x_{3,2},y_{2,1}],dx=\sigma z,dy=\sigma^{2}).

One calculates that its homology has basis

1,\sigma,z,x\sigma-yz,xz,x(x\sigma-yz),x^{2}z,x^{2}(x\sigma-yz),x^{3}z,,\ldots

ordered by grading. Thus
\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{R})
has a vanishing line of slope
\tfrac{2}{3}
. But also it vanishes in positive gradings divisible by 3, so every element of
\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{R})
is nilpotent, and hence
\mathbb{R}
has no non-nilpotent
\mathbb{R}
-module endomorphisms other than scalar multiples of the identity.

The formula for
\mathbb{R}
makes sense for
\mathbbm{k}=\mathbb{Z}
and so can be reduced modulo
p
. Let us suppose
p
is odd. The spectral sequence for
\mathrm{fil}_{*}^{E_{\infty}}\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
takes the form

E^{1}_{*,*,*}=\pi_{*,*,*}(\mathbb{E}_{\infty}(S^{1,0,-1}\sigma\oplus S^{2,1,-1}z\oplus S^{3,2,-1}x\oplus S^{2,1,-1}y))\Longrightarrow\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}).

There is a differential
d^{1}(x)=\sigma z
and so (by the principle explained in Remark
5.8
, cf.
[
14
, Theorem 16.8]
) the class
x^{p}
survives until
E^{p}_{3p,2p,-p}
and then

d^{p}(x^{p})=d^{p}(Q^{1}(x))=Q^{1}(\sigma z)=Q^{1}(\sigma)Q^{0}(z)+Q^{0}(\sigma)Q^{1}(z),

which vanishes because
Q^{0}(z)=0
by instability, and
Q^{0}(\sigma)=\sigma^{p}
vanishes as there is an earlier differential
d^{1}(y)=\sigma^{2}
. In fact, considering
E^{2}_{*,*,*}
we see that there are no possible targets for differentials on
x^{p}
so it is a permanent cycle: thus there is an filtered homotopy class
\xi_{p}\in\pi_{3p,2p,-p}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}})
detected by
x^{p}
. It is easy to see that
\tau^{-1}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}/\xi_{p})
has a vanishing line of slope
\geq\tfrac{3}{4}
.

Here we see another reason why an integral or rational statement is not possible: an endomorphism of
\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}}
is defined after inverting finitely-many primes, and so can be reduced modulo
p
for the infinitely-many remaining primes. But the minimal period of a non-nilpotent slope
\tfrac{2}{3}
endomorphism of
\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
depends on
p
.

10.4.
Change-of-coefficients

The ring homomorphisms
\mathbb{Q}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
induce adjunctions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{Q})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{F}_{p}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\mathbb{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}}

\dashv

\dashv

The left adjoints are strong symmetric monoidal, so the right adjoints are lax symmetric monoidal (in fact
U_{\mathbb{Q}}
is strong, as
\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}-
is a localisation).

Suppose that
\mathbb{R}\in\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{2}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{Z})^{\mathbb{Z}})
, i.e. is defined over the integers. For
\mathbbm{k}\in\{\mathbb{F}_{p},\mathbb{Q}\}
we may form
\mathbb{R}_{\mathbbm{k}}:=\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbbm{k}
, the tensor product implicitly derived. This may be considered as lying in either
\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{2}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbbm{k})^{\mathbb{Z}})
or via the lax symmetric monoidal functor
U_{\mathbbm{k}}
as lying in
\mathsf{Alg}_{E_{2}}(\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{Z})^{\mathbb{Z}})
: in the latter case, there is an
E_{2}
-algebra map
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{\mathbbm{k}}
.

Let
L^{f}_{\lambda}(-)
denote the finite localisation as previously discussed applied in the category of
\mathbb{R}
-modules, and
L^{f,\mathbbm{k}}_{\lambda}(-)
denote the analogue in
\mathbb{R}_{\mathbbm{k}}
-modules, using the class
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}^{f,\mathbbm{k}}
of finite
\mathbb{R}_{\mathbbm{k}}
-modules with a vanishing line of slope
\lambda
. Similarly, let
L_{\lambda}(-)
denote the non-finite localisation discussed in Section
4.5
, using the class
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
of all
\mathbb{R}
-modules with a slope
\lambda
vanishing line, and
L^{\mathbbm{k}}_{\lambda}(-)
the same in
\mathbb{R}_{\mathbbm{k}}
-modules.

Proposition 10.4
.

In the following we implicitly forget down to
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
.

(i)

There are factorisations
\mathbb{R}_{\mathbbm{k}}\to L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbbm{k}})\to L_{\lambda}^{f,\mathbbm{k}}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbbm{k}})
and
\mathbb{R}_{\mathbbm{k}}\to L_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbbm{k}})\to L_{\lambda}^{\mathbbm{k}}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbbm{k}})
.

(ii)

L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbbm{k}})\simeq L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbbm{k}
.

(iii)

L_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}})\simeq L_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{F}_{p}
.

(iv)

When
\mathbbm{k}=\mathbb{F}_{p}
, the map
L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}})\to L^{f,\mathbb{F}_{p}}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}})
is an equivalence.

(v)

When
\mathbbm{k}=\mathbb{Q}
, the map
L_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}})\to L^{\mathbb{Q}}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}})
is an equivalence.

Proof.

For (
i
), if
\mathbb{T}\in\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
then
\mathbb{T}_{\mathbbm{k}}\in\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}^{f,\mathbbm{k}}
by the Universal Coefficient Theorem, so
\mathrm{map}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T},L_{\lambda}^{f,\mathbbm{k}}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbbm{k}}))\simeq\mathrm{map}_{\mathbb{R}_{\mathbbm{k}}}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathbbm{k}},L_{\lambda}^{f,\mathbbm{k}}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbbm{k}}))\simeq 0
, and therefore
L_{\lambda}^{f,\mathbbm{k}}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbbm{k}})
is
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
-local: this gives a factorisation

\mathbb{R}_{\mathbbm{k}}\longrightarrow L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbbm{k}})\longrightarrow L_{\lambda}^{f,\mathbbm{k}}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbbm{k}}).

The argument for the non-finite localisation is the same.

For (
ii
) we use that
L_{\lambda}^{f}
is smashing to write
L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbbm{k}})=L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{R}_{\mathbbm{k}}=L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbbm{k}=L^{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbbm{k}
. For (
iii
) we apply the left adjoint
L_{\lambda}
to the cofibre sequence
\mathbb{R}\overset{p\cdot-}{\to}\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
, to get a cofibre sequence
L_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R})\overset{p\cdot-}{\to}L_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R})\to L_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}})
in the
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
-local category. But cofibres in the
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
-local category may be computed in the ambient category (as they are suspended fibres), so
L_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}})
is the cofibre of multiplication by
p
on
L_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R})
.

For (
iv
), if
\mathbb{T}^{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
is a finite
\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
-module with a slope
\lambda
vanishing line, then it is also finite as an
\mathbb{R}
-module, because
\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
is a finite
\mathbb{R}
-module (namely the cofibre of
p\cdot-:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
). Thus it lies in
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}^{f}
, so
\mathcal{A}^{f}_{\lambda}
-local objects are in particular
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}^{f,\mathbb{F}_{p}}
-local, giving a map
L_{\lambda}^{f,\mathbb{F}_{p}}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}})\to L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}})
. The universal properties show that it is a homotopy inverse to the natural map.

For (
v
), first note that
L_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}})
is a
\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}}
-algebra, and so is rational. If
\mathbb{T}^{\mathbb{Q}}
is a
\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}}
-module with a slope
\lambda
vanishing line, then it is also a
\mathbb{R}
-module with a slope
\lambda
vanishing line. As rationalising is a localisation

0\simeq\mathrm{map}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathbb{Q}},L_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}}))\simeq\mathrm{map}_{\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}}}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathbb{Q}},L_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}}))

so
L_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}})
is in particular
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Q}}_{\lambda}
-local, giving a map
L^{\mathbb{Q}}_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}})\to L_{\lambda}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}})
. The universal properties show that it is a homotopy inverse to the natural map.
∎

The following example shows that the conclusion of Proposition
10.4
(
iv
) cannot hold for
\mathbbm{k}=\mathbb{Q}
.

Example 10.5
.

Let
\mathbbm{k}=\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathbb{R}:=\mathbb{E}_{2}(S^{1,0}\sigma)
. We first claim that any finite
\mathbb{R}
-module
\mathbb{M}
with a slope 1 vanishing line is rationally trivial. This is because the finite
\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}
-module
\mathbb{M}_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}
again has a slope 1 vanishing line, so by Example
10.3
is trivial. Thus 2 is invertible on
\mathbb{M}
, so its homotopy groups are
\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{2}]
-modules, but they are also finitely-generated abelian groups so must be finite, and therefore
\mathbb{M}
is rationally trivial.

If
\mathbb{T}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}^{f}
it follows from the claim that
\mathbb{T}_{\mathbb{Q}}=0
, which means that
\mathrm{map}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}})\simeq\mathrm{map}_{\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}}}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}})=0
. Thus
\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}}
is
\mathcal{A}_{1}^{f}
-local, so
L^{f}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}})=\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}}
and therefore
L^{f}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}}/\sigma)=\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}}/\sigma
. On the other hand we have

\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}})=\mathbb{Q}[\sigma]\otimes\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}[\,[\sigma,\sigma]\,],

so
\pi_{*,*}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}}/\sigma)\cong\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}[\,[\sigma,\sigma]\,]
. This is finite-dimensional over
\mathbb{Q}
so has a vanishing line of slope 1 (or in fact of any slope), so
\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}}/\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{1}^{f,\mathbb{Q}}
and hence
L_{1}^{f,\mathbb{Q}}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}}/\sigma)=0\neq L^{f}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}}/\sigma)
. It follows that

\text{the map }L_{1}^{f}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}})\longrightarrow L_{1}^{f,\mathbb{Q}}(\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}})\text{ is not an equivalence}.

(In fact one may show that this is the map
\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\sigma^{-1}\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{Q}}
.)

10.5.
Further structure on Smith–Toda complexes

Let us briefly indicate some further things which can most likely be done with our constructions of Smith–Toda complexes, though we have not checked in detail. Let us write
x_{1},\ldots,x_{i}
for the
x
’s of
\tfrac{d}{n}
-slope
<\lambda
.

Firstly, following
[
28
, Proposition 4.11]
, it seems that one may produce filtered Smith–Toda complexes
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{i})
equipped with a (non-associative!) left-unital multiplication
\mu
in
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
. Such filtered Smith–Toda complexes are “atomic” in the sense used there, because
C\tau\otimes(\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{i}))=(C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R})/(x_{1}^{p^{M_{1}}},\ldots,x_{i}^{p^{M_{i}}})
and
x_{1}^{p^{M_{1}}},\ldots,x_{i}^{p^{M_{i}}}
is a regular sequence in
\pi_{*,*,*}(C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R})
, so any endomorphism of
C\tau\otimes(\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{i}))
under
C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}
is an equivalence, and hence the same is true before applying
C\tau\otimes-
. The (modest) advantage of having such a multiplication is that to produce a self-map of
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{i})
it now suffices to give it on the bottom cell, i.e. to give a filtered homotopy class. Using the methods of
[
8
]
one may probably even construct
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{i})
as an
E_{2}
-algebra.

Secondly, following
[
28
, Section 4, Proposition 7.10 (a)]

[
39
, Section 3]
, if
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{i})
is a filtered Smith–Toda complex obtained by the proof of Theorem
5.6
, where
C\tau\otimes\phi_{j}=(C\tau\otimes u)_{*}(x_{j}^{p^{M_{j}}})
, then it is not hard to show using Theorem
6.7
that there are divergent sequences
M_{1}(n),\ldots,M_{i}(n)
and a sequence of filtered Smith–Toda complexes
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1}(n),\ldots,\phi_{i}(n))
with
C\tau\otimes\phi_{j}(n)=(C\tau\otimes u)_{*}(x_{j}^{p^{M_{j}(n)}})
and starting with
\phi_{j}(0)=\phi_{j}
, assembling into a tower

\cdots\longrightarrow\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1}(2),\ldots,\phi_{i}(2))\longrightarrow\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1}(1),\ldots,\phi_{i}(1))\longrightarrow\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{i})

under
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}
. Inverting
\tau
and then dualising with
D^{r}(-)=\underline{\mathrm{map}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{r}(-,\mathbb{R})
gives a direct system of
\mathbb{R}
-modules over
\mathbb{R}
, whose colimit can be shown to be
C_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
. As in
[
28
, Proposition 4.18]
these filtered Smith–Toda complexes can be constructed to be self-dual up to a shift, so this colimit describing
C_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})
is quite explicit. Its cofibre therefore gives another explicit model for
L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})
different from that of Section
4.4
.

Thirdly, when a filtered Smith–Toda complex
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}/(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{i})
exists, we may Bousfield localise away from it in
\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{mod}
: provisionally call this
L_{\lambda}^{f,\mathrm{fil}}(-)
. Constructing this localisation by the analogue of Section
4.4
, or perhaps as in the previous paragraph, one finds that
\tau^{-1}L_{\lambda}^{f,\mathrm{fil}}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R})=L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})
and that
C\tau\otimes L_{\lambda}^{f,\mathrm{fil}}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R})
is Bousfield localisation of
C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}
away from
(C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R})/(x_{1}^{p^{M_{1}}},\ldots,x_{i}^{p^{M_{i}}})
. The latter may be described as the homotopy pull back in
C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}
-modules of the punctured
i
-cube obtained by tensoring together the maps
C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R}\to x_{j}^{-1}(C\tau\otimes\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R})
for
j=1,\ldots,i
, as in Example
3.9
. The completeness of the filtered object
L_{\lambda}^{f,\mathrm{fil}}(\mathrm{fil}_{*}\mathbb{R})
does not seem obvious, so convergence of the associated spectral sequence in unclear: but in principle this gives some access to
L_{\lambda}^{f}(\mathbb{R})
. See
[
39
, Section 7]
for the analogue in stable homotopy theory.
